7. **Interest and relevance**: link content to learner's problems and life.
8. **Comprehension**: use the learner's language and level.
9. **Feedback**: tells the learner what is right and what to correct.
10. **Primacy and recency**: first and last items are remembered best.
11. **Intensity**: vivid, dramatic experiences (demonstration, real cases) are remembered longer.
12. **Spaced learning** is better than massed (cramming).
13. **Multisensory**: using several senses (audio-visual aids) improves retention.
14. **Individual differences**: pace and method suited to the learner.
15. **Good human relations** between teacher and learner.

**Application**

Health education, patient counselling, training of CHWs and students.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.99
**Definition**

**Personality**: the dynamic organisation within the individual of those psychophysical systems that determine his unique adjustment to his environment (after Allport); the sum of a person's characteristic patterns of thinking, feeling and behaving.

**Classification**

**A. Type theories**

| Theory | Types | Example features |
|---|---|---|
| Hippocrates (humours) | Sanguine, choleric, melancholic, phlegmatic | Cheerful; irritable; sad; calm |
| Kretschmer (body build) | Pyknic, asthenic, athletic | Stout, sociable; thin, withdrawn; muscular, energetic |
| Sheldon (somatotype) | Endomorph, mesomorph, ectomorph | Relaxed, comfort-loving; assertive; shy, restrained |
| Jung | Introvert, extrovert, ambivert | Reserved; outgoing; mixed |
| Friedman and Rosenman | Type A, Type B | Hurried, competitive (CHD risk); relaxed |

**B. Trait theories**

- **Allport**: cardinal, central and secondary traits.
- **Cattell**: 16 personality factors.
- **Eysenck**: extraversion, neuroticism, psychoticism.
- **Big Five (OCEAN)**: openness, conscientiousness, extraversion, agreeableness, neuroticism.

**C. Psychoanalytic (Freud)**

Id, ego, superego.

**Determinants**

Heredity, physique, endocrine glands, family, culture, school, peers, life experiences.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.100
**Type A vs Type B personality (Friedman and Rosenman)**

| Feature | Type A | Type B |
|---|---|---|
| Time | Sense of **time urgency**; always hurried | Relaxed; no hurry |
| Drive | Highly **competitive**, ambitious, achievement-oriented | Less competitive; easy-going |
| Emotions | Impatient, **hostile**, easily angered | Patient, calm, tolerant |
| Work | Workaholic; many tasks at once | Balanced work and leisure |
| Speech and manner | Fast talking, interrupts, restless | Slow, unhurried speech |
| Leisure | Feels guilty when relaxing | Enjoys leisure without guilt |
| Stress response | High stress, high sympathetic arousal | Low stress |
| Health risk | Higher risk of **coronary heart disease**, hypertension, peptic ulcer | Lower risk |

**Notes**

- The **hostility / anger** component of Type A is most strongly linked to CHD.
- Later types: Type C (suppresses emotions; linked to cancer coping), Type D ("distressed": negative affect + social inhibition; poorer cardiac outcome).

**Relevance**

Stress management, relaxation, lifestyle counselling for Type A persons as part of CHD prevention.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences; Epidemiology of Chronic Non-communicable Diseases).

## DU.101
**Development of personality**

Personality develops through the interaction of **heredity** (genes, physique, temperament, endocrine) and **environment** (family, school, peers, culture, religion, life events), across stages of life.

**A. Freud's psychosexual stages**

| Stage | Age | Focus |
|---|---|---|
| Oral | 0–1 year | Mouth (sucking, feeding) |
| Anal | 1–3 years | Toilet training, control |
| Phallic | 3–6 years | Genitals; Oedipus/Electra complex |
| Latency | 6 years–puberty | Sexual drive dormant; school, skills |
| Genital | Puberty onward | Mature sexual relationships |

**B. Erikson's psychosocial stages**

| Stage | Age | Conflict | Virtue |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Infancy (0–1) | Trust vs mistrust | Hope |
| 2 | Early childhood (1–3) | Autonomy vs shame and doubt | Will |
| 3 | Preschool (3–6) | Initiative vs guilt | Purpose |
| 4 | School age (6–12) | Industry vs inferiority | Competence |
| 5 | Adolescence (12–18) | Identity vs role confusion | Fidelity |
| 6 | Young adult | Intimacy vs isolation | Love |
| 7 | Middle adult | Generativity vs stagnation | Care |
| 8 | Old age | Integrity vs despair | Wisdom |

**Agents of development**

Family (first and most important), school, peer group, religion, mass media, occupation, culture.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.102
**Definition**

**Emotion**: a complex state of feeling, arising in response to a stimulus, with three components:
1. **Subjective** experience (feeling)
2. **Physiological** arousal (autonomic: heart rate, BP, sweating; hormones)
3. **Behavioural / expressive** response (facial expression, voice, action)

**Primary (basic) emotions**

| Model | Emotions |
|---|---|
| Ekman (6, universal facial expressions) | Happiness, sadness, fear, anger, surprise, disgust |
| Plutchik (8) | Joy, trust, fear, surprise, sadness, disgust, anger, anticipation |

Secondary (complex) emotions arise from mixtures: love, guilt, shame, jealousy, pride, anxiety.

**Features**

1. Aroused by internal or external stimuli.
2. Accompanied by bodily changes.
3. Influence thinking and behaviour.
4. Can be pleasant or unpleasant.

**Relevance to health**

- Chronic negative emotions (anxiety, anger) → stress → hypertension, peptic ulcer, CHD (psychosomatic illness).
- Fear influences care-seeking and compliance.
- Emotional dimension of health; emotional intelligence of doctors improves care.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences; Mental Health).

## DU.103
**Features of a mentally healthy person (Park)**

1. Free from internal **conflicts**; not at war with himself.
2. **Well adjusted**: gets along with others; accepts praise and criticism.
3. Searches for **identity**.
4. Strong sense of **self-esteem**.
5. Knows himself, his needs, problems and goals (**self-actualisation**).
6. Good **self-control**: balances rationality and emotionality.
7. Faces problems and tries to solve them **intelligently**; copes with stress and anxiety.

**Other features**

- Realistic perception of reality.
- Ability to form satisfying relationships (love and friendship).
- Works productively; contributes to the community.
- Takes responsibility for own actions.
- Adapts to change; flexible.

**Five criteria (Jahoda)**

1. Positive attitude towards self
2. Growth and self-actualisation
3. Integration of personality
4. Autonomy
5. Accurate perception of reality and environmental mastery

**WHO (2022)**: mental health is a state of mental well-being that enables people to cope with stresses, realise abilities, learn and work well, and contribute to their community.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Mental Health); WHO, 2022.

## DU.104
**Intelligence**

The global capacity to act purposefully, think rationally and deal effectively with the environment (Wechsler); ability to learn, reason, solve problems and adapt.

**IQ (Intelligence Quotient)**

A numerical index of intelligence relative to age.

**Formula (ratio IQ; Stern, Terman)**

IQ = (Mental age ÷ Chronological age) × 100

- **Mental age (MA)**: the age level at which a person performs on an intelligence test.
- **Chronological age (CA)**: actual age.
- Average IQ = 100 (MA = CA).

**Deviation IQ**

Modern tests (Wechsler) compare a person with peers of the same age; mean 100, standard deviation 15.

**Tests**

Stanford–Binet; Wechsler Adult Intelligence Scale (WAIS); Wechsler Intelligence Scale for Children (WISC); Raven's Progressive Matrices (non-verbal).

**Uses**

1. Diagnosis and grading of **intellectual disability**.
2. Educational placement; special education.
3. Vocational guidance and selection.
4. Research (effects of iodine deficiency, malnutrition, lead).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Mental Health).

## DU.105
**Classification of people by IQ**

| IQ range | Category |
|---|---|
| ≥140 | Genius / near genius |
| 120–139 | Very superior |
| 110–119 | Superior |
| 90–109 | **Average (normal)** |
| 80–89 | Dull normal (low average) |
| 70–79 | Borderline |
| <70 | **Intellectual disability (mental retardation)** |

**Grades of intellectual disability**

| Grade | IQ | Mental age (adult) | Features |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mild | 50–69 | 9–12 years | Educable; can live independently with some support |
| Moderate | 35–49 | 6–9 years | Trainable; self-care and simple work under supervision |
| Severe | 20–34 | 3–6 years | Needs constant supervision; limited speech |
| Profound | <20 | <3 years | Total care needed |

**Interpretation**

- About two-thirds of people have IQ 85–115 (mean ± 1 SD).
- IQ must be interpreted with adaptive behaviour (social and daily-living skills) before diagnosing intellectual disability.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Mental Health).

## DU.106
**Calculation**

IQ = (Mental age ÷ Chronological age) × 100

**Given**

Chronological age = 10 years; mental age = 2 years.

IQ = (2 ÷ 10) × 100 = **20**

**Interpretation**

| IQ range | Grade |
|---|---|
| 50–69 | Mild intellectual disability |
| 35–49 | Moderate |
| **20–34** | **Severe** |
| <20 | Profound |

IQ 20 = **severe intellectual disability** (at the borderline with profound).

**Implications**

1. Functions like a 2-year-old: limited speech, needs help with self-care.
2. Needs **constant supervision** and family support.
3. Requires **special education**, training in basic self-care, speech and physiotherapy.
4. Look for causes: birth asphyxia, congenital hypothyroidism, iodine deficiency, genetic syndromes (Down syndrome), infections, malnutrition.
5. Assess adaptive behaviour; counsel the family; link to disability services.

**Note**

Ratio IQ is used for children; for adults, deviation IQ is used (chronological age above ~15–16 years is not used in the formula).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Mental Health).

## DU.107
**Definition**

- **Park**: a group of biologically related individuals living together and eating from a common kitchen.
- **Sociological (Burgess and Locke)**: a group of persons united by marriage, blood or adoption, forming a single household, interacting in their social roles (husband, wife, parents, children, siblings) and sharing a common culture.

**Characteristics**

1. Universal social unit; the **primary group**.
2. Based on **marriage** and blood ties (or adoption).
3. Common **residence** and kitchen.
4. Economic provision and shared resources.
5. Emotional bonds; nurtures and socialises children.
6. Limited size; permanent and temporary aspects.
7. Has a system of names and descent.

**Family as a unit of health care**

- First line of care for the sick.
- Shapes health habits, diet and hygiene.
- Decides use of health services.
- Family health is the basis of community health.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.108
**Functions of the family**

| Function | Meaning |
|---|---|
| 1. **Biological / reproductive** | Regulates sexual relations; produces and continues generations |
| 2. **Socialisation and education** | Teaches language, values, norms, habits, culture |
| 3. **Economic** | Earns, shares income and property; provides food, clothing, shelter |
| 4. **Emotional (affectional)** | Love, security, companionship |
| 5. **Protective and caring** | Care of children, sick, disabled and elderly |
| 6. **Social status (placement)** | Gives name, identity, religion, social position |
| 7. **Recreational** | Leisure and relaxation |
| 8. **Religious / moral** | Moral and religious training |

**Functions relevant to health and health behaviour**

1. **Nutrition** and child feeding: breastfeeding, complementary feeding.
2. **Hygiene and sanitation** at home: hand-washing, safe water, latrine use.
3. **Health habits** learned from parents: diet, exercise, tobacco avoidance.
4. **Care of the sick**: home nursing, ORS, compliance with treatment.
5. **Decision making** on care-seeking, ANC, facility delivery, immunisation.
6. **Family planning** and birth spacing.
7. **Financing** of health care.
8. **Emotional support** in chronic illness, mental illness and disability.
9. **Care of the elderly** and rehabilitation.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.109
**Role of family in health and disease**

**A. In causation of disease**

1. **Heredity**: genetic diseases (thalassaemia, haemophilia), consanguineous marriage.
2. **Family size and spacing**: malnutrition, infections, maternal depletion.
3. **Socio-economic status**: poverty, overcrowding, poor housing → TB, diarrhoea.
4. **Habits and lifestyle** passed on: diet, smoking, betel quid, inactivity.
5. **Spread of infection** within household: TB, scabies, hepatitis, COVID-19.
6. **Family disharmony**: broken or problem families → delinquency, depression, substance abuse, child abuse.

**B. In recognition of disease**

Family members notice symptoms and danger signs first; they decide when and where to seek care.

**C. In care and treatment**

1. Home care and nursing; ORS for diarrhoea.
2. Ensures **compliance** (DOTS, insulin, antihypertensives).
3. Pays for treatment.
4. Emotional support reduces stress and aids recovery.

**D. In rehabilitation**

Care of disabled, elderly, mentally ill; reintegration into society.

**E. In prevention and promotion**

Immunisation, breastfeeding, family planning, hygiene, healthy diet, ANC, safe delivery.

**Concept**

The family is the **basic unit of health care**; family-centred care improves outcomes.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.110
**Classification of family**

| Basis | Types | Meaning / example |
|---|---|---|
| **Structure (Park)** | **Nuclear** (elementary) | Husband, wife and unmarried children |
| | **Joint** (extended) | Several nuclear families sharing residence, kitchen and property (brothers with wives and children, parents) |
| | **Three-generation** | Grandparents, parents and children together |
| **Marriage** | Monogamous | One husband, one wife |
| | Polygamous: polygyny / polyandry | One husband, several wives / one wife, several husbands |
| **Authority** | Patriarchal / matriarchal / egalitarian | Father / mother / both hold authority |
| **Residence** | Patrilocal / matrilocal / neolocal | Live with husband's / wife's family / new home |
| **Descent** | Patrilineal / matrilineal | Descent through father (Bangladesh) / mother (Garo community) |
| **Membership** | Family of orientation / procreation | Family one is born into / family one creates by marriage |
| **Other** | Single-parent, broken, blended, problem family | One parent; separated; remarriage; family with persistent social and health problems |

**Trend in Bangladesh**

Shift from joint to **nuclear** families with urbanisation and migration; implications: less support for elderly and childcare.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.111
**Types of leadership**

**A. By style (Lewin)**

| Type | Features | Health example |
|---|---|---|
| **Autocratic (authoritarian)** | Leader decides alone; strict control; one-way communication | Emergency resuscitation, disaster response |
| **Democratic (participative)** | Decisions shared with group; two-way communication | Upazila health team planning EPI micro-plans |
| **Laissez-faire (free-rein)** | Leader gives freedom; minimal direction | Experienced research team |

**B. Other types**

| Type | Features |
|---|---|
| Bureaucratic | Works strictly by rules and procedures |
| Paternalistic | Leader acts like a parent; caring but controlling |
| Charismatic | Influence through personal charm and vision |
| Transactional | Reward and punishment for performance |
| Transformational | Inspires, motivates change, develops followers |
| Situational (Hersey–Blanchard) | Style varies with followers' readiness: telling, selling, participating, delegating |
| Servant | Leader serves the needs of the team |

**C. By position**

Formal (appointed: UH&FPO, civil surgeon) vs informal (emerges from the group: respected village elder, imam, teacher).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Planning and Management; Communication for Health Education).

## DU.112
**Criteria / qualities of a good leader**

1. **Vision**: clear goals and direction.
2. **Integrity and honesty**: trusted by the team.
3. **Competence and knowledge** of the field.
4. **Communication**: listens, explains, gives feedback.
5. **Decision-making** and problem-solving ability.
6. **Motivates and inspires** others; recognises good work.
7. **Delegation**: assigns tasks according to ability.
8. **Fairness**: impartial; no favouritism.
9. **Empathy and emotional intelligence**.
10. **Accountability**: accepts responsibility for outcomes.
11. **Confidence and courage** in difficult situations.
12. **Flexibility**: adapts style to the situation.
13. **Team building** and conflict resolution.
14. **Commitment and hard work**: leads by example.
15. **Humility**: open to advice and criticism.
16. **Creativity and innovation**.

**Three characteristics (short answer)**

Vision, integrity, good communication.

**In health care**

A good leader (e.g., UH&FPO) coordinates the team, community and other sectors, uses data for decisions and maintains quality of care.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Planning and Management).

## DU.113
**Ideal leadership: democratic (participative), adapted to the situation**

**Why democratic is generally ideal in health care**

1. Health care is **teamwork**; members have different skills.
2. **Shared decisions** use the knowledge of all members → better decisions.
3. **Motivation and job satisfaction** rise; staff feel valued (esteem needs).
4. **Commitment and acceptance** of decisions; easier implementation.
5. **Two-way communication**; problems surface early.
6. Develops **future leaders**; builds trust.
7. Fits **community participation** principle of PHC.

**Limitations**

Slow in emergencies; needs skilled, willing members.

**Comparison**

| Style | Best used when | Drawback |
|---|---|---|
| Autocratic | Emergencies, disasters, inexperienced staff | Low morale, no creativity |
| **Democratic** | Routine planning, programme management, team care | Slow decisions |
| Laissez-faire | Highly skilled, self-motivated experts | Lack of direction, chaos |

**Conclusion**

No single style suits every situation (**situational leadership**); democratic is ideal for most health settings, with an autocratic shift in emergencies; transformational qualities make it more effective.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Planning and Management).

## DU.114
**Answer**

**Democratic (participative) leadership** encourages participation of staff in decision-making.

**Features**

1. Leader consults staff and shares decision-making.
2. Two-way communication; ideas welcomed.
3. Responsibility delegated; teamwork encouraged.
4. Decisions often by consensus or majority.
5. Leader guides, coordinates and keeps final accountability.

**Advantages**

Higher motivation and job satisfaction; better-quality decisions; commitment to implementation; creativity; staff development.

**Disadvantages**

Time-consuming; unsuitable in emergencies; needs competent staff.

**Example**

UH&FPO holding a monthly meeting with medical officers, nurses, HIs and CHCPs to plan dengue control and EPI micro-plans.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Planning and Management).

## DU.115
**Definition**

**Leader**: a person who influences, guides and directs the behaviour of a group towards achieving common goals, and whom the group accepts.

**Leadership**: the process (art) of influencing people so that they work willingly and enthusiastically towards group goals.

**Key elements**

1. **Influence** over others.
2. **Followers** who accept the influence.
3. **Common goal**.
4. **Situation** in which leadership occurs.

**Types of leaders**

- **Formal**: appointed to a position (UH&FPO, hospital director).
- **Informal**: emerges from the group by respect (village elder, teacher, imam).
- **Opinion leaders** in the community: key to health education and community participation.

**Leader vs manager (brief)**

Leader: vision, inspiration, change | Manager: planning, organising, control.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Planning and Management; Communication for Health Education).

## DU.116
**Vital statistics**

Numerical records of vital events occurring in a population (births, deaths, marriages and related events); collected by registration and sample registration.

**Major vital events (UN list)**

1. **Live birth**
2. **Death** (including infant, neonatal and maternal deaths)
3. **Fetal death** (stillbirth)
4. **Marriage**
5. **Divorce**
6. Annulment of marriage
7. Judicial separation
8. Adoption
9. Legitimation
10. Recognition (of paternity)

Births and deaths are the most important for health; marriages and divorces matter for fertility.

**Rates derived**

Crude birth rate, crude death rate, IMR, NMR, MMR, life expectancy, fertility rates, marriage rate.

**Bangladesh**

- Births and Deaths Registration Act 2004 (civil registration).
- Sample Vital Registration System (SVRS) by BBS gives annual national estimates.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics; Demography and Family Planning).

## DU.117
**Sources of demographic statistics**

| Source | Description | Bangladesh example |
|---|---|---|
| 1. **Census** | Complete count of population at one time; age, sex, education, occupation, housing | Population and Housing Census (every 10 years; latest 2022) |
| 2. **Registration of vital events** (civil registration) | Continuous, compulsory recording of births, deaths, marriages | Births and Deaths Registration Act 2004 |
| 3. **Sample registration system** | Continuous registration in a representative sample with independent survey (dual record) | SVRS (BBS) |
| 4. **Sample surveys** | Periodic national surveys | BDHS, MICS, HIES, Labour Force Survey |
| 5. **Population registers** | Continuous record of each resident | National ID database |
| 6. **Health service records** | Hospital records, EPI and FP registers, HMIS/DHIS2 | DGHS, DGFP records |
| 7. **Notification and special registers** | Notifiable diseases, cancer registry | IEDCR |
| 8. **Other records** | School enrolment, migration, NGO records | Ministry of Education data |
| 9. **International estimates** | UN World Population Prospects, World Bank | UN WPP |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Demography and Family Planning; Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.118
**Biostatistics**

Application of statistical methods to biological, medical and health data.

**Uses**

**A. Medicine and research**
1. Define **normal limits** (Hb, BP, height, weight).
2. Compare efficacy of **drugs, vaccines and procedures** (clinical trials).
3. Find **associations** between risk factors and disease (smoking and lung cancer).
4. Design studies: sample size, sampling, randomisation.
5. Test **hypotheses**; judge significance (p-value, confidence interval).
6. Evaluate **diagnostic and screening tests** (sensitivity, specificity).

**B. Community medicine / public health**
1. Measure **health status** of a community (IMR, MMR, life expectancy).
2. **Compare** health status between areas, groups and times.
3. Identify **priority problems** and high-risk groups (community diagnosis).
4. **Plan, monitor and evaluate** health programmes (EPI coverage, CPR).
5. **Surveillance** and outbreak detection (trends, epidemic thresholds).
6. Demography: population growth, projections.

**C. Health administration**
1. Planning services, manpower, beds, budget.
2. Hospital statistics: bed occupancy, average length of stay.

**D. Basic sciences**
Physiology, anatomy, pharmacology (dose–response, bioassay), genetics.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.119
**Definition**

**Variable**: a characteristic that varies (takes different values) from person to person, place to place or time to time (e.g., age, sex, Hb, BP).

**Types**

| Type | Sub-type | Meaning | Examples |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Qualitative (categorical)** | **Nominal** | Categories without order | Sex, blood group, religion |
| | **Ordinal** | Categories with natural order | Stage of cancer, socio-economic class, grade of anaemia |
| **Quantitative (numerical)** | **Discrete** | Whole numbers; counted | Number of children, pulse rate, number of decayed teeth |
| | **Continuous** | Any value in a range; measured | Height, weight, Hb, BP, temperature |

**By role in research**

| Type | Meaning | Example (smoking and lung cancer) |
|---|---|---|
| Independent (exposure) | Presumed cause | Smoking |
| Dependent (outcome) | Presumed effect | Lung cancer |
| Confounding | Related to both, distorts association | Age, air pollution |

**Discrete vs continuous**

| Discrete | Continuous |
|---|---|
| Counted | Measured |
| Whole numbers only | Fractions possible |
| Gaps between values | Unbroken scale |
| Number of ANC visits | Birth weight |
| Shown by bar diagram | Shown by histogram, frequency polygon |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.120
**Definition**

**Data**: a collection of facts, figures or observations (numbers or categories) recorded on variables, from which information is derived.

**Classification**

| Basis | Types | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Nature | **Qualitative** / **Quantitative** | Sex, blood group / Hb, age |
| Quantitative | **Discrete** / **Continuous** | Number of children / height |
| Scale of measurement | Nominal, ordinal, interval, ratio | Religion; pain grade; temperature (°C); weight |
| Source | **Primary** / **Secondary** | Own survey / census, hospital records |
| Arrangement | Ungrouped (raw) / Grouped | List of ages / frequency table by age group |
| Time | Cross-sectional / Time-series | One survey / yearly IMR trend |

**Qualitative vs quantitative data**

| Point | Qualitative | Quantitative |
|---|---|---|
| Nature | Attributes, categories | Numbers, measurements |
| Obtained by | Classification, counting frequency | Measurement or counting |
| Sub-types | Nominal, ordinal | Discrete, continuous |
| Summary | Proportion, percentage, ratio | Mean, median, SD |
| Presentation | Bar diagram, pie chart | Histogram, frequency polygon, line graph |
| Tests | Chi-square | t-test, ANOVA, correlation |
| Example | Immunised / not immunised | Birth weight 2.8 kg |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.121
**Sources of data**

**A. Primary data**: collected first-hand by the investigator for the study.

| Method | Example |
|---|---|
| Surveys (interview, questionnaire) | Household survey on hand-washing |
| Observation | Observing hygiene practices in a school |
| Measurement / examination | BP, Hb, anthropometry |
| Experiments and trials | Vaccine trial |
| Focus group discussions, in-depth interviews | Barriers to ANC |

**B. Secondary data**: already collected by others for other purposes.

| Source | Example |
|---|---|
| Census | Population and Housing Census 2022 |
| Vital registration, SVRS | Birth and death rates |
| National surveys | BDHS, MICS, HIES |
| Hospital and health service records | OPD registers, HMIS/DHIS2 |
| Disease notification, registers | IEDCR surveillance, cancer registry |
| Published literature and reports | Journals, WHO and UNICEF reports |

**Primary vs secondary**

| Primary | Secondary |
|---|---|
| Original, specific to purpose | Already available |
| More accurate and relevant | May not fit the purpose; quality unknown |
| Costly, time-consuming | Cheap, quick |

**Other classifications**

Internal (within the institution) vs external; routine (continuous) vs ad hoc (special surveys).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.122
**Methods of data collection**

1. **Observation**: participant / non-participant; structured (checklist) / unstructured.
2. **Interview**: face-to-face or telephone; structured, semi-structured, unstructured; interview schedule.
3. **Questionnaire**: self-administered, mailed, online.
4. **Physical examination and measurement**: clinical examination, anthropometry (weight, height, MUAC), BP.
5. **Laboratory investigations**: Hb, blood glucose, stool, sputum.
6. **Record review (documentary)**: hospital records, registers, case notes.
7. **Qualitative methods**: focus group discussion (FGD), in-depth interview (IDI), key informant interview (KII).
8. **Experiments / trials**.
9. **Mechanical / electronic**: data loggers, mobile data collection (ODK, KoBo).

**Parts of a table**

| Part | Content |
|---|---|
| 1. Table number | Serial number (Table 1) |
| 2. Title | Brief, clear: what, where, when |
| 3. Column headings (captions) | Labels of columns, with units |
| 4. Row headings (stubs) | Labels of rows (categories) |
| 5. Body (cells) | The data (frequencies, %) |
| 6. Totals | Row and column totals |
| 7. Footnote | Explanations, abbreviations |
| 8. Source | Origin of data |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.123
**Methods of data presentation**

**A. Textual**: short description in sentences.

**B. Tabulation**

1. Simple table (one variable).
2. Frequency distribution table (grouped).
3. Complex / cross-table (two or more variables).

**C. Graphical (charts and diagrams)**

| For quantitative (continuous) data | For qualitative / discrete data |
|---|---|
| **Histogram** | **Bar diagram**: simple, multiple, component, proportional |
| **Frequency polygon** | **Pie (sector) chart** |
| Frequency curve | **Pictogram** |
| **Line graph** (trend over time) | **Map diagram**: spot map, shaded map |
| Cumulative frequency curve (ogive) | |
| **Scatter diagram** (correlation) | |
| Box-and-whisker plot; stem-and-leaf | |

**Principles of good presentation**

1. Self-explanatory title; numbered.
2. Clear labels and units; scales start at zero.
3. Simple; not overcrowded.
4. Source and footnotes.
5. Right diagram for the type of data.

**Uses**

Makes data easy to understand, compare and remember; shows trends and relationships.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.124
**Criteria (characteristics) of the normal distribution curve**

1. **Bell-shaped** and **symmetrical** about the mean.
2. **Mean = median = mode** (at the centre).
3. **Unimodal**: one peak.
4. **Asymptotic**: tails approach but never touch the x-axis.
5. Total **area under the curve = 1 (100%)**.
6. Defined fully by two parameters: **mean (μ)** and **standard deviation (σ)**.
7. Fixed area relationships:

| Range | Area (observations) |
|---|---|
| Mean ± 1 SD | 68.27% |
| Mean ± 1.96 SD | 95% |
| Mean ± 2 SD | 95.45% |
| Mean ± 2.58 SD | 99% |
| Mean ± 3 SD | 99.73% |

8. **Skewness = 0**; kurtosis mesokurtic.
9. Points of inflection at mean ± 1 SD.
10. **Standard normal curve**: mean 0, SD 1 (z-score = (x − mean) ÷ SD).

**Examples**

Height, weight, Hb, BP of healthy adults.

**Uses**

Setting **normal limits** (mean ± 2 SD); z-scores (growth standards); basis of parametric tests and confidence intervals.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.125
**Bar diagram: characteristics**

1. Used for **qualitative (categorical)** or **discrete** data.
2. Bars of **equal width**; only the **length (height)** shows the value.
3. Length proportional to frequency or percentage.
4. **Equal gaps** between bars (bars do not touch).
5. Bars may be **vertical (column)** or **horizontal**.
6. Scale on the value axis **starts from zero**.
7. Bars arranged in a logical order (ascending, descending, alphabetical or time).
8. Clear **title**, axis labels, units, legend (for multiple / component bars).
9. Simple to draw and easy to compare.

**Types**

| Type | Use | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Simple | One variable | Immunisation coverage by vaccine |
| Multiple (grouped) | Two or more variables side by side | Male vs female literacy by division |
| Component (sub-divided) | Parts of a total within one bar | Causes of child deaths each year |
| Proportional (100%) | Percentage composition | Proportion of deliveries by place |

**Bar diagram vs histogram**

| Bar diagram | Histogram |
|---|---|
| Qualitative / discrete data | Continuous data |
| Gaps between bars | Bars touch (no gaps) |
| Width has no meaning | Width = class interval; area = frequency |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.126
**Data**: 12, 14, 16, 18, 20, 22, 25, 30, 35, 40 (n = 10)

**Formula**

Mean (x̄) = Σx ÷ n

**Calculation**

Σx = 12 + 14 + 16 + 18 + 20 + 22 + 25 + 30 + 35 + 40 = 232

x̄ = 232 ÷ 10 = **23.2 years**

**Interpretation**

The average age of the 10 persons is 23.2 years. The data are skewed to the right (a few older ages: 30, 35, 40), so the mean is greater than the median (21 years).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.127
**Data**: 65, 45, 46, 35, 33, 77, 39, 49 (n = 8)

**Mean**

Σx = 65 + 45 + 46 + 35 + 33 + 77 + 39 + 49 = 389

x̄ = 389 ÷ 8 = **48.63** (≈ 48.6)

**Median**

Arrange in ascending order: 33, 35, 39, **45, 46**, 49, 65, 77

n = 8 (even) → median = average of the (n/2)th and (n/2 + 1)th values = 4th and 5th values

Median = (45 + 46) ÷ 2 = **45.5**

**Interpretation**

Mean (48.6) > median (45.5) → slight positive (right) skew due to high values 65 and 77; median better represents the centre.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.128
**Data**: 7, 10, 6, 3, 2, 7, 8, 10, 5, 4 (n = 10)

**Ascending order**

2, 3, 4, 5, **6, 7**, 7, 8, 10, 10

**Median**

n = 10 (even) → average of 5th and 6th values

Median = (6 + 7) ÷ 2 = **6.5**

**Mode**

| Value | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 | 10 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Frequency | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | **2** | 1 | **2** |

7 and 10 each occur twice → **Mode = 7 and 10 (bimodal series)**

(Mean = 62 ÷ 10 = 6.2, for reference.)

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.129
**Data (height, cm)**: 70, 68, 85, 75, 72, 80 (n = 6)

**Ascending order**

68, 70, **72, 75**, 80, 85

**Median**

n = 6 (even) → average of (n/2)th = 3rd and (n/2 + 1)th = 4th values

Median = (72 + 75) ÷ 2 = **73.5 cm**

**Interpretation**

Half of the children are shorter than 73.5 cm and half are taller.

**Note**

Median is not affected by extreme values; useful for skewed data.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.130
**Type of diagram**

Data are **qualitative (categorical)** (levels of mother's education) with a percentage for each → **simple bar diagram** (vertical or horizontal).

**Diagram (simple bar diagram)**

```
Percentage (%)
100 |
 80 |  ████
 60 |  ████     ████
 50 |  ████     ████     ████
 40 |  ████     ████     ████
 20 |  ████     ████     ████
  0 +--------------------------------
       Nil     Primary  Secondary
          Mother's education
```

**Figure**: Percentage by level of mother's education (Nil 80%, Primary 60%, Secondary 50%).

**Features shown**

1. Bars of equal width with equal gaps.
2. Height proportional to percentage; scale starts at zero.
3. Title, axis labels and units given.

**Interpretation**

The percentage falls as mother's education rises (80% → 60% → 50%), showing an inverse relationship between maternal education and the outcome.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.131
**Definition**

**Census**: the total process of collecting, compiling, evaluating, analysing and publishing demographic, economic and social data relating to **all persons** in a country (or defined part) at a **specified time** (UN).

**Essential features**

1. **Individual enumeration** of every person.
2. **Universality** within a defined territory.
3. **Simultaneity**: refers to one reference date/time.
4. **Defined periodicity**: usually every 10 years.
5. Conducted by the government (Bangladesh: BBS).

**Types**

| Basis | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| Place of counting | **De facto** | Counts people where they are found on census night |
| | **De jure** | Counts people at their usual place of residence |
| Coverage | Complete census | Whole population |
| | Sample census | Part of population for detailed questions |
| Subject | Population and housing census; agricultural; economic census | — |

**Bangladesh**

Population and Housing Census every 10 years: 1974, 1981, 1991, 2001, 2011, 2022 (first digital census).

**Uses**

Population size, structure and distribution; denominators for rates; planning of health, education and housing; projections; electoral boundaries.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Demography and Family Planning).

## DU.132
**Types of bar diagram**

**1. Simple bar diagram**: one variable; one bar per category.

```
Coverage (%)
 90 | ████
 80 | ████  ████
 70 | ████  ████  ████
    +------------------
      BCG   Penta3  MR1
```

**2. Multiple (grouped) bar diagram**: two or more related variables side by side for each category; shading distinguishes them.

```
Literacy (%)     ▓ Male  ░ Female
 80 | ▓▓░░
 60 | ▓▓░░   ▓▓░░
 40 | ▓▓░░   ▓▓░░
    +----------------
      Urban   Rural
```

**3. Component (sub-divided) bar diagram**: each bar shows the total, divided into parts.

```
Total deaths
 100 | ░░░ (others)
  60 | ▒▒▒ (diarrhoea)
  30 | ▓▓▓ (pneumonia)
     +-----
       2024
```

**4. Proportional (percentage) bar diagram**: all bars of equal height (100%), divided into percentage components; compares composition across groups.

```
100% | ░░░  ░░░
     | ▒▒▒  ░░░
     | ▓▓▓  ▒▒▒
     +-----------
      Urban Rural
  ▓ Facility  ▒ Home (trained)  ░ Home (untrained)
```

**General rules**

Equal bar width and spacing; scale from zero; title, labels, legend.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.133
**Importance (uses) of vital statistics**

**A. Health**

1. **Measure health status** of a population: CBR, CDR, IMR, MMR, life expectancy.
2. **Compare** health between regions, groups and countries; over time.
3. **Identify problems** and high-risk groups (high neonatal or maternal deaths).
4. **Plan, monitor and evaluate** health programmes (MCH, EPI, FP).
5. **Research**: trends and causes of death; epidemiological studies.
6. Monitor **national and international goals** (SDG 3).

**B. Demographic**

1. Population growth, natural increase, fertility.
2. Population **projections** for planning.

**C. Legal and administrative (individual)**

| Certificate | Uses |
|---|---|
| Birth certificate | Proof of age, nationality; school admission; marriage age; passport, NID; inheritance; employment |
| Death certificate | Inheritance and property transfer; insurance and pension claims; cause-of-death statistics |
| Marriage registration | Legal rights of spouses; prevention of child marriage |

**D. Social and economic planning**

Education, housing, employment, food supply, social security.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics; Demography and Family Planning).

## DU.134
**De facto vs de jure census**

| Point | De facto census | De jure census |
|---|---|---|
| Meaning | Persons counted **where they are found** at the census moment | Persons counted at their **usual place of residence** |
| Basis | Actual presence | Legal / usual residence |
| Visitors | Counted where present | Counted at home |
| Absent residents | Not counted at home | Counted at home |
| Procedure | Simpler; usually done in one night | Longer period; needs rules for "usual residence" |
| Error risk | Less omission or double count | Possible double counting or omission |
| Picture given | Population present (good for services on the spot) | Permanent population (good for planning) |
| Example | Traveller on census night counted at the hotel | Traveller counted at his home district |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Demography and Family Planning).

## DU.135
**Definition**

**Research instrument**: a tool or device used to collect, measure and record data from study subjects in a valid and reliable way.

**Classification**

| Type | Use | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Questionnaire** (self-administered) | Literate respondents; large samples | Online survey on smoking |
| **Interview schedule** (structured / semi-structured / unstructured) | Interviewer asks and records | Household survey on ANC |
| **Observation checklist** | Recording observed behaviour or conditions | Hand-hygiene audit |
| **Measuring instruments** | Physical and physiological measurements | Weighing scale, stadiometer, MUAC tape, sphygmomanometer |
| **Laboratory instruments / tests** | Biochemical and microbiological data | Haemoglobinometer, glucometer, GeneXpert |
| **Rating scales / psychometric tools** | Attitudes, symptoms, quality of life | Likert scale, PHQ-9, WHOQOL-BREF |
| **Record review form (data extraction sheet)** | Secondary data | Hospital record review |
| **Qualitative guides** | FGD guide, in-depth interview guide | Barriers to facility delivery |

**Qualities of a good instrument**

Valid; reliable; simple; acceptable; culturally appropriate; pretested; easy to code and analyse.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics; Principles of Epidemiology).

## DU.136
**Importance of research in biomedical science**

1. **New knowledge**: structure and function of the body, mechanisms of disease.
2. **Causes and risk factors**: e.g., smoking and lung cancer; HPV and cervical cancer.
3. **New diagnostics**: rapid tests, GeneXpert for TB.
4. **New treatments and vaccines**: antibiotics, ART, HPV vaccine.
5. **Evidence-based medicine**: replaces opinion with tested evidence.
6. **Evaluation** of drugs, procedures and programmes (efficacy, safety, cost-effectiveness).
7. **Prevention strategies** and public health policy.
8. **Health systems improvement**: quality, access, efficiency (operational research).
9. **Local problems**: research in the local setting finds suitable solutions.
10. **Training and critical thinking** of doctors.
11. **Economic benefit**: reduces cost of disease; supports pharmaceutical industry.
12. **Ethics and safety**: detects adverse effects (pharmacovigilance).

**Bangladesh examples**

- **ORS** developed and proven at icddr,b; one of the greatest medical advances of the 20th century.
- Research on **zinc** for diarrhoea, **chlorhexidine** cord care, and kala-azar treatment supported national programmes.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Principles of Epidemiology).

## DU.137
**Literature review**

A systematic search, reading and critical summary of published and unpublished work on the research topic.

**Importance**

1. **Shows what is already known** and what is not → identifies the **gap**.
2. **Avoids duplication** of research already done.
3. **Refines the research problem**, question, objectives and hypothesis.
4. **Justifies the study** (rationale, significance).
5. Builds the **conceptual / theoretical framework** and identifies variables.
6. Guides choice of **study design, methods, sample size and instruments** (validated tools).
7. Warns of **problems and biases** faced by others.
8. Helps **interpret and compare results** in the discussion.
9. Provides **references** and credibility.
10. Improves the researcher's knowledge and critical thinking.
11. **Ethical**: prevents exposing subjects to unnecessary research.

**Sources**

Textbooks, journals (PubMed, Google Scholar, BanglaJOL), reports (WHO, BDHS), theses, grey literature.

**Steps**

Search → Select → Read critically → Organise → Write summary → Cite references

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Principles of Epidemiology).

## DU.138
**Definition**

**Research**: a systematic, organised and objective investigation, using the scientific method, to discover new facts, verify existing knowledge and find relationships, in order to solve problems.

**Classification**

| Basis | Types | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Purpose** | Basic (pure) / Applied / Action research | Mechanism of insulin resistance / best diabetes education method / improving CC services |
| **Approach** | Quantitative / Qualitative / Mixed methods | Prevalence of hypertension / reasons for home delivery (FGD) |
| **Design** | Descriptive (case report, case series, cross-sectional) | Prevalence of anaemia |
| | Analytical observational (case-control, cohort) | Smoking and lung cancer |
| | Experimental / interventional (RCT, field trial, community trial) | Vaccine trial |
| **Time** | Cross-sectional / Longitudinal; retrospective / prospective | — |
| **Field** | Biomedical, clinical, epidemiological, health systems / operational research | — |
| **Setting** | Laboratory / clinical (hospital) / field (community) | — |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Principles of Epidemiology).

## DU.139
**Steps of research**

Problem → Literature review → Objectives / hypothesis → Design → Sampling → Tools → Ethics → Data collection → Analysis → Report → Use

1. **Identify and define the research problem** (relevant, feasible, ethical).
2. **Review the literature**.
3. **Formulate research question, objectives** (general, specific) and **hypothesis**.
4. Identify **variables**; draw the **conceptual framework**; operational definitions.
5. Select the **study design** (cross-sectional, case-control, cohort, trial).
6. Define **study population, place and period**; inclusion and exclusion criteria.
7. Decide **sample size and sampling technique**.
8. Choose **data collection methods** and prepare **instruments**; **pretest**.
9. Plan **data management and analysis** (statistical tests, software).
10. Obtain **ethical clearance** (e.g., BMRC / institutional review board) and informed consent.
11. Prepare **work plan, budget** → complete **research protocol**.
12. **Collect data**; supervise quality.
13. **Process and analyse data**.
14. **Interpret** results; draw conclusions.
15. **Write the report** / paper and **disseminate** (seminar, journal).
16. **Utilise findings** for policy and practice.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Principles of Epidemiology).

## DU.140
**Rules of writing an abstract**

**A. Structure (structured abstract)**

| Heading | Content |
|---|---|
| Background / Introduction | Problem and why it matters (1–2 lines) |
| Objective | Aim of the study |
| Methods | Design, setting, period, participants, sample size, sampling, tools, analysis |
| Results | Main findings with key numbers (%, mean, OR, CI, p-value) |
| Conclusion | Main message and recommendation |
| Keywords | 3–6 terms (MeSH) |

**B. Rules**

1. **Brief**: within word limit (usually 150–300 words; follow the journal or conference).
2. **Self-contained**: understandable without the full paper.
3. **Write it last**, after the full report.
4. **Consistent** with the main text; no new information.
5. **No references, tables, figures** or long quotations.
6. Avoid **abbreviations**, or define them at first use.
7. Use **past tense** for methods and results.
8. Give **specific results** with numbers, not vague statements.
9. Conclusion must follow from the results; no over-claiming.
10. Clear, simple language; no jargon.
11. Title and abstract should contain the key words for indexing.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Principles of Epidemiology).

## DU.141
**Three qualities of a good questionnaire**

1. **Valid**: measures what it is meant to measure.
2. **Reliable**: gives consistent answers on repetition.
3. **Simple and clear**: short, easy, unambiguous questions in the respondent's language.

**Other qualities**

4. **Relevant** to study objectives; no unnecessary questions.
5. **Logical sequence**: general to specific; sensitive questions later.
6. **Unbiased**: no leading or loaded questions; no double-barrelled questions.
7. **Appropriate mix** of closed (easy to analyse) and open questions.
8. **Pretested** (pilot study) and revised.
9. Clear **instructions** and skip patterns.
10. Attractive **layout**; easy to code and enter.
11. **Ethical**: consent statement; confidentiality; respects culture.
12. Reasonable **length** (avoids respondent fatigue).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.142
**Definitions**

| Term | Definition | Example (study of hypertension among adults in Mymensingh) |
|---|---|---|
| **Population (target / reference population)** | The whole group of individuals (or units) about whom information is required and to whom results will apply | All adults (≥18 years) of Mymensingh |
| **Study population (accessible population)** | The part of the target population that is actually available and from which the sample is drawn | Adults of selected wards listed in the sampling frame |
| **Sample** | A part (subset) of the population selected for study, which should represent the population | 400 adults selected by random sampling |
| **Sampling** | The process of selecting a sample from the population | Simple random, systematic, cluster sampling |

**Related terms**

- **Sampling unit**: element selected (person, household, village).
- **Sampling frame**: list of all sampling units (voter list, household list).
- **Sample size**: number of units in the sample (e.g., n = z²pq ÷ d²).
- **Sampling error**: difference between sample estimate and true population value due to chance.

**Why sampling**

Saves time, money and manpower; allows better accuracy and supervision; needed when the population is large or testing is destructive.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.143
**Classification of sampling**

**A. Probability (random) sampling**: every unit has a known, non-zero chance of selection → representative; results can be generalised.

| Method | Procedure | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Simple random** | Each unit has an equal chance; lottery or random number table/computer | 50 students chosen by lottery from a list of 500 |
| **Systematic** | Every kᵗʰ unit after a random start (k = N ÷ n) | Every 10th household in a village |
| **Stratified** | Population divided into homogeneous strata (age, sex, urban/rural); random sample from each | Sampling urban and rural mothers separately |
| **Cluster** | Groups (villages, schools) randomly selected; all units in chosen clusters studied | EPI 30-cluster coverage survey |
| **Multistage** | Sampling in stages (district → upazila → union → household) | BDHS |
| Multiphase | Some information from whole sample, more from a sub-sample | Screening all, detailed tests in positives |

**B. Non-probability sampling**: selection not by chance; may not represent the population.

| Method | Procedure | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Convenience | Whoever is easily available | OPD patients on study days |
| Purposive (judgement) | Chosen by researcher for a purpose | Key informants for FGD |
| Quota | Fixed numbers from each category, chosen conveniently | 50 men and 50 women |
| Snowball | Subjects refer others | Injecting drug users |
| Consecutive | All eligible patients in a period | All MI cases admitted in 3 months |

**Simple random sampling (example)**

To select 50 out of 500 school children: number them 1–500 → draw 50 numbers by lottery or random number table → each child has an equal (1 in 10) chance.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.144
**Types of probability sampling**

| Type | Description | Example |
|---|---|---|
| 1. **Simple random sampling** | Every unit has an equal and independent chance; lottery, random number table or computer | Selecting 100 patients from a list of 1,000 registered diabetics |
| 2. **Systematic random sampling** | First unit random, then every kᵗʰ unit (sampling interval k = N ÷ n) | N = 2,000 households, n = 200 → every 10th household after a random start |
| 3. **Stratified random sampling** | Population divided into strata by a characteristic; random sample from each (proportionate or not) | Selecting students separately from each year of MBBS |
| 4. **Cluster sampling** | Naturally occurring groups (clusters) randomly selected; all members studied | 30 villages selected; all under-5 children examined |
| 5. **Multistage sampling** | Random selection in successive stages | Division → district → upazila → union → household |
| 6. **Multiphase sampling** | All sampled units give basic data; a sub-sample gives detailed data | All children screened for anaemia; detailed tests in a sub-sample |

**Merits of probability sampling**

Representative; free from selection bias; sampling error can be measured; results can be generalised.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.145
**Sample vs population**

| Point | Population | Sample |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Meaning | Whole group of units about which information is required | Part (subset) of the population selected for study |
| 2. Size | Large (N) | Smaller (n) |
| 3. Measures | **Parameters** (μ, σ, P), fixed but usually unknown | **Statistics** (x̄, s, p), vary from sample to sample; estimate parameters |
| 4. Study method | Census (complete enumeration) | Sampling survey |
| 5. Cost and time | High | Low |
| 6. Error | No sampling error (may have non-sampling errors) | Sampling error present |
| 7. Accuracy of collection | Hard to supervise large numbers | Better supervision, more detailed data |
| 8. Example | All adults of Bangladesh | 2,000 adults in a national survey |

**Three important differences (short answer)**

1. Population is the whole; sample is a part.
2. Population values are **parameters**; sample values are **statistics**.
3. Sample has **sampling error**; studying a sample is cheaper and quicker.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.146
**a) Study design and why**

**Descriptive cross-sectional study**.
Why:
1. Aim is **prevalence** (existing cases at one point in time).
2. Exposure and outcome measured **once**, at the same time; no follow-up needed.
3. Quick, cheap, feasible in a community.
4. Gives baseline data for planning services for the elderly.

**b) Sample and sampling technique**

- **Sample**: a representative number of senior citizens (aged ≥60 years) living in old Dhaka, selected from the population, who meet inclusion criteria (permanent residents, consent) and exclusion criteria (seriously ill, unable to respond).
- **Sample size**: n = z²pq ÷ d² (z = 1.96; p = expected prevalence of hypertension; q = 1 − p; d = allowable error).
- **Sampling technique**: **multistage (cluster) random sampling**:
  Old Dhaka wards → random selection of wards (clusters) → random selection of mohallas → systematic random selection of households → all eligible elderly persons in selected households.
  (If a complete list of the elderly is available: simple or systematic random sampling.)

**c) Population**

- **Target population**: all senior citizens (≥60 years) residing in old Dhaka.
- **Study population**: senior citizens of the selected wards who are available during the study period.

**Outcome measurement**

BP measured with a standard sphygmomanometer (two readings); hypertension = SBP ≥140 and/or DBP ≥90 mmHg or on treatment.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Principles of Epidemiology; Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.147
**Measures of central tendency (statistical averages)**

A single value that represents the centre of a set of data.

| Measure | Definition | Formula / method | Example (2, 3, 3, 5, 7) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Arithmetic mean** | Sum of observations ÷ number | x̄ = Σx ÷ n; grouped: Σfx ÷ Σf | 20 ÷ 5 = 4 |
| **Median** | Middle value when data are arranged in order | Odd n: (n + 1)/2 th value; even n: average of two middle values | 3 |
| **Mode** | Most frequently occurring value | Value with highest frequency | 3 |
| Geometric mean | nth root of product of n values | antilog (Σ log x ÷ n) | Antibody titres, bacterial counts |
| Harmonic mean | Reciprocal of mean of reciprocals | n ÷ Σ(1/x) | Rates, speeds |

**Merits and demerits**

| Measure | Merits | Demerits | Best used for |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mean | Uses all values; basis for further statistics | Affected by extreme values | Symmetrical data (Hb, height) |
| Median | Not affected by extremes; easy | Ignores actual values; less used in tests | Skewed data (income, survival time) |
| Mode | Easy; usable for qualitative data | May be absent or multiple; unstable | Most common category (commonest blood group) |

**Relationship**

Symmetrical distribution: mean = median = mode. Skewed: Mode ≈ 3 Median − 2 Mean.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.148
**Data**: 1, 2, 3, 3, 3, 4, 5 (n = 7; already in ascending order)

**Mean**

Σx = 1 + 2 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 4 + 5 = 21
x̄ = 21 ÷ 7 = **3**

**Median**

n = 7 (odd) → median = (n + 1)/2 = 4th value
1, 2, 3, **3**, 3, 4, 5 → Median = **3**

**Mode**

3 occurs 3 times (most frequent) → Mode = **3**

**Interpretation**

Mean = median = mode = 3 → the distribution is **symmetrical**.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.149
**Data**: 4, 7, 5, 8, 6, 9, 7, 10, 8 (n = 9)

**Mean**

Σx = 4 + 7 + 5 + 8 + 6 + 9 + 7 + 10 + 8 = 64
x̄ = 64 ÷ 9 = **7.11**

**Median**

Ascending order: 4, 5, 6, 7, **7**, 8, 8, 9, 10
n = 9 (odd) → (n + 1)/2 = 5th value → Median = **7**

**(Mode)**: 7 and 8 each occur twice → bimodal (7, 8).

**Interpretation**

Mean (7.11) and median (7) are almost equal → nearly symmetrical distribution.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.150
**Formula (grouped data)**

Mean x̄ = Σfx ÷ Σf, where x = mid-point of class interval

**Calculation**

| Age (years) | Mid-point (x) | Frequency (f) | fx |
|---|---|---|---|
| 5–9 | 7 | 6 | 42 |
| 10–14 | 12 | 8 | 96 |
| 15–19 | 17 | 9 | 153 |
| 20–24 | 22 | 5 | 110 |
| 25–29 | 27 | 3 | 81 |
| 30–34 | 32 | 2 | 64 |
| **Total** | | **Σf = 33** | **Σfx = 546** |

Mid-point = (lower limit + upper limit) ÷ 2, e.g., (5 + 9) ÷ 2 = 7

x̄ = 546 ÷ 33 = **16.55 years**

**Interpretation**

The mean age of the 33 persons is about 16.5 years; the modal class is 15–19 years (highest frequency, 9).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.151
**Data (Hb of 8 pregnant mothers)**: 8, 8.5, 9.5, 10, 10.5, 11, 13, 12
(Unit should be **g/dl**, not mg/dl.)

**Median**

Ascending order: 8, 8.5, 9.5, **10, 10.5**, 11, 12, 13
n = 8 (even) → average of 4th and 5th values
Median = (10 + 10.5) ÷ 2 = **10.25 g/dl**

**Interpretation**

Half the mothers have Hb below 10.25 g/dl; since anaemia in pregnancy is Hb <11 g/dl (WHO), 5 of 8 mothers (62.5%) are anaemic.

**Graphical presentation (simple bar diagram of each mother's Hb)**

```
Hb (g/dl)
13 |                               ██
12 |                          ██   ██
11 |                     ██   ██   ██
10 |           ██   ██   ██   ██   ██
 9 |      ██   ██   ██   ██   ██   ██
 8 | ██   ██   ██   ██   ██   ██   ██   ██
   +---------------------------------------
     M1   M2   M3   M4   M5   M6   M7   M8
 (M1 = 8, M2 = 8.5, M3 = 9.5, M4 = 10, M5 = 10.5, M6 = 11, M7 = 12, M8 = 13; arranged in ascending order)
```

Alternatively: dot plot along an Hb scale, with a line at 11 g/dl marking the anaemia cut-off.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics); WHO, haemoglobin cut-offs for anaemia.

## DU.152
**Data**: 52, 65, 52, 60, 45 (n = 5)

**Mean**

Σx = 52 + 65 + 52 + 60 + 45 = 274
x̄ = 274 ÷ 5 = **54.8**

**Median**

Ascending order: 45, 52, **52**, 60, 65
n = 5 (odd) → (n + 1)/2 = 3rd value → Median = **52**

**Mode**

52 occurs twice → Mode = **52**

**Interpretation**

Mean (54.8) > median (52) = mode (52) → slight positive skew (pulled up by 65).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.153
**a) Flowchart: cohort study to find outcomes of tobacco consumption**

Defined population free of the outcomes (e.g., adults without cancer or CHD)
→ Classify by exposure:
  **Exposed**: tobacco users | **Non-exposed**: non-users
→ Follow up both groups over time (e.g., 10 years)
→ Record outcomes: lung / oral cancer, CHD, stroke, COPD (develop / do not develop)
→ Calculate incidence in exposed and non-exposed
→ Relative risk = incidence in exposed ÷ incidence in non-exposed; attributable risk = difference

| | Outcome + | Outcome − | Total |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tobacco users | a | b | a + b |
| Non-users | c | d | c + d |

RR = [a/(a + b)] ÷ [c/(c + d)]

**b) Standard deviation of age**

Data: 83, 75, 81, 79, 71, 95, 75, 77, 84, 90 (n = 10)
Mean = 810 ÷ 10 = **81 years**

| x | x − x̄ | (x − x̄)² |
|---|---|---|
| 83 | 2 | 4 |
| 75 | −6 | 36 |
| 81 | 0 | 0 |
| 79 | −2 | 4 |
| 71 | −10 | 100 |
| 95 | 14 | 196 |
| 75 | −6 | 36 |
| 77 | −4 | 16 |
| 84 | 3 | 9 |
| 90 | 9 | 81 |
| | | **Σ = 482** |

SD = √[Σ(x − x̄)² ÷ (n − 1)] = √(482 ÷ 9) = √53.56 = **7.32 years**
(Small sample, n <30, so n − 1 is used; with n as divisor, SD = √48.2 = 6.94 years.)

**Interpretation**: ages vary on average by about 7.3 years around the mean of 81 years.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Principles of Epidemiology; Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.154
**Data**: 62, 75, 62, 70, 55, 52, 56, 68, 46, 63, 50 (n = 11)

**Mean**

Σx = 62 + 75 + 62 + 70 + 55 + 52 + 56 + 68 + 46 + 63 + 50 = 659
x̄ = 659 ÷ 11 = **59.91**

**Median**

Ascending order: 46, 50, 52, 55, 56, **62**, 62, 63, 68, 70, 75
n = 11 (odd) → (n + 1)/2 = 6th value → Median = **62**

**Mode**

62 occurs twice → Mode = **62**

**Interpretation**

Mean (59.9) < median (62) = mode (62) → slight negative (left) skew, pulled down by low values (46, 50).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.155
**Simple random sample (SRS)**

A sample in which **every unit of the population has an equal and independent chance** of being selected; drawn by lottery, random number table or computer from a complete sampling frame.
Example: selecting 50 children by lottery from a school list of 500.

**Calculation**

Data: 52, 65, 52, 60, 45, 42, 46, 58, 36, 52, 40 (n = 11)

**Mean**

Σx = 52 + 65 + 52 + 60 + 45 + 42 + 46 + 58 + 36 + 52 + 40 = 548
x̄ = 548 ÷ 11 = **49.82**

**Median**

Ascending order: 36, 40, 42, 45, 46, **52**, 52, 52, 58, 60, 65
n = 11 (odd) → 6th value → Median = **52**

**Mode**

52 occurs 3 times → Mode = **52**

**Interpretation**

Mean (49.8) < median = mode (52) → slight negative skew.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.156
**Measures of dispersion (variation)**

Show how widely observations are scattered around the central value.

| Measure | Definition / formula | Example (2, 4, 6, 8, 10; mean 6) |
|---|---|---|
| 1. **Range** | Highest − lowest value | 10 − 2 = 8 |
| 2. **Interquartile range** | Q3 − Q1 (middle 50% of data) | Used with median in skewed data |
| 3. **Mean deviation** | Σ\|x − x̄\| ÷ n | (4 + 2 + 0 + 2 + 4) ÷ 5 = 2.4 |
| 4. **Standard deviation (SD)** | √[Σ(x − x̄)² ÷ (n − 1)] | √(40 ÷ 4) = 3.16 |
| 5. **Variance** | SD² | 10 |
| 6. **Coefficient of variation (CV)** | (SD ÷ mean) × 100 | (3.16 ÷ 6) × 100 = 52.7% |

**Notes**

- **SD** is the most widely used and most reliable measure.
- **CV** compares variability of different variables or units (e.g., height vs weight).
- **Range** is simplest but depends only on extreme values.

**Uses**

Judge reliability of mean; compare variability between groups; define normal limits (mean ± 2 SD); needed for sample size and tests of significance.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.157
**Data (DBP, mmHg)**: 83, 75, 81, 79, 71, 90, 75, 95, 77, 94

**Range**

Range = highest value − lowest value
Highest = 95 mmHg; lowest = 71 mmHg
Range = 95 − 71 = **24 mmHg** (71 to 95 mmHg)

**Interpretation**

Diastolic pressures of the 10 people spread over 24 mmHg.

**Note**

Range is the simplest measure of dispersion but uses only two extreme values; SD is more reliable.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.158
**Proportion**

Proportion = (number with the characteristic ÷ total in the group from which they come) × 100

**Calculation**

Children with PEM = 50; total children = 500

Proportion of PEM among children = (50 ÷ 500) × 100 = **10%**

**Note**

- The denominator must be **children** (the population at risk), not the total slum population (2,000), because the question asks about PEM among children.
- 2,000 would give PEM per total population = (50 ÷ 2,000) × 100 = 2.5%, which is not the asked measure.

**Interpretation**

One in every ten children in the slum has protein-energy malnutrition; this is the **prevalence** of PEM among children.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

Median = (45 + 52) ÷ 2 = **48.5 years**

## DU.160
**Data (MUAC, cm; n = 22)**: 20, 19, 22, 16, 18, 17, 14, 20, 12, 18, 16, 17, 21, 24, 22, 20, 18, 19, 20, 18, 17, 20

**Frequency table**

| MUAC (x) | 12 | 14 | 16 | 17 | 18 | 19 | 20 | 21 | 22 | 24 | Total |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| f | 1 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 2 | **5** | 1 | 2 | 1 | 22 |
| fx | 12 | 14 | 32 | 51 | 72 | 38 | 100 | 21 | 44 | 24 | **408** |

**Mean**

x̄ = Σfx ÷ n = 408 ÷ 22 = **18.55 cm**

**Median**

Ascending order: 12, 14, 16, 16, 17, 17, 17, 18, 18, 18, **18, 19**, 19, 20, 20, 20, 20, 20, 21, 22, 22, 24
n = 22 (even) → average of 11th and 12th values = (18 + 19) ÷ 2 = **18.5 cm**

## DU.161
**Data**: 1, 2, 3, 5, 7, 9 (n = 6; already in ascending order)

**Mean**

Σx = 1 + 2 + 3 + 5 + 7 + 9 = 27
x̄ = 27 ÷ 6 = **4.5**

**Median**

n = 6 (even) → average of 3rd and 4th values
1, 2, **3, 5**, 7, 9 → Median = (3 + 5) ÷ 2 = **4**

**Interpretation**

Mean (4.5) > median (4) → slight positive skew (higher values 7 and 9).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.162
**Data (SBP, mmHg; 10 values as printed)**: 120, 130, 125, 140, 135, 128, 132, 130, 138, 126

**Mean**

Σx = 120 + 130 + 125 + 140 + 135 + 128 + 132 + 130 + 138 + 126 = 1304
x̄ = 1304 ÷ 10 = **130.4 mmHg**

**Median**

Ascending order: 120, 125, 126, 128, **130, 130**, 132, 135, 138, 140
n = 10 (even) → average of 5th and 6th values
Median = (130 + 130) ÷ 2 = **130 mmHg**

**Mode**

130 occurs twice → Mode = **130 mmHg**

**Interpretation**

Mean (130.4) ≈ median (130) = mode (130) → nearly **symmetrical** distribution; average SBP of OPD adults is about 130 mmHg.

**Note**

If 11 values are given in the paper, add the extra value, recompute Σx ÷ 11, and take the 6th ordered value as the median.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.163
**a) Greater variability (2)**

**Group B** (SD = 20 mmHg) has greater variability than Group A (SD = 5 mmHg), though both have the same mean (120 mmHg).

**b) Interpretation of SD (3)**

Assuming normal distribution:

| Range | Group A (120 ± 5) | Group B (120 ± 20) |
|---|---|---|
| Mean ± 1 SD (~68%) | 115–125 mmHg | 100–140 mmHg |
| Mean ± 2 SD (~95%) | 110–130 mmHg | 80–160 mmHg |
| Mean ± 3 SD (~99.7%) | 105–135 mmHg | 60–180 mmHg |

- **Group A**: SBP values lie close to the mean; members are similar (homogeneous); the mean represents the group well.
- **Group B**: SBP values are widely scattered; some members may be hypotensive and others hypertensive (≥140 mmHg); the mean alone is misleading.

**Coefficient of variation**

CV(A) = (5 ÷ 120) × 100 = **4.2%**; CV(B) = (20 ÷ 120) × 100 = **16.7%**

**c) More consistent (2)**

**Group A** is more consistent (uniform): smaller SD and CV, less spread of values around the mean.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Information and Basic Medical Statistics).

## DU.164
**Why communication skills are essential for health education**

Health education is basically a **communication process**: Communicator → Message → Channel → Audience → Feedback. Its success depends on the communicator's skill.

1. **Information transfer**: correct facts must reach people clearly (e.g., EPI schedule, danger signs).
2. **Understanding (comprehension)**: messages must be in the audience's language and level; jargon blocks learning.
3. **Motivation and persuasion**: skilful communication arouses interest and changes attitudes, not just knowledge.
4. **Behaviour change**: the goal of health education is adoption of healthy practices; this needs two-way, convincing communication.
5. **Credibility and trust**: good communicators win confidence of the community.
6. **Feedback**: lets the educator check understanding and correct misconceptions.
7. **Overcoming barriers**: physiological, psychological, environmental and cultural barriers can be reduced by skilled communicators.
8. **Choice of methods and media**: right method (individual, group, mass) and AV aids for the audience.
9. **Counselling**: sensitive issues (family planning, HIV, mental illness) need empathy and listening.
10. **Community participation**: dialogue builds partnership.
11. **Compliance**: patients follow treatment better when it is explained well (TB, diabetes).
12. **Doctor–patient relationship** improves; satisfaction rises.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.165
**Components (elements) of communication**

| Component | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| 1. **Communicator (sender, source)** | Person who originates the message | Doctor, health worker |
| 2. **Message** | Information, ideas or feelings sent (content) | "Wash hands with soap before eating" |
| 3. **Encoding** | Converting ideas into words, symbols, gestures | Simple Bangla words, pictures |
| 4. **Channel (medium)** | Means by which message travels | Face-to-face, radio, TV, poster, mobile |
| 5. **Audience (receiver)** | Person or group receiving the message | Mothers, school children |
| 6. **Decoding** | Receiver interprets the message | Understanding the advice |
| 7. **Feedback** | Receiver's response returned to sender | Questions, demonstration, change in practice |
| 8. **Noise / barriers** | Anything that distorts the message | Loud environment, language difference |
| 9. **Effect** | Change in knowledge, attitude, practice | Mothers start using ORS |

**Communication process (model)**

Communicator → (encoding) → Message → Channel → (decoding) → Audience → Effect
          ↑______________________ Feedback ______________________|

(Noise can interfere at every step.)

**Functions of communication**

1. Information
2. Education
3. Motivation
4. Persuasion
5. Counselling
6. Raising morale
7. Health development
8. Organisation (coordinating people and activities)

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.166
**Motive**

An internal state (need or desire) that energises and directs behaviour towards a goal.

**Types of motives**

| Type | Meaning | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Primary (biological / physiological)** | Inborn, unlearned; necessary for survival | Hunger, thirst, sleep, sex, avoidance of pain, need for air, temperature regulation |
| **Secondary (social / psychological / learned)** | Acquired through experience and society | Affiliation (belonging), achievement, power, approval, recognition, money, security |
| **Intrinsic** | Arise from within; activity is rewarding in itself | Curiosity, satisfaction of learning |
| **Extrinsic** | Arise from external rewards or pressures | Prize, salary, fear of punishment |
| General (unlearned, non-physiological) | Curiosity, exploration, need for activity, contact comfort | Child exploring surroundings |

**In health education**

- Motivation is the central task: "people must feel the need" (felt need).
- Use secondary motives (child's health, family approval, social status) to promote behaviour change, e.g., mothers immunise children for their child's safety.
- **Incentives** (rewards) act as external motivators.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.167
**A. In medical education**

1. **Clear learning objectives** (knowledge, attitude, skill) shared with students.
2. **Two-way, interactive teaching**: questions, discussion, problem-based learning, case-based learning.
3. **Small-group teaching**: tutorials, seminars, bedside teaching.
4. **Audio-visual aids**: slides, videos, models, simulation and skills labs.
5. **Simple, organised language**; known → unknown; simple → complex.
6. **Feedback**: formative assessment, constructive comments, OSCE debriefing.
7. **Role modelling** of good communication with patients.
8. **Communication skills training**: history-taking, counselling, breaking bad news, role play with simulated patients.
9. **Digital platforms**: learning management systems, online resources.
10. Good teacher–student relationship; mentorship.

**B. In research**

1. Clear **research question** and protocol shared with the team.
2. **Informed consent** explained in the participant's language.
3. Standardised **instructions and training** of data collectors.
4. Regular **team meetings**; clear roles.
5. Communication with **ethics committees** and stakeholders.
6. **Scientific writing**: structured abstract, IMRaD format, tables and graphs.
7. **Dissemination**: seminars, conferences, journal publication, policy briefs, plain-language summaries for the community.
8. Feedback of results to participants and communities.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.168
**Criteria of a sender with good communication skill**

1. **Knowledge** of the subject; accurate information.
2. **Clear objective**: knows what change he wants.
3. **Credibility**: trustworthy, respected, sincere.
4. **Knows the audience**: their language, level, needs, culture, beliefs.
5. **Simple, clear language**; avoids jargon.
6. **Good listener**; encourages questions.
7. **Empathy** and respect for the audience.
8. **Good non-verbal skills**: eye contact, posture, facial expression, tone.
9. **Uses feedback** to check understanding and adjust the message.
10. **Organised** presentation: logical, brief, relevant.
11. **Uses suitable methods and AV aids**.
12. **Confidence**, clear voice and modulation.
13. **Patience** and good human relations.
14. **Sets an example** (practises what he preaches).
15. **Persuasive and motivating**.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.169
**Audio-visual (AV) aids**

Devices that appeal to the senses of hearing and sight to make communication and learning more effective.

**Classification (Park)**

| Group | Examples |
|---|---|
| **1. Auditory aids** | Radio, tape and digital audio, public address system (miking), telephone, podcasts |
| **2. Visual aids** (a) not requiring projection | Chalkboard / whiteboard, leaflets, posters, charts, flannel graph, flash cards, exhibits, models, specimens, photographs |
| (b) requiring projection | Slides, film strips, overhead projector, multimedia projector |
| **3. Combined audio-visual aids** | Television, sound films, video, cinema, computer and mobile multimedia, internet |

**Importance / advantages**

1. **Attract attention** and arouse interest.
2. **Clarify** difficult concepts (models, diagrams).
3. **Better retention**: more senses involved ("seeing is believing").
4. **Overcome language and literacy barriers** (pictures).
5. **Save time** in explanation.
6. **Reach large audiences** (TV, radio).
7. Make learning **concrete and realistic**.
8. **Stimulate discussion** and participation.
9. **Reinforce** messages.
10. Increase **credibility** of messages.

**Limitations**

Cost, need for electricity and equipment, can distract if overused, need pretesting.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.170
