**Criteria of a good message (Park)**

1. In line with the **objectives** of the programme.
2. **Meaningful**: relevant to the audience.
3. Based on **felt needs** of the audience.
4. **Clear and understandable**: simple language, no jargon.
5. **Specific**: tells exactly what to do.
6. **Accurate** and scientifically correct.
7. **Timely**: given when needed (e.g., dengue messages before monsoon).
8. **Adequate**: neither too little nor too much.
9. **Fits the culture** and social context of the audience.
10. **Interesting**: attracts attention and arouses curiosity.

**Other qualities**

- **Brief** and easy to remember (slogans).
- **Positive** tone; gives hope and solution, not just fear.
- **Consistent** across channels; repeated for reinforcement.
- **Pretested** on a sample audience.
- **Credible** source.

**Example**

"Wash hands with soap and water for 20 seconds before eating and after using the toilet."

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.171
**Barriers of communication (Park)**

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| **1. Physiological** | Difficulty in hearing (deafness), poor vision, aphasia, speech defects, fatigue, pain |
| **2. Psychological** | Emotional disturbance, anxiety, fear, neurosis, low intelligence, poor attention, prejudice, comprehension difficulty |
| **3. Environmental (physical)** | Noise, poor visibility, congestion, poor lighting, interruptions, distance, faulty equipment |
| **4. Cultural** | Illiteracy, differences in levels of knowledge and understanding, customs, beliefs, religion, attitudes, economic and social class differences, language, cultural gap between communicator and audience |

**Other barriers**

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| Semantic / language | Medical jargon, different dialect (Chattogram, Sylhet), indigenous languages |
| Organisational | Rigid hierarchy, overcrowded OPD, short consultation time |
| Interpersonal | Lack of trust, poor rapport, arrogance, gender difference |
| Technological | Poor network, no electricity |
| Message-related | Too long, too technical, contradictory messages |

**Example**

A doctor uses English medical terms to explain insulin use to an illiterate rural woman in a noisy OPD → language, cultural, environmental and psychological (anxiety) barriers together.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.172
**Functions of health communication (Park)**

| Function | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| 1. **Information** | Basic function: spreading knowledge about health | Dengue symptoms and prevention |
| 2. **Education** | Systematic learning to change knowledge, attitude and practice | School health education |
| 3. **Motivation** | Arousing interest and desire to act | Encouraging ANC visits |
| 4. **Persuasion** | Convincing people to adopt a practice | Quitting smoking |
| 5. **Counselling** | Helping individuals understand problems and decide | Family planning, HIV counselling |
| 6. **Raising morale** | Building confidence and team spirit | Encouraging health workers during epidemics |
| 7. **Health development** | Promoting community action for better health | Community-led total sanitation |
| 8. **Organisation** | Coordinating people and activities | Planning vaccination campaigns |

**Other roles**

- Advocacy for healthy policies (tobacco control).
- Risk and crisis communication in outbreaks (COVID-19).
- Increasing demand for and use of services.
- Correcting misinformation and rumours.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.173
**Lecture as one-way communication**

In a lecture, the **flow of communication is from the communicator to the audience only** (didactic method); the audience listens passively, with little or no chance to ask questions or give feedback.

Lecturer → Message → Passive audience (no feedback)

**Features**

1. Authoritative; knowledge "imposed".
2. Audience passive.
3. No feedback; speaker cannot check understanding.
4. Fast; covers much content to many people.

**Disadvantages of one-way communication**

1. Learning is **passive**; little participation.
2. **No feedback**; misunderstandings remain.
3. Little effect on **attitude and behaviour**.
4. Poor **retention**; attention declines after 15–20 minutes.
5. Ignores individual differences.
6. Can be boring; monotonous.
7. Audience needs and doubts not addressed.

**Advantages**

Saves time; reaches large groups; useful for introducing new information.

**Making a lecture two-way**

Ask and invite questions; brief discussion; AV aids; demonstrations; case examples; quizzes; follow-up group work.

**Two-way (Socratic) communication**: communicator and audience both take part → active learning, feedback, more likely to change behaviour.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.174
**Types of communication**

| Basis | Type | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Direction** | **One-way** (didactic) | Lecture, radio talk |
| | **Two-way** (Socratic) | Discussion, counselling |
| **Mode** | **Verbal**: oral | Talk, telephone |
| | **Verbal**: written | Letters, leaflets, prescriptions |
| | **Non-verbal** | Gestures, facial expression, posture, eye contact, touch |
| | **Visual** | Charts, posters, pictures, maps |
| | **Telecommunication and internet** | TV, mobile phone, SMS, social media, email |
| **Formality** | **Formal** | Official orders, reports, meetings |
| | **Informal** ("grapevine") | Chat among colleagues |
| **Number involved** | Intrapersonal | Thinking, self-talk |
| | Interpersonal | Doctor–patient talk |
| | Group | Group discussion with mothers |
| | Mass | TV campaign |
| **Organisational flow** | Downward / upward / horizontal | Director to staff / staff report / peer coordination |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.175
**Traditional / folk media**

Indigenous forms of entertainment and communication rooted in local culture, used to carry health messages.

**Bangladesh examples**

Jatra (folk theatre), puppet show (*putul nach*), *kobigan* (poetic duel), *jari-sari* and *baul* songs, *gombhira* (Rajshahi), *pot gaan* (scroll painting with songs), *pala gaan*, street drama (*pathanatok*), storytelling, *haat-bazaar* announcements, folk proverbs.

**How they influence communication**

1. **Culturally familiar** → high acceptance and credibility.
2. **Local language and dialect** → easy to understand.
3. **Reach illiterate and rural people** who lack access to print media.
4. **Entertaining** → attracts large crowds and holds attention.
5. **Participatory**: audience reacts, sings along; allows discussion and feedback.
6. **Emotional appeal** → can change attitudes, not just knowledge.
7. **Low cost**; local artists and materials.
8. **Flexible**: messages can be adapted to local problems.
9. **Respects tradition** → less resistance to sensitive topics (family planning, child marriage, HIV).

**Health uses in Bangladesh**

Family planning, EPI, ORS, breastfeeding, sanitation, arsenic awareness, dengue, HIV prevention, anti-dowry and anti-child-marriage campaigns.

**Limitations**

Limited reach compared to mass media; quality depends on performers; risk of message distortion; needs pretesting and monitoring.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.176
**Potential key persons (PKP)**

Respected, influential persons in a community through whom health messages can be passed effectively (opinion leaders, gatekeepers).

**PKPs in a Bangladeshi community**

1. **Religious leaders**: imam, khatib, priest, monk.
2. **Teachers** of schools and madrasas.
3. **Local government representatives**: union parishad chairman and members, ward councillor.
4. **Village elders** (*matbor*, *murubbi*).
5. **Community health workers**: CHCP, health assistant, FWA, NGO volunteers.
6. **Traditional birth attendants** (*dai*) and village doctors.
7. **Women's group and mothers' club leaders**.
8. **Youth club, sports and cultural leaders**.
9. **Community group (CG) members** of community clinics.
10. **Local businessmen**, pharmacy owners.
11. **Students** (child-to-child approach).
12. **Local journalists** and social media influencers.

**Why involve PKPs**

Trusted; influence attitudes and norms; link health system with community; mobilise people and resources; help counter rumours (e.g., vaccine hesitancy).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.177
**a) Characteristics of effective communication (2)**

1. **Clear**: simple language the patient understands (Bangla, local dialect).
2. **Concise and specific**: only key points (e.g., "avoid sweets, eat more vegetables").
3. **Correct and complete**: accurate, all needed information.
4. **Two-way**: listening, questions, **feedback** (teach-back).
5. **Relevant** to patient's needs and culture (local foods).
6. **Courteous and empathetic**; respectful.
7. **Supported by aids**: food pictures, diet chart.
8. **Right time and place**: patient attentive, quiet setting.

**b) Other barriers of communication (2)** (besides language and poor attention)

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| Physiological | Hearing or vision problems, fatigue, pain, illness |
| Psychological | Anxiety, fear, denial, depression, low intelligence, prejudice |
| Environmental | Noise, overcrowded OPD, lack of privacy, interruptions, little time |
| Cultural | Illiteracy; food beliefs and taboos; religious practices (fasting); social class gap |
| Semantic | Medical jargon ("glycaemic index", "carbohydrate") |
| Interpersonal | Lack of trust, doctor's hurried or rude manner |

**c) Improving doctor–patient relationship (3)**

1. Use the **patient's language**; interpreter if needed; avoid jargon.
2. **Listen actively**; give adequate time; show empathy and respect.
3. **Explain** diagnosis and plan; use **teach-back** to confirm understanding.
4. **Involve the patient and family** in decisions (shared decision-making).
5. Use **AV aids**: printed diet chart with pictures.
6. Maintain **privacy and confidentiality**.
7. Be **honest** about cost, prognosis and options; respect beliefs.
8. **Follow-up** and continuity of care; accessible for questions.
9. System support: reduce OPD crowding, appointment systems, communication skills training, complaint handling.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education; Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.178
**Definitions**

1. **Park (National Conference on Preventive Medicine, USA)**: a process that **informs, motivates and helps** people to adopt and maintain healthy practices and lifestyles, advocates environmental changes needed for this goal, and conducts professional training and research to the same end.
2. **Alma-Ata (1978)**: "education concerning prevailing health problems and the methods of preventing and controlling them" (first element of PHC).
3. **WHO (Health Promotion Glossary, 1998)**: consciously constructed opportunities for learning, involving communication, designed to improve **health literacy** (knowledge and life skills) for individual and community health.

**Key features**

1. A process of **learning**, not just giving information.
2. Aims at change in **knowledge, attitude and practice (KAP)**.
3. Voluntary; based on people's participation.
4. Translates health knowledge into desirable behaviour.
5. Part of **health promotion** and **primary prevention**.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.179
**Objectives of health education (WHO; Park)**

| Objective | Example |
|---|---|
| 1. To encourage people to **adopt and sustain health-promoting lifestyles and practices** | Hand-washing, balanced diet, exercise, no tobacco |
| 2. To promote the **proper use of health services** available to them | ANC, facility delivery, immunisation, DOTS completion |
| 3. To arouse interest, provide new knowledge, improve skills and change attitudes so people make **rational decisions** to solve their own problems | Mothers learning to prepare ORS and recognise pneumonia danger signs |
| 4. To stimulate **individual and community self-reliance and participation** in health development, from identifying problems to solving them | Community dengue source-reduction drives; community clinic management |

**Other aims**

- Promote **positive health** and prevent disease.
- Develop **health literacy**.
- Support **healthy public policy** and environmental change.
- Reduce **stigma** (TB, HIV, mental illness).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.180
**Approaches to health education (Park)**

| Approach | Meaning | Example | Limitation |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Regulatory (legal) approach** | Laws and regulations to protect health | Tobacco control law, food adulteration laws, helmet law | Behaviour cannot be changed by law alone; enforcement difficult |
| **2. Service approach** | Providing health services to people (taking services to their doorstep) | Free latrines, tube wells | Failed when people's felt needs and beliefs were ignored; services not used |
| **3. Health education approach** | Educating people to adopt healthy behaviour voluntarily | Mass campaigns, counselling | Slow; results take time |
| **4. Primary health care approach** | Health education as part of PHC with community participation, starting from people's own needs ("bottom-up") | Community clinics, CHWs, community-led total sanitation | Needs organisation and commitment |

**Models of health education**

1. **Medical model**: emphasis on disease prevention by medical interventions; health education as an appendage.
2. **Motivation model**: individual motivation through knowledge leads to behaviour change (KAP).
3. **Social intervention model**: changing the social and physical environment (policy, community action) to support healthy behaviour.

**Best approach**

A **combination**, with the PHC approach (community participation) as the core.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.181
**Stages of adoption of new ideas and practices (Park)**

Awareness → Interest → Evaluation → Trial → Adoption

| Stage | What happens | Example (exclusive breastfeeding) |
|---|---|---|
| 1. **Awareness** | Person first hears of the new idea; knows little | Mother hears about exclusive breastfeeding on TV |
| 2. **Interest** | Seeks more information, details | Asks the CHCP what it means |
| 3. **Evaluation** | Weighs advantages and disadvantages for herself; mental trial | Thinks about whether her baby will get enough |
| 4. **Trial** | Tries the practice on a small scale | Breastfeeds only for a few days |
| 5. **Adoption** | Accepts and practises regularly after satisfaction | Continues exclusive breastfeeding to 6 months |

**Notes**

1. People move through stages at different speeds; some reject at any stage.
2. Adopter categories: innovators → early adopters → early majority → late majority → laggards.
3. Health educator must support each stage: information (awareness), counselling (interest, evaluation), demonstration and support (trial), reinforcement (adoption).
4. **Rejection** can occur at any stage; follow-up prevents relapse.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.182
**Principles of health education (Park)**

1. **Interest**: people learn what interests them; start from felt needs.
2. **Participation**: active involvement (learning by doing, discussion) → acceptance.
3. **Motivation**: awaken desire to learn and act; use incentives.
4. **Comprehension**: communicate at the level and in the language of the audience.
5. **Reinforcement**: repeat messages at intervals.
6. **Learning by doing**: "I hear and forget; I see and remember; I do and I know".
7. **Known to unknown**: begin with what people know.
8. **Setting an example**: educator must practise what he teaches.
9. **Good human relations**: friendly, sympathetic approach.
10. **Feedback**: check understanding; adjust.
11. **Leaders**: use local opinion leaders to spread messages.
12. **Credibility**: message consistent with scientific knowledge, local culture and goals; trusted source.

**"Soil, seed and sower"**

People = soil; health facts = seed; communicator = sower. All three must be favourable for results.

**Important 10 (short answer)**: interest, participation, motivation, comprehension, reinforcement, learning by doing, known to unknown, setting example, good human relations, feedback.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.183
**Methods of health education (Park)**

| Approach | Methods / media | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Individual** | Personal contact, home visits, personal letters, counselling, phone | FWA counselling a couple on contraception |
| **2. Group** | Lecture (chalk talk), demonstration (method and result), group discussion, panel discussion, symposium, workshop, seminar, conference, role play | ORS demonstration to mothers |
| **3. Mass** | Television, radio, newspapers, printed material (leaflets, posters), direct mailing, health museums and exhibitions, folk media, internet and social media, SMS | National campaigns (EPI, dengue) |

**Media (AV aids)**

Auditory (radio, audio); visual (posters, charts, flannel graph, models, slides); combined (TV, film, video, mobile multimedia).

**Why methods and media matter**

Choice depends on objective, audience, message and resources; using several together gives best effect.

**How health can be promoted**

1. Health education (all three approaches).
2. Environmental modification (water, sanitation, housing).
3. Nutritional interventions.
4. Lifestyle and behavioural change.
5. Healthy public policy and laws (Ottawa Charter actions).
6. Community action and participation.
7. Reorienting health services towards prevention.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education; Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.184
**Developing attitude (affective domain)**

| Method | Media / tools |
|---|---|
| Role modelling by teachers | Observation in wards and community |
| Role play, simulated patients | Scenarios on breaking bad news, consent |
| Group discussion, reflective writing | Case discussions on ethics, professionalism |
| Community attachment, field visits | Residential field sites, village surveys, home visits |
| Video demonstrations and films | Patient stories, documentaries |
| Mentoring and counselling | Faculty mentors |
| Feedback on behaviour | Multisource (360°) feedback |
| Ethics and professionalism sessions | Case-based ethics seminars |

**Developing skill (psychomotor domain)**

| Method | Media / tools |
|---|---|
| Demonstration → supervised practice → independent practice ("see one, do one, teach one", with safety) | Bedside teaching |
| Skills laboratory | Mannequins, task trainers (IV cannulation, suturing, CPR) |
| Simulation | High-fidelity simulators, OSCE stations |
| Clinical rotations and internship | Logbooks, direct observation (DOPS, mini-CEX) |
| Communication skills training | Simulated patients, video recording with feedback |
| Field practice | Immunisation, health education sessions, surveys |

**Principles**

Clear objectives; learning by doing; repeated practice; immediate feedback; assessment by OSCE and workplace-based tools.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.185
**How health education promotes public health**

1. **Prevents disease**: hand-washing, safe water, sanitation, safe sex, vector control.
2. **Promotes healthy lifestyles**: diet, exercise, no tobacco or alcohol → fewer NCDs.
3. **Increases use of services**: immunisation, ANC, facility delivery, family planning, screening.
4. **Early care-seeking**: recognising danger signs (pneumonia, pregnancy complications) reduces deaths.
5. **Improves compliance**: TB, hypertension, diabetes treatment.
6. **Self-care**: ORS at home, first aid, self-monitoring.
7. **Community participation**: people take part in identifying and solving health problems.
8. **Supports national programmes**: EPI, family planning, nutrition, dengue, TB.
9. **Reduces stigma and misconceptions** (TB, HIV, mental illness, vaccine rumours).
10. **Advocacy**: builds public support for healthy policies (tobacco control, food safety).
11. **Health literacy** and empowerment → equity.
12. **Cost-effective**: low-cost intervention with wide impact.

**Bangladesh examples**

Home-made ORS teaching, family planning campaigns, EPI mobilisation, arsenic awareness, dengue source reduction, tobacco-control messages.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.186
**Contents (components) of health education (Park)**

| Content | Topics |
|---|---|
| 1. **Human biology** | Structure and function of the body; how disease affects it; effects of alcohol, tobacco, drugs |
| 2. **Nutrition** | Balanced diet, local foods, breastfeeding, complementary feeding, food hygiene, avoiding junk food |
| 3. **Hygiene** | Personal hygiene (bathing, hand-washing, oral care) and environmental hygiene (safe water, latrines, waste disposal) |
| 4. **Family health care** | Family planning, ANC, safe delivery, child care, immunisation, care of elderly |
| 5. **Control of communicable diseases** | Mode of spread and prevention of diarrhoea, TB, dengue, malaria, HIV, COVID-19 |
| 6. **Mental health** | Stress management, recognising mental illness, reducing stigma |
| 7. **Prevention of accidents** | Road safety, drowning prevention, burns, home and occupational safety |
| 8. **Use of health services** | Where and when to seek care, referral, rights |

**Additional modern contents**

NCD risk factors; sexual and reproductive health for adolescents; substance abuse; environmental health (arsenic, air pollution); disaster preparedness; oral health; consumer health (rational drug use).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.187
**Behaviour change communication (BCC)**

An interactive process with individuals and communities to develop **tailored messages and approaches**, using multiple channels, to promote **positive behaviours** and to provide a **supportive environment** that enables people to adopt and maintain them.

**Features**

1. Focus on **specific behaviours**, not just knowledge.
2. Based on **research** of the audience (barriers, motivators).
3. Uses **multiple channels**: interpersonal, community, mass media, digital.
4. **Two-way**, participatory.
5. Addresses **social norms** and enabling environment (services, policy).
6. Monitored and evaluated by behaviour change indicators.

**Relationship between BCC and health education**

| Point | Health education | BCC |
|---|---|---|
| Scope | Broad learning process (KAP) | Strategic, behaviour-focused communication |
| Main aim | Knowledge, attitude, practice | Measurable behaviour change and maintenance |
| Planning | Often general messages | Audience research, segmentation, pretesting |
| Environment | Mainly individual learning | Also social and service environment |
| Relation | **Foundation** | **Evolution / application** of health education |

Both share principles (participation, motivation, feedback); BCC builds on health education and IEC (information, education, communication) and adds strategy and evaluation; it moves people from awareness to sustained practice.

**Example**

Hand-washing with soap: health education explains germs; BCC identifies barriers (no soap near latrine), promotes a hand-washing station, uses TV spots + school activities + mothers' groups, and measures observed hand-washing.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.188
**Limitations of mass media**

1. **One-way communication**: no immediate feedback; misunderstandings not corrected.
2. **Cannot individualise**: same message for all; personal doubts not answered.
3. **Mainly creates awareness**; less effective in changing attitudes and behaviour alone.
4. **Reach is unequal**: poor, rural, illiterate people may lack TV, internet or newspapers; print excludes the illiterate.
5. **Message may be misinterpreted** or ignored; selective attention.
6. **Costly** to produce and air (TV, newspapers).
7. **Short exposure**; messages quickly forgotten without repetition.
8. **Competition** with commercial and entertainment content; message overload.
9. **Misinformation** and rumours spread quickly on social media.
10. **Difficult to evaluate** impact.
11. Cultural and language differences limit relevance in diverse groups.

**Overcoming limitations**

Combine mass media with interpersonal and group methods; pretest messages; repeat; use local languages and folk media; use feedback channels (hotlines).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.189
**"Known to unknown": principle of health education**

Begin with what the audience **already knows** (their experience, beliefs, local terms) and move step by step to **new knowledge**.

Existing knowledge → link → new idea → understanding → acceptance

**Justification**

1. **Learning theory**: new learning is anchored to existing knowledge (meaningful learning); easier to understand.
2. **Comprehension**: familiar examples and words make abstract ideas concrete.
3. **Interest and confidence**: people feel respected when their knowledge is acknowledged.
4. **Less resistance**: building on beliefs (where harmless) avoids rejection.
5. **Better retention**: new facts linked to old memories are remembered longer.
6. **Participation**: starting from people's experience encourages discussion.
7. **Corrects misconceptions** gently by contrasting known ideas with correct ones.

**Examples**

- Explaining **ORS**: start from the known practice of giving saline water / *lobon-gur* → then exact proportions and why it prevents dehydration.
- Explaining **germs**: start from the known fact that rotten food smells and causes illness → invisible germs on dirty hands.
- Explaining **vaccination**: start from known experience that a person who once had measles does not get it again → vaccine gives the same protection safely.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.190
**Classification of environment (Park)**

| Component | Includes | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Physical** | Non-living things and physical forces | Air, water, soil, housing, climate, geography, heat, light, noise, radiation, debris |
| **2. Biological** | Living things around man, including other humans | Plants, animals, insects (vectors), rodents, microbes, other people |
| **3. Psychosocial** | Social and cultural surroundings | Cultural values, customs, beliefs, habits, attitudes, religion, morals, education, lifestyle, community life, health services, social and political organisation |

**Other classification**

- **Internal environment**: inside the body (tissues, organs, homeostasis).
- **External (macro) environment**: physical + biological + psychosocial.
- **Micro (domestic) environment**: home, family, personal way of living.

**Role of biological environment in disease causation**

1. **Source of disease agents**: bacteria, viruses, parasites, fungi in water, food, soil, air.
2. **Animal reservoirs (zoonoses)**: dog (rabies), cattle (anthrax, TB), rats (plague, leptospirosis), birds (avian influenza), bats (Nipah).
3. **Vectors**: Anopheles (malaria), Aedes (dengue, chikungunya), Culex (filariasis, Japanese encephalitis), sandfly (kala-azar), rat flea (plague), housefly (mechanical carrier of diarrhoeal diseases).
4. **Intermediate hosts**: snail (schistosomiasis), cyclops (guinea worm), pig (taeniasis), fish (fish tapeworm).
5. **Other humans**: cases and carriers spread TB, typhoid, HIV, COVID-19.
6. **Plants**: pollen allergens (asthma), poisonous plants, mycotoxins (aflatoxin → liver cancer).
7. **Food chain**: contaminated food; biological magnification.
8. Ecological imbalance (deforestation, urbanisation) brings humans close to vectors and wildlife → emerging diseases.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.191
**Macro-environment (external environment)**

Everything external to the individual human host; classified into three components.

| Component | Examples | Health relevance |
|---|---|---|
| **Physical** | Air, water, soil, housing, climate, geography, heat, light, noise, radiation | Air pollution → COPD, asthma; arsenic water → arsenicosis; heat → heat stroke |
| **Biological** | Plants, animals, insects, rodents, microorganisms, other humans | Mosquitoes → dengue, malaria; dogs → rabies; microbes → infections |
| **Psychosocial** | Customs, culture, beliefs, habits, religion, education, lifestyle, social organisation, health services | Child marriage → maternal deaths; smoking culture → cancer |

**Contrast**

- **Micro-environment (domestic)**: individual's immediate surroundings and way of living at home (housing, kitchen smoke, family habits).
- **Internal environment**: within the body.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.192
**Definition**

**Environment**: all that which is **external to the individual human host** (Park); the sum total of all physical, biological and psychosocial factors and conditions surrounding man that influence his health and life.

**Components**

1. **Physical**: air, water, soil, housing, climate, noise, radiation.
2. **Biological**: plants, animals, microbes, insects, other humans.
3. **Psychosocial**: culture, customs, beliefs, social and political organisation.

**Related concepts**

- **Internal environment**: body's own tissues and systems.
- **Ecology**: study of the relationship between living organisms and their environment.
- **Environmental health**: control of all factors in man's physical environment that may harm his physical development, health or survival (WHO).

**Importance**

Environment is one of the three arms of the epidemiological triad; a large share of disease (diarrhoea, respiratory infections, vector-borne diseases, cancers) is environment-related and preventable.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.193
**Definition**

**Offensive trades**: trades or industries that are **injurious to health** or create a **nuisance** to the neighbourhood by offensive smell, smoke, dust, noise, effluents or flies; they need licensing and control under public health law.

**Examples**

Slaughter houses; blood boiling and blood drying; bone boiling and bone crushing; fat melting; tripe cleaning; gut scraping; glue and size making; soap boiling; tanning and leather dressing; fellmongering (hide and skin storing); fish drying and fish markets; manure, rag and bone dealing; lime burning; brick kilns; cattle sheds and dairies; poultry farms.

**Classification**

| By nuisance | Examples (Bangladesh) |
|---|---|
| Offensive smell | Tanneries, slaughter houses, bone mills, fish drying (*shutki*) |
| Smoke and fumes | Brick kilns, lime burning |
| Dust | Stone crushing, rice mills, cement works |
| Noise | Saw mills, metal workshops |
| Water pollution (effluents) | Tanneries, textile dyeing, slaughter houses |
| Flies and rodents | Manure stores, poultry and dairy farms, rag and bone shops |

**Control**

Licensing and inspection; location away from residential areas (zoning); effluent treatment; proper waste disposal; ventilation and chimneys; legislation (Environment Conservation Act 1995; brick kiln control law).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.194
**Safe and wholesome water**

Water that is:
1. **Free from pathogenic agents** (bacteria, viruses, protozoa, helminths).
2. **Free from harmful chemical substances** (arsenic, lead, excess fluoride, nitrate).
3. **Pleasant to taste**: free from colour, odour, turbidity.
4. **Usable for domestic purposes** (drinking, cooking, washing).

**Criteria (quality standards)**

| Aspect | Criteria |
|---|---|
| **Physical** | Clear (low turbidity; <1 NTU desirable for disinfection), colourless, odourless, pleasant taste, cool |
| **Chemical** | Toxic chemicals within guideline values (arsenic ≤0.01 mg/L, lead ≤0.01 mg/L, fluoride ≤1.5 mg/L, nitrate ≤50 mg/L; WHO); pH 6.5–8.5; acceptable hardness and iron |
| **Bacteriological** | **E. coli / thermotolerant coliforms not detectable in any 100 ml sample** (WHO) |
| **Virological / parasitological** | Free from viruses, protozoal cysts, helminth ova |
| **Radiological** | Below WHO screening levels |
| **Disinfection** | Free residual chlorine ≥0.5 mg/L after 30–60 minutes' contact; ≥0.2 mg/L at point of delivery |

**Note**

**Safe** = no health hazard; **wholesome** = also palatable and acceptable; "potable" water.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health); WHO, Guidelines for Drinking-water Quality.

## DU.195
**Impounding reservoir**

An **artificial lake** formed by building a **dam across a river or valley**, to store water for public supply.

**Features**

1. Usually fed by streams from upland catchment areas.
2. Water is **soft**, pleasant to taste and relatively free from pathogens because of storage.
3. Quality depends on protection of the **catchment area**.

**Self-purification by storage**

| Type | Change | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Physical | Sedimentation | Most suspended matter settles within 24 hours; water clears |
| Chemical | Oxidation of organic matter by aerobic bacteria | Free ammonia falls; nitrates rise |
| Biological | Pathogens die (unfavourable conditions) | Large fall in bacteria within the first week |

Optimum storage period: about **10–14 days**; longer storage favours algae growth (bad taste and odour).

**Drawbacks**

Algae and vegetation growth; tastes and odours; contamination from catchment (grazing, human habitation); stratification and low oxygen in deep water.

**Protective measures**

1. Catchment area free of human habitation, cultivation and grazing.
2. Fence and patrol the area; no bathing or washing.
3. Control algae (copper sulphate).
4. Further treatment: filtration and **chlorination** before supply.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.196
**Sources of water (Park)**

| Source | Types | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Rain** | Rainwater (directly collected) | **Prime (ultimate) source** of all water; purest natural water; soft; may pick up dust and gases; storage needed |
| **2. Surface water** | (a) Impounding reservoirs (b) Rivers and streams (c) Tanks, ponds and lakes | Easily contaminated by sewage, industrial and agricultural waste; needs treatment |
| **3. Ground water** | (a) Shallow wells (b) Deep wells (c) Springs | Filtered through soil: usually free of pathogens; may contain dissolved minerals (hardness, iron, arsenic, fluoride) |

**Wells**

- **Shallow well**: taps water above the first impervious layer; easily polluted.
- **Deep well / deep tube well**: taps water below the first impervious layer; safer.
- **Sanitary well**: properly located (at least 15 m from sources of contamination), lined, covered, with parapet, platform and drain, and a hand pump.

**Bangladesh**

Groundwater (shallow and deep tube wells) is the main drinking source in rural areas; problems: arsenic, iron, salinity (coast), falling water table. Rainwater harvesting and pond sand filters are used in coastal and arsenic areas.

**Prime source**: **rain**.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.197
**Classification of water-borne diseases (Park)**

**A. Biological**

| Group | Diseases |
|---|---|
| (i) Caused by presence of infective agent in water | |
| Viral | Hepatitis A, hepatitis E, poliomyelitis, rotavirus diarrhoea |
| Bacterial | Cholera, typhoid and paratyphoid, bacillary dysentery (shigellosis), *E. coli* diarrhoea |
| Protozoal | Amoebiasis, giardiasis, cryptosporidiosis |
| Helminthic | Roundworm (ascariasis), threadworm, hydatid disease |
| Leptospiral | Weil's disease (leptospirosis) |
| (ii) Due to presence of an aquatic host | Snail → schistosomiasis; cyclops → guinea worm (dracunculiasis), fish tapeworm |

**B. Chemical (water-related)**

Arsenicosis (arsenic), fluorosis (fluoride), methaemoglobinaemia (nitrate), lead poisoning.

**Other water-related groups (Bradley)**

Water-washed (scabies, trachoma); water-related insect vectors (malaria, dengue, filariasis).

**Five major water-borne diseases of Bangladesh**

1. Cholera / acute watery diarrhoea
2. Typhoid (enteric) fever
3. Hepatitis E and A
4. Bacillary dysentery (shigellosis)
5. Amoebiasis / giardiasis
(+ arsenicosis from groundwater)

**Among slum dwellers**: diarrhoea, cholera, typhoid, hepatitis E, worm infestation, skin infections (water-washed), dengue.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.198
**Prevention of water-borne diseases**

**A. Safe water supply**

1. Protected sources: sanitary tube wells, deep tube wells, piped supply.
2. **Purification**: large scale (storage → filtration → chlorination); household (boiling, chlorine tablets, filters).
3. Maintain **residual chlorine** in distribution; repair leaking pipes; avoid cross-connection with sewers.
4. Safe storage at home: covered containers, use of a ladle.

**B. Sanitation**

1. Sanitary latrines; end open defecation.
2. Safe disposal of sewage, sullage and refuse.
3. Protect water sources from contamination (distance from latrines ≥15 m).

**C. Personal and food hygiene**

Hand-washing with soap (after toilet, before eating and feeding); safe food handling.

**D. Water quality surveillance**

Regular bacteriological (E. coli) and chemical (arsenic) testing; sanitary inspection.

**E. Specific protection**

Vaccines: typhoid conjugate vaccine, oral cholera vaccine (outbreaks, camps), hepatitis A.

**F. Health education**

Safe water use, hygiene, ORS, early care-seeking.

**G. Case management and outbreak control**

Early diagnosis and treatment (ORS, zinc, antibiotics where indicated); notification; outbreak investigation; chlorination of sources during floods.

**H. Control of aquatic hosts and chemical hazards**

Snail and cyclops control; arsenic-safe water options.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.199
**Sources of water pollution (Park)**

| Source | Examples |
|---|---|
| **1. Sewage** | Domestic waste water: faeces, urine, sullage; decomposable organic matter and pathogens |
| **2. Industrial and trade wastes** | Toxic chemicals, heavy metals (chromium, lead, mercury), acids, alkalis, dyes, oils |
| **3. Agricultural pollutants** | Fertilisers (nitrates, phosphates → eutrophication), pesticides, animal waste |
| **4. Physical pollutants** | Heat (thermal pollution), radioactive substances |
| **5. Others** | Solid waste dumping, hospital waste, oil spills, plastics, natural arsenic and fluoride |

**Sources of pollution of the river Buriganga**

1. **Untreated domestic sewage** of Dhaka city through drains and canals.
2. **Tannery effluents** (historically from Hazaribagh: chromium, sulphides, organic matter).
3. **Industrial effluents**: textile dyeing, washing, chemical and pharmaceutical units.
4. **Solid waste and plastics** dumped along banks.
5. **Hospital and clinical waste**.
6. **Waste, oil and fuel from launches and boats** at Sadarghat.
7. **Kitchen-market waste** and slaughter waste.
8. **Encroachment** and silting, which reduce flow and self-purification.

**Effects**

Low dissolved oxygen, foul smell, fish death, health hazards for riverside people (diarrhoea, skin disease), unsuitable for drinking water treatment.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.200
**Coliform organisms**

Aerobic and facultatively anaerobic, Gram-negative, non-sporing, rod-shaped bacilli that grow in the presence of bile salts and ferment lactose at 35–37 °C with acid and gas production within 48 hours.

**Indicators of faecal pollution**

| Indicator | Significance |
|---|---|
| **Total coliforms** | Include faecal and non-faecal (soil) types; indicate treatment efficiency and integrity of distribution |
| **Faecal (thermotolerant) coliforms**: ***Escherichia coli*** | Grow at 44 °C; definite faecal origin; **best indicator** |
| Faecal streptococci (enterococci) | Supportive evidence of faecal pollution |
| *Clostridium perfringens* (sulphite-reducing clostridia) | Spores survive long; indicate remote or intermittent pollution |

**Two important indicators of bacteriological quality**

E. coli (thermotolerant coliforms) and total coliforms.

**Why E. coli is an important tool**

1. **Always present in large numbers** in human and animal faeces.
2. **Exclusively faecal origin**; not found in unpolluted water or soil.
3. Presence = **recent faecal contamination** → possible presence of pathogens.
4. **Easily detected and counted** by simple, cheap methods (multiple tube / MPN, membrane filtration).
5. **Survives in water at least as long as most bacterial pathogens**; more resistant to chlorination than some pathogens.
6. **Absence** indicates water is bacteriologically safe (for bacterial pathogens).
7. WHO standard: **E. coli not detectable in any 100 ml sample** of drinking water.

**Limitation**

Not a perfect indicator for viruses and protozoal cysts, which resist chlorine more.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health); WHO, Guidelines for Drinking-water Quality.

## DU.201
**Purification of water on a large scale**

Storage → Filtration → Disinfection

**1. Storage**

Water stored in reservoirs: physical (sedimentation), chemical (oxidation) and biological (pathogens die) self-purification; optimum 10–14 days.

**2. Filtration**

| Type | Basis | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| **Slow sand (biological) filter** | Vital (biological) layer | Sand bed ~1 m; rate 0.1–0.4 m³/m²/hour; removes 99.9% of bacteria; large area |
| **Rapid sand (mechanical) filter** | Coagulation and mechanical straining | Preceded by coagulation (alum), rapid mixing, flocculation and sedimentation; rate 5–15 m³/m²/hour; cleaned by backwashing |

**3. Disinfection**

- **Chlorination** (most common): chlorine gas, chloramine, perchloron / high-test hypochlorite.
- Others: ozonation, UV irradiation.
- Aim: free residual chlorine **0.5 mg/L after 1 hour contact**.

**Additional treatment as needed**

Aeration (removes iron, gases, odours); softening; fluoridation or defluoridation; arsenic and iron removal.

**Typical modern plant flow**

Intake → Screening → Aeration → Coagulation and flocculation → Sedimentation → Rapid sand filtration → Chlorination → Storage → Distribution

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.202
**Purification of water at household (small-scale) level**

**1. Boiling**

- Rolling boil for 5–10 minutes.
- Kills bacteria, viruses, spores, cysts and ova; removes temporary hardness and dissolved gases.
- Drawbacks: fuel cost; no residual protection; can be recontaminated; flat taste.

**2. Chemical disinfection**

| Agent | Features |
|---|---|
| **Bleaching powder** (chlorinated lime, ~33% available chlorine) | Cheap; used as 1% stock solution or for wells |
| **Chlorine solution** | Prepared from bleaching powder |
| **High-test hypochlorite (HTH / perchloron)** | 60–70% available chlorine; stable |
| **Chlorine tablets** (sodium dichloroisocyanurate, halazone) | Convenient for emergencies and floods; 30 minutes' contact |
| **Iodine** | 2 drops of 2% tincture per litre; short-term use |
| Potassium permanganate | Not recommended (weak against coliforms) |

**3. Filtration**

Ceramic filters (Pasteur–Chamberland, Berkefeld, Katadyn); candle filters; cloth filtration (reduces cholera by removing plankton); bio-sand filters.

**4. Solar disinfection (SODIS)**

Clear plastic bottles exposed to sunlight for 6 hours.

**5. Combined methods**

Flocculant + chlorine sachets (for turbid water); alum settling followed by chlorination.

**6. Special (Bangladesh)**

Household arsenic removal filters (e.g., SONO), pond sand filters.

**Safe storage**

Narrow-mouthed, covered container; pour or use a long-handled ladle.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.203
**Slow sand (biological) filter: elements**

| Element | Description |
|---|---|
| **1. Supernatant (raw) water** | Depth 1–1.5 m above the sand; provides constant head to drive water through; waiting period 3–12 hours allows sedimentation, oxidation and agglutination of particles |
| **2. Bed of graded sand** | About 1 m thick; effective size 0.2–0.3 mm; supported by about 0.3 m of graded gravel; the **vital layer** forms on its surface |
| **3. Under-drainage system** | Porous or perforated pipes at the bottom; collects filtered water and supports the bed |
| **4. System of filter control valves** | Regulate and maintain a constant rate of filtration (0.1–0.4 m³/m²/hour); venturi meter measures resistance (loss of head) |

**Diagram (vertical section)**

```
        Raw water inlet →
  ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Supernatant water (1–1.5 m)    │
  │.................................│ ← Vital layer (schmutzdecke)
  │  Sand bed (~1 m; 0.2–0.3 mm)    │
  │  Graded gravel (~0.3 m)         │
  │═══ Under-drainage pipes ════════│ → Filter control valve → Filtered water
  └─────────────────────────────────┘
```

**Mechanism**

Mechanical straining, sedimentation, adsorption, oxidation and **biological action** of the vital layer.

**Cleaning**

When resistance becomes too high (loss of head about 1.3 m), the top 1–2 cm of sand (with vital layer) is scraped off; after repeated scrapings the bed is re-sanded.

**Advantages**

Simple to build and operate; cheap; excellent quality: bacterial removal 99.9–99.99%, E. coli 99–99.9%.

**Disadvantages**

Needs large area; slow; cannot handle very turbid water; affected by algae; not suitable for big cities.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.204
**Vital layer (biological layer)**

Also called **schmutzdecke**, zoogleal layer, dirty skin or biological layer: a slimy, gelatinous film that forms on the **surface of the sand bed** of a slow sand filter within a few days of starting.

**Formation**

New filter → raw water passes → organisms and particles collect on the sand surface → layer grows in several days → filter is "**ripened**". Until then, filtered water is run to waste.

**Constituents**

1. Thread-like **algae**
2. **Plankton**
3. **Diatoms**
4. **Bacteria**
5. Other micro-organisms and trapped organic matter

**Role (functions)**

1. The **"heart" of the slow sand filter**.
2. **Removes organic matter**.
3. **Holds back bacteria** and destroys them.
4. **Oxidises ammoniacal nitrogen into nitrates**.
5. Helps yield **bacteria-free water** (99.9% removal).
6. Acts as a fine strainer for suspended particles.

**Practical point**

As the layer thickens, resistance rises; it is scraped off during cleaning and re-forms naturally.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.205
**Rapid sand (mechanical) filtration: steps**

| Step | Process |
|---|---|
| 1. **Coagulation** | Alum (5–40 mg/L, by turbidity) added to raw water |
| 2. **Rapid mixing** | Violent agitation for a few minutes to disperse alum |
| 3. **Flocculation** | Slow, gentle stirring (~30 minutes) → thick, white flocs of aluminium hydroxide form |
| 4. **Sedimentation** | Water held in settling tanks for 2–6 hours; flocs carry down impurities and bacteria |
| 5. **Filtration** | Partly clarified water passes through rapid sand filters: sand bed ~1 m, effective size 0.4–0.7 mm, rate 5–15 m³/m²/hour; remaining floc forms a layer that removes bacteria |
| 6. **Backwashing** | When resistance (loss of head) becomes high, flow is reversed with water (and compressed air) to clean the sand; takes about 15 minutes |
| 7. **Chlorination** | Final disinfection before distribution |

Raw water → Alum → Rapid mix → Flocculation → Sedimentation → Rapid sand filter → Chlorination → Supply

**Advantages**

Less space; fast; deals with raw, turbid water; easy and quick cleaning; plant can be compact.

**Disadvantages**

Costly to build and run; needs skilled staff and chemicals; bacterial removal (98–99%) lower than slow sand filter, so chlorination is essential.

**Slow vs rapid sand filter**

| Point | Slow sand | Rapid sand |
|---|---|---|
| Space | Large | Small |
| Rate | 0.1–0.4 m³/m²/hour | 5–15 m³/m²/hour |
| Sand size | 0.2–0.3 mm | 0.4–0.7 mm |
| Pre-treatment | Plain sedimentation | Coagulation and flocculation |
| Main action | Biological (vital layer) | Mechanical |
| Cleaning | Scraping | Backwashing |
| Bacterial removal | 99.9–99.99% | 98–99% |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.206
**Action of chlorine**

Cl₂ + H₂O → HOCl (hypochlorous acid) + HCl; HOCl ⇌ H⁺ + OCl⁻ (hypochlorite ion). **HOCl** is the main germicidal agent (stronger at low pH); chlorine also oxidises iron, manganese, hydrogen sulphide and organic matter, and controls algae.

**Principles of chlorination (Park)**

1. Water must be **clear**, free from turbidity (turbidity shields organisms).
2. **Chlorine demand** must be estimated: the amount of chlorine needed to destroy bacteria and oxidise organic matter and ammonia; the point where free residual chlorine begins to appear is the **break point**.
3. **Contact period**: free residual chlorine must be present for **at least 1 hour** to kill bacteria and viruses.
4. **Minimum free residual chlorine: 0.5 mg/L** for 1 hour.
5. **Dose = chlorine demand + free residual chlorine**.

**Methods of chlorination (large scale)**

| Method | Features |
|---|---|
| **Chlorine gas** | First choice: cheap, quick, efficient, easy to apply; needs special equipment (chlorinator) |
| **Chloramine** | Chlorine + ammonia; slow action; less taste; longer-lasting residual in distribution |
| **Perchloron (high-test hypochlorite)** | Calcium hypochlorite, 60–70% available chlorine; used where gas is not available |

**Other**

- **Super-chlorination** followed by dechlorination for heavily polluted water.
- Testing residual chlorine: **orthotoluidine (OT) test**; OTA test separates free and combined chlorine.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.207
**Break-point chlorination**

Adding chlorine to water **beyond the break point**, so that a **free residual chlorine** is present after the chlorine demand is fully met.

**What happens as chlorine is added (chlorine demand curve)**

| Stage | Event |
|---|---|
| 1 | Chlorine first used up by reducing substances (iron, H₂S, organic matter) → no residual |
| 2 | Chlorine combines with ammonia → **combined chlorine (chloramines)**; residual rises |
| 3 | More chlorine **oxidises chloramines** → residual falls |
| 4 | **Break point**: lowest point; all ammonia and organic matter oxidised |
| 5 | Beyond break point, **free residual chlorine** rises in direct proportion to chlorine added |

Chlorine added → demand met → combined residual ↑ → destroyed ↓ → **break point** → free residual ↑

**Significance**

1. Free chlorine (HOCl) is far more bactericidal than combined chlorine.
2. Removes tastes and odours caused by ammonia compounds.
3. Ensures reliable disinfection with free residual ≥0.5 mg/L after 1 hour.
4. **Dose = chlorine demand + free residual chlorine**.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.208
**Steps in disinfection of a well (with bleaching powder)**

**1. Find the volume of water in the well**

Measure diameter (d, metres) and depth of water column (h, metres).
Volume (litres) = (3.14 × d² × h ÷ 4) × 1000

**2. Find the amount of bleaching powder required**

Estimate chlorine demand with **Horrock's apparatus**; as a rule of thumb about **2.5 g of good bleaching powder per 1,000 litres** gives 1 mg/L chlorine with 0.5 mg/L free residual after 1 hour.

**3. Dissolve the bleaching powder**

Make a thin paste in a bucket with a little water; add more water to fill, stir, let settle for ~10 minutes; use the clear supernatant (chlorine solution); discard sediment (lime).

**4. Deliver the chlorine solution into the well**

Lower the bucket deep into the well water; jerk it up and down several times to mix thoroughly.

**5. Contact period**

Allow **1 hour** before water is drawn.

**6. Test for residual chlorine (OT test)**

Free residual chlorine should be **≥0.5 mg/L** after 1 hour; if less, repeat the process.

**Example**

Well diameter 2 m, water depth 5 m:
Volume = 3.14 × 2² × 5 ÷ 4 × 1000 = **15,700 litres**
Bleaching powder = 15.7 × 2.5 g ≈ **39 g**

**Note**

Disinfect wells **every week** during epidemics (cholera, floods), preferably at night so contact time is long.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.209
**Chemicals in excess that make public water supply unacceptable (grounds for rejection)**

**A. Toxic substances (health hazard)**

| Chemical | WHO guideline value (mg/L) | Health effect |
|---|---|---|
| **Arsenic** | 0.01 | Arsenicosis, skin, lung, bladder cancers |
| **Lead** | 0.01 | Neurotoxicity in children, anaemia |
| **Cadmium** | 0.003 | Kidney damage |
| **Mercury** (inorganic) | 0.006 | Kidney and nervous system damage |
| **Chromium** | 0.05 | Carcinogen (Cr VI) |
| **Selenium** | 0.04 | Hair and nail loss, neuropathy |
| **Cyanide** | — | Acute poisoning |
| **Barium**, **antimony**, **nickel**, **uranium** | Low guideline values | Various organ toxicity |

**B. Chemicals affecting health in excess**

| Chemical | WHO guideline value (mg/L) | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| **Fluoride** | 1.5 | Dental and skeletal fluorosis |
| **Nitrate** (as NO₃⁻) | 50 | Methaemoglobinaemia (blue baby) |
| **Nitrite** | 3 | Methaemoglobinaemia |
| Boron, manganese | Guideline values set | Toxicity |
| Pesticides, organic chemicals (benzene, vinyl chloride) | Very low values | Cancers, organ toxicity |

**C. Substances affecting acceptability (aesthetic)**

Iron, manganese, high hardness, chloride, sulphate, total dissolved solids, zinc, copper, hydrogen sulphide; colour, taste, odour, turbidity.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health); WHO, Guidelines for Drinking-water Quality (4th ed.).

## DU.210
**Microbiological parameters of drinking water (WHO Guidelines for Drinking-water Quality)**

| Water | Parameter | Guideline |
|---|---|---|
| **All water directly intended for drinking** | ***E. coli*** or thermotolerant coliform bacteria | **Not detectable in any 100 ml sample** |
| **Treated water entering the distribution system** | *E. coli* or thermotolerant coliforms | Not detectable in any 100 ml sample |
| **Treated water in the distribution system** | *E. coli* or thermotolerant coliforms | Not detectable in any 100 ml sample |

**Supporting criteria**

1. **Total coliforms**: should not be detectable in treated water entering distribution; their presence indicates inadequate treatment or post-treatment contamination.
2. **No pathogenic organisms** (bacteria, viruses, protozoa, helminths).
3. **Turbidity** below 1 NTU (ideally lower) for effective disinfection.
4. **Free residual chlorine**: ≥0.5 mg/L after at least 30 minutes' contact (pH <8); ≥0.2 mg/L at the point of delivery.
5. Regular **sanitary inspection** and monitoring (Water Safety Plans).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health); WHO, Guidelines for Drinking-water Quality (4th ed.).

## DU.211
**Hardness of water**

The **soap-destroying power** of water; caused mainly by dissolved **calcium and magnesium** salts (bicarbonates, sulphates, chlorides, nitrates). Hard water needs more soap to lather.

**Types**

| Type | Caused by | Removed by boiling? |
|---|---|---|
| **Temporary (carbonate) hardness** | Bicarbonates of Ca and Mg | Yes |
| **Permanent (non-carbonate) hardness** | Sulphates, chlorides, nitrates of Ca and Mg | No |

**Classification (Park)**

| Classification | Level of hardness (mEq/L) |
|---|---|
| Soft water | <1 (<50 mg/L as CaCO₃) |
| Moderately hard | 1–3 (50–150 mg/L) |
| Hard water | 3–6 (150–300 mg/L) |
| Very hard | >6 (>300 mg/L) |

(1 mEq/L = 50 mg/L CaCO₃.) Desirable level: 1–3 mEq/L.

**Disadvantages of hard water**

1. More **soap** consumption.
2. **Scale** in boilers, kettles, pipes → fuel waste, blockage, boiler explosion risk.
3. Food (pulses, meat) cooks poorly.
4. Fabrics wear out faster.
5. Unpleasant taste when very hard.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.212
**Removal of hardness**

**A. Temporary hardness**

| Method | Reaction / description |
|---|---|
| **1. Boiling** | Ca(HCO₃)₂ → CaCO₃↓ + H₂O + CO₂↑ (calcium carbonate precipitates) |
| **2. Addition of lime (Clark's method)** | Ca(HCO₃)₂ + Ca(OH)₂ → 2CaCO₃↓ + 2H₂O; lime removes CO₂ and precipitates carbonate; used on a large scale |

**B. Permanent hardness**

| Method | Reaction / description |
|---|---|
| **1. Addition of sodium carbonate (washing soda)** | CaSO₄ + Na₂CO₃ → CaCO₃↓ + Na₂SO₄ (also removes temporary hardness) |
| **2. Base exchange process (permutit / zeolite)** | Water passed through sodium permutit: Na₂-Z + Ca²⁺ / Mg²⁺ → Ca-Z / Mg-Z + 2Na⁺; removes both types (can give zero hardness); permutit regenerated with strong brine (NaCl) |

**C. Modern methods**

Ion-exchange resins (demineralisation); reverse osmosis; nanofiltration.

**Advantages of softening**

Saves soap and fuel; prevents scaling in pipes and boilers; better cooking and washing.

**Note**

Base exchange adds sodium: not ideal for people on salt-restricted diets.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.213
**Arsenicosis**

A chronic health condition caused by prolonged ingestion (usually ≥6 months) of arsenic above the safe level, usually shown by **characteristic skin lesions**, with or without involvement of internal organs and malignancies (WHO).

**Source in Bangladesh**

Naturally occurring arsenic in **groundwater** drawn by shallow tube wells; first detected in Bangladesh in 1993 (Chapai Nawabganj). WHO guideline value: **0.01 mg/L (10 µg/L)**.

**Characteristic skin manifestations**

| Lesion | Description |
|---|---|
| **Melanosis** | Diffuse or spotted ("raindrop") dark pigmentation, mainly on trunk and limbs |
| **Leucomelanosis** | Mixed white (depigmented) and dark spots |
| **Keratosis** | Thickening and nodules of **palms and soles** (hyperkeratosis) |
| Later | Bowen's disease, squamous and basal cell carcinoma of skin |

**Stages**

Pre-clinical (no symptoms; arsenic in urine, hair, nails) → Clinical (skin lesions) → Complications (internal organ disease, cancers)

**Three important health effects (beyond skin)**

1. **Cancers**: skin, lung, urinary bladder, kidney, liver.
2. **Cardiovascular**: hypertension, peripheral vascular disease (blackfoot disease, gangrene).
3. **Others**: chronic lung disease, peripheral neuropathy, diabetes, liver enlargement, adverse pregnancy outcomes, impaired child development.

**Diagnosis**

History of tube-well water use; skin lesions; arsenic in hair, nails, urine; water testing.

**Management**

Stop exposure (arsenic-safe water: most important); good nutrition; keratolytic ointment (salicylic acid) for keratosis; treat complications and cancers; screen family members.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health; Occupational Health).

## DU.214
**Provision of safe water in an arsenic-affected community**

**A. Identify safe and unsafe sources**

1. **Test every tube well** (field kits / laboratory).
2. **Mark** tube wells: **red** = unsafe (above standard), **green** = safe.
3. Encourage **well-switching** to nearby green (safe) wells.

**B. Alternative safe water options**

| Option | Description |
|---|---|
| **Deep tube wells** | Tap deeper, usually arsenic-free aquifers (below the first impervious layer) |
| **Rainwater harvesting** | Collect roof rainwater in clean tanks; arsenic-free |
| **Pond sand filter (PSF)** | Pond water filtered through a sand filter for community use |
| **Dug (ring) wells** | Shallow, well-aerated; usually low arsenic; need protection and disinfection |
| **Piped water supply** | Treated surface or deep groundwater supplied through pipes |
| **Arsenic removal technologies** | Household filters (e.g., SONO, three-pitcher); community arsenic-iron removal plants |
| **Treated surface water** | River or pond water with filtration and chlorination |

**C. Supporting measures**

1. Health education on arsenic risk and use of safe sources.
2. Community participation in maintaining shared safe sources.
3. Screening and management of arsenicosis patients.
4. Nutrition support; no stigma for patients.
5. Regular water-quality monitoring; ban on installing new shallow wells in contaminated areas.

**Two community-level preventive measures (short answer)**

1. Install **deep tube wells** / piped supply.
2. **Test and mark** all tube wells and switch to safe (green) wells.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.215
**Daily water requirement**

| Use | Requirement (per person per day) |
|---|---|
| **Physiological (drinking)** | About 2–3 litres (more in hot climate and heavy work) |
| **Basic access (WHO)** | At least **20 litres** for drinking, cooking and basic hygiene |
| **All domestic needs (UN / WHO)** | **50–100 litres** |
| **Urban: all purposes (Park)** | **150–200 litres** (drinking, cooking, bathing, washing, flushing, plus public, trade and industrial uses) |
| **Rural (Park; Indian norm)** | About **40 litres** for domestic needs |

**Breakdown of domestic use (example)**

Drinking and cooking; bathing; washing clothes and utensils; flushing latrines; house cleaning; animals (rural).

**Factors affecting requirement**

Climate; standard of living; habits; type of sanitation (flush vs pit); industry and trade; cost and availability; leakage and waste.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health); WHO / UN, right to water.

## DU.216
**Situation**

Tube wells submerged → groundwater sources contaminated by flood water, faeces and debris → high risk of cholera, diarrhoea, typhoid, hepatitis E, leptospirosis, skin infections.

**A. Immediate assessment**

1. Rapid survey of population, shelters, water sources, latrines; identify vulnerable groups.
2. Coordinate with Upazila administration, DPHE, NGOs, WASH partners; set up a control room.
3. Start **disease surveillance** (daily diarrhoea reporting) and ORS corners.

**B. Emergency safe water supply**

1. **Distribute household water treatment**: water purification tablets (chlorine/NaDCC), bleaching powder solution, or flocculant–disinfectant sachets with clear instructions; 30 minutes' contact.
2. **Boil water** where fuel is available (rolling boil).
3. **Supply bottled or tanker water** to shelters; mobile water treatment plants.
4. **Rainwater harvesting** with clean containers.
5. Provide **clean, covered containers** (jerry cans) and ladles; at least 15 litres/person/day in emergency (Sphere), minimum 2–3 litres for drinking.
6. Use **raised or high-platform tube wells** not submerged; clean their surroundings.

**C. After water recedes: restore sources**

1. **Disinfect tube wells**: pump out water until clear; pour chlorine solution (bleaching powder) into the well pipe; contact for several hours (overnight); pump out until chlorine smell disappears.
2. **Disinfect dug wells**: Horrock's test; ~2.5 g bleaching powder/1,000 L; 1 hour contact; OT test for residual ≥0.5 mg/L.
3. Repair platforms and drains; test water (E. coli, arsenic) before reuse.

**D. Sanitation and hygiene**

1. Emergency latrines away (≥15 m) and downhill from water sources; avoid open defecation.
2. Hygiene kits: soap, sanitary pads; hand-washing points.
3. Safe disposal of refuse and dead animals; chlorinate flooded latrines.

**E. Health measures**

Health education (boil/chlorinate, hand-washing, safe food); ORS and zinc stocks; early case management and referral; **oral cholera vaccine** if outbreak risk; outbreak response team.

**F. Monitoring**

Daily residual chlorine checks at distribution points (≥0.2–0.5 mg/L); disease trends; feedback to community.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health; Disaster Management).

## DU.217
**a) Five diseases transmitted through contaminated water (2)**

1. Cholera / acute watery diarrhoea
2. Typhoid (enteric) fever
3. Hepatitis E and A
4. Bacillary dysentery (shigellosis)
5. Amoebiasis / giardiasis
(Also: poliomyelitis, rotavirus diarrhoea, leptospirosis, worm infestations.)

**b) Purification of water at community level (2.5)**

Storage → Filtration → Disinfection

1. **Storage**: sedimentation, oxidation, natural die-off of pathogens.
2. **Filtration**:
   - Slow sand filter (biological; vital layer; pond sand filter in villages).
   - Rapid sand filter (coagulation with alum → flocculation → sedimentation → filtration → backwashing).
3. **Disinfection: chlorination**: principles: clear water, estimate chlorine demand, free residual chlorine 0.5 mg/L after 1 hour contact; methods: chlorine gas, chloramine, perchloron.
4. **Disinfection of wells** with bleaching powder (Horrock's test; OT test).
5. **Alternative safe sources**: sanitary tube wells, deep tube wells, piped supply; protect pond (fencing, no bathing or washing).

**c) Purification at household level (2.5)**

1. **Boiling**: rolling boil 5–10 minutes; kills all pathogens.
2. **Chemical disinfection**: bleaching powder solution, chlorine tablets (NaDCC), high-test hypochlorite; 30 minutes' contact.
3. **Filtration**: ceramic or candle filters; cloth filtration (folded sari cloth reduces cholera).
4. **Solar disinfection (SODIS)**: clear bottles in sunlight for 6 hours.
5. **Combined**: alum settling + chlorination; flocculant–disinfectant sachets.
6. **Safe storage**: covered, narrow-mouthed container; ladle for drawing water.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.218
**(a) Routes of contamination of drinking water (2)**

1. **Flood water entering submerged tube wells** through the pump head, broken platforms and loose joints.
2. **Overflowing latrines** near water sources → faecal matter washed into wells and ponds.
3. **Seepage** of faecally contaminated surface water into shallow aquifers.
4. **Contaminated storage and handling**: dirty containers, hands, utensils.
5. Use of **flood or pond water** for drinking, washing and cooking.
6. Animal carcasses, solid waste and sewage in flood water.

**(b) Environmental factors responsible (2)**

1. **Flooding**: spreads faecal contamination; damages water and sanitation infrastructure.
2. **Poor siting of latrines** (<15 m from water sources; upslope).
3. **Unsafe water sources**: submerged shallow tube wells, ponds.
4. **Inadequate sanitation**: open defecation, unsanitary latrines.
5. **Crowding** in shelters; poor hygiene; lack of soap.
6. **Warm, humid climate** favouring bacterial growth; flies.
7. Poor **food hygiene**; lack of fuel for boiling.

**(c) Community-level plan to prevent further transmission (3)**

| Area | Actions |
|---|---|
| Surveillance and case management | Daily case reporting; ORS corners; zinc; refer severe cases; stool samples to IEDCR; line list |
| Safe water | Distribute chlorine tablets/bleaching powder; boil water; tanker or bottled water; disinfect tube wells after flood; test water |
| Sanitation | Emergency latrines ≥15 m and downslope from water sources; desludge or seal flooded latrines; safe disposal of child faeces |
| Hygiene | Hand-washing stations with soap; hygiene kits; food hygiene |
| Health education | Mass and interpersonal messages through CHWs, imams, teachers, miking |
| Specific protection | Oral cholera vaccine campaign if cholera confirmed |
| Coordination | Upazila health and disaster committee, DPHE, NGOs; community volunteers |
| Long-term | Raised-platform tube wells; proper latrine siting; flood-resilient WASH |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health; Epidemiology of Communicable Diseases).

## DU.219
**Definition**

**Air pollution**: the presence in the outdoor (or indoor) air of one or more contaminants (gases, particles, biological agents) in such quantity and for such duration as to be harmful to human, animal or plant life or property, or to interfere unreasonably with the comfortable enjoyment of life or property (WHO concept).

**Types**

| Basis | Types |
|---|---|
| Place | Outdoor (ambient) / Indoor (household) |
| Origin | Primary pollutants (emitted directly: SO₂, CO, NOx, PM) / Secondary pollutants (formed in air: ozone, photochemical smog, acid rain) |
| Source | Natural (dust storms, volcanoes, pollen) / Man-made (vehicles, industry, brick kilns, biomass burning) |

**Major pollutants**

Particulate matter (PM₂.₅, PM₁₀), carbon monoxide, sulphur dioxide, nitrogen oxides, ozone, lead, hydrocarbons.

**WHO air quality guidelines (2021)**

PM₂.₅: annual 5 µg/m³, 24-hour 15 µg/m³; PM₁₀: annual 15 µg/m³, 24-hour 45 µg/m³.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health); WHO Global Air Quality Guidelines, 2021.

## DU.220
**How air becomes polluted: sources (causes)**

| Source | Pollutants |
|---|---|
| **1. Automobiles** | CO, NOx, hydrocarbons, PM, lead (leaded fuel), smoke |
| **2. Industries** | SO₂, NOx, PM, fluorides, metals, organic chemicals |
| **3. Domestic sources** | Burning of wood, coal, cow dung, kerosene → smoke, CO, PM |
| **4. Brick kilns** | PM, SO₂, CO, black carbon |
| **5. Construction and road dust** | Coarse and fine PM |
| **6. Miscellaneous** | Burning of refuse, incinerators, pesticide spraying, aircraft, nuclear tests |
| **7. Natural** | Volcanoes, forest fires, dust storms, pollen, sea salt |

**Process**

Emission of primary pollutants → dispersion (affected by wind, temperature inversion, humidity) → chemical reactions (sunlight → ozone, smog; SO₂ + water → acid rain) → exposure.

**Air-borne diseases**

| Group | Diseases |
|---|---|
| Infections spread by droplets / droplet nuclei | Tuberculosis, influenza, COVID-19, measles, chickenpox, diphtheria, pertussis, pneumonia, mumps, meningococcal meningitis |
| Due to air pollutants | Asthma, chronic bronchitis, COPD, lung cancer, ischaemic heart disease, stroke, pneumoconiosis (occupational dusts), allergic rhinitis |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.221
**Effects of air pollution**

**A. Health effects**

| Type | Effects |
|---|---|
| **Immediate (acute)** | Eye, nose and throat irritation; cough; acute bronchitis; asthma attacks; CO poisoning; deaths in smog episodes (London smog, 1952) |
| **Delayed (chronic)** | Chronic bronchitis, COPD, emphysema, **lung cancer**, reduced lung function in children, ischaemic heart disease, stroke |
| **Specific pollutants** | CO → carboxyhaemoglobin, hypoxia; SO₂ → bronchospasm; NO₂ → lung irritation, infections; ozone → reduced lung function; lead → anaemia, neurotoxicity in children; PM₂.₅ → enters lungs and blood, CVD |
| **Vulnerable groups** | Children, elderly, pregnant women (low birth weight, preterm birth), people with heart and lung disease |

**B. Social and economic effects**

1. Damage to plants and crops; reduced yield.
2. Harm to animals.
3. Corrosion and soiling of buildings, metals, monuments.
4. Reduced visibility → accidents, flight delays.
5. Loss of working days; health-care costs.

**C. Environmental effects**

Acid rain; photochemical smog; ozone layer depletion (CFCs); global warming (CO₂, methane, black carbon).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.222
**Indoor (household) air pollution**

Pollution of air inside homes, schools and workplaces, mainly from burning solid fuels for cooking and heating in poorly ventilated kitchens.

**Sources**

Biomass fuels (wood, crop residue, cow dung), coal, kerosene; tobacco smoke; mosquito coils; incense; building materials (asbestos, formaldehyde); dampness and moulds; radon; cleaning chemicals.

**Impacts on health**

| Group | Effects |
|---|---|
| **Children** | Acute respiratory infections and **pneumonia** (leading cause of under-5 deaths); otitis media; reduced lung growth |
| **Women** (cooks) | **COPD**, chronic bronchitis, lung cancer (coal), cataract, headache, eye irritation |
| **Pregnancy** | Low birth weight, stillbirth, preterm birth |
| **All** | TB, asthma, ischaemic heart disease, stroke |
| **Acute** | CO poisoning, burns, kerosene poisoning in children |

**Other impacts**

1. Time lost collecting fuel; drudgery for women and girls.
2. Deforestation; contribution to climate change (black carbon).
3. Economic loss from illness.

**Prevention**

Clean fuels (LPG, electricity, biogas); improved cookstoves with chimney; kitchen ventilation; separate kitchen; keep children away from cooking smoke; no smoking indoors.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.223
**Tools for monitoring air pollution**

**A. Classical methods (Park)**

| Tool / index | Measures |
|---|---|
| **Sulphur dioxide** measurement (lead peroxide candle; chemical methods) | SO₂ concentration |
| **Smoke or soiling index** | Darkening of filter paper by smoke |
| **Grit and dust measurement** (deposit gauge) | Settleable particles |
| **Coefficient of haze** | Light transmission through filtered air |
| **Air pollution index** | Combined value from several pollutants |

**B. Modern methods**

1. **Continuous Air Monitoring Stations (CAMS)**: automatic analysers for PM₂.₅, PM₁₀, SO₂, NO₂, CO, O₃ (Bangladesh Department of Environment).
2. **High-volume and low-volume samplers** for particulate matter (gravimetric).
3. **Air Quality Index (AQI)**: converts pollutant levels into colour-coded categories for public warning.
4. **Low-cost sensors** and online platforms.
5. **Satellite remote sensing** (aerosol optical depth, NO₂).
6. **Personal exposure monitors** (indoor air, workers).
7. **Biomonitoring**: blood lead, carboxyhaemoglobin, lichens as bio-indicators.
8. **Health surveillance**: hospital visits for asthma, COPD during high pollution days.

**Uses**

Compare with standards (WHO AQG 2021, national standards); warn the public; evaluate control measures.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.224
**Air pollution in Dhaka city**

Dhaka is among the most polluted cities in the world; levels of **PM₂.₅** many times the WHO guideline (annual 5 µg/m³), worst in the **dry season (November–March)**.

**Main sources**

1. **Brick kilns** around the city (dry season).
2. **Vehicle emissions**: old, poorly maintained buses and trucks; diesel; traffic congestion.
3. **Construction and road dust**: unpaved roads, open building sites, metro and flyover works, digging.
4. **Industrial emissions**: steel re-rolling, chemical and textile units.
5. **Open burning** of waste and biomass.
6. **Trans-boundary pollution** from neighbouring regions in winter.
7. Household fuel use in slums.

**Contributing factors**

Rapid unplanned urbanisation; high population density; weak enforcement; temperature inversion and low wind in winter.

**Health impact**

Respiratory infections, asthma, COPD, lung cancer, heart disease, stroke; reduced life expectancy; worst in children, elderly and outdoor workers.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health); WHO Global Air Quality Guidelines, 2021.

## DU.225
**Prevention and control of air pollution (Park)**

| Principle | Measures |
|---|---|
| **1. Containment** | Prevent escape of pollutants: filters, electrostatic precipitators, scrubbers, closed processes |
| **2. Replacement** | Change process or fuel: unleaded petrol, CNG/LPG, electric vehicles, cleaner technology |
| **3. Dilution** | Green belts, tall chimneys, zoning of industries away from residential areas |
| **4. Legislation** | Air quality standards; emission limits; vehicle fitness tests; licensing |
| **5. International action** | WHO air quality guidelines; monitoring networks; climate agreements |

**Control in Dhaka city**

1. **Brick kilns**: replace old kilns with cleaner technology (zigzag, hybrid Hoffmann); promote hollow and concrete blocks; relocate kilns away from city.
2. **Vehicles**: phase out old and unfit vehicles; emission testing; CNG and electric public transport; mass transit (metro rail, BRT); traffic management.
3. **Construction dust**: cover sites and materials; water spraying; finish road works quickly; pave roads; street cleaning.
4. **Industry**: emission control devices; enforcement by Department of Environment.
5. **No open burning** of waste; proper solid waste management.
6. **Urban greening**: trees, parks.
7. **Monitoring**: CAMS, public AQI alerts; health advisories (masks, limit outdoor activity).
8. **Laws**: Environment Conservation Act 1995; Air Pollution (Control) Rules 2022.
9. **Household**: clean cooking fuels.
10. **Regional cooperation** on trans-boundary pollution.

**WHO strategy**

Global Air Quality Guidelines (2021); WHO road map on health and air pollution; promote clean household energy, sustainable transport, monitoring and health-sector advocacy.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health); WHO Global Air Quality Guidelines, 2021.

## DU.226
**Components of air quality**

**A. Normal composition of air (dry, by volume)**
