An occupational **hypersensitivity pneumonitis (extrinsic allergic alveolitis)** caused by inhalation of dust from **mouldy hay, straw or grain** containing **thermophilic actinomycetes** (*Saccharopolyspora rectivirgula*, formerly *Micropolyspora faeni*; *Thermoactinomyces vulgaris*). It is an **organic-dust pneumoconiosis**.

**At risk**

Farmers handling damp, stored hay, straw, grain; agricultural and dairy workers.

**Clinical features**

- **Acute**: 4–8 hours after exposure: fever, chills, malaise, cough, breathlessness, crepitations; resolves in 1–2 days if exposure stops.
- **Chronic** (repeated exposure): progressive breathlessness, weight loss, **pulmonary fibrosis**, cor pulmonale.

**Diagnosis**

History of exposure; chest X-ray (diffuse nodular shadows; fibrosis later); lung function (restrictive pattern); serum precipitins to the antigens.

**Prevention**

1. Dry hay and grain properly before storage (prevents mould growth).
2. Mechanical handling; good ventilation of barns.
3. Dust masks / respirators.
4. Health education; early recognition.
5. Removal from exposure of affected workers.

**Treatment**

Avoid further exposure; corticosteroids in acute severe cases.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.288
**Occupational cancers and causative exposures**

| Cancer | Agent | Industry / workers |
|---|---|---|
| **Skin** | Soot, tar, pitch, mineral oils, arsenic, UV | Chimney sweeps (scrotal cancer, Pott 1775), tar and petroleum workers, outdoor workers |
| **Lung** | Asbestos, arsenic, chromium, nickel, uranium/radon, coal-tar fumes, cadmium, beryllium, silica | Asbestos and ship-breaking, mining, chrome plating, tanneries, nickel refining |
| **Mesothelioma** | Asbestos | Insulation, ship-breaking, construction |
| **Urinary bladder** | Aromatic amines (benzidine, β-naphthylamine) | **Dye**, rubber, cable, chemical industries |
| **Leukaemia** | Benzene; ionising radiation | Petroleum, rubber, shoe industries; radiology |
| **Liver (angiosarcoma)** | Vinyl chloride | PVC plastic industry |
| **Nasal sinus** | Wood dust, nickel, leather dust | Furniture, nickel, leather workers |
| **Bone** | Radium | Luminous dial painters |

**Industries in Bangladesh with cancer risk**

Ship-breaking (asbestos), tanneries (chromium), dyeing and textile (aromatic amines), rubber, chemical and pesticide factories, battery recycling, agriculture (pesticides).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.289
**Control measures of occupational cancers (Park)**

1. **Elimination or substitution** of carcinogens (ban asbestos; replace benzidine dyes; replace benzene with safer solvents).
2. **Engineering control**: closed processes, enclosure, automation, local exhaust ventilation.
3. **Medical examination**:
   - Pre-placement.
   - **Periodic**: urine cytology (bladder cancer in dye workers), chest X-ray and sputum cytology (asbestos, chromium), skin examination, blood counts (benzene).
   - Continued follow-up **after leaving the job** (long latent period).
4. **Personal hygiene**: daily bath, change of clothes, no eating in work areas.
5. **Personal protective equipment**: respirators, gloves, aprons.
6. **Inspection of factories** and monitoring of exposure levels against permissible limits.
7. **Notification** of occupational cancers; cancer registries.
8. **Licensing** of industries using carcinogens.
9. **Health education** and training of workers; no smoking (synergy with asbestos).
10. **Legislation** and compensation.
11. **Research**: identify new carcinogens.

**Examples**

| Cancer | Specific control |
|---|---|
| Bladder (dyes) | Ban benzidine; urine cytology every 6 months |
| Mesothelioma, lung (asbestos) | Ban or strict control of asbestos; wet methods; respirators; no smoking |
| Skin (tar, oils) | Protective clothing, washing, barrier creams |
| Leukaemia (benzene, radiation) | Substitution; radiation protection; blood counts |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.290
**Workers at risk of plumbism (lead poisoning)**

1. **Lead–acid battery** manufacturing and **recycling** (major risk in Bangladesh's informal sector).
2. Lead **mining, smelting and refining**.
3. **Painters** using lead-based paints; paint manufacture; scraping old paint.
4. **Printing** (type-metal workers).
5. **Plumbers** and pipe makers.
6. **Ceramic and pottery** glazing; glass making.
7. **Ship-breaking**, welding and cutting of lead-painted metal.
8. Cable and solder makers; electronics recycling.
9. **Rubber and plastic** industries (lead stabilisers).
10. Ammunition makers; firing-range staff.
11. Petrol industry (historically tetraethyl lead).
12. Cosmetic (kohl/surma) and traditional medicine makers.

**Routes**

Mainly **inhalation** of fumes and dust; also ingestion (eating, smoking with dirty hands). Children of workers exposed through dust brought home.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.291
**Features of plumbism (lead poisoning)**

**A. Symptoms and signs (Park)**

1. **Facial pallor**, especially around the mouth: earliest and most consistent sign.
2. **Abdominal colic** (lead colic), obstinate **constipation**, loss of appetite, metallic taste.
3. **Burtonian line**: blue line on the gums (lead sulphide).
4. **Anaemia**: microcytic hypochromic, with **punctate basophilia** (basophilic stippling of RBCs).
5. **Peripheral neuropathy** (motor): **wrist drop**, foot drop.
6. **Encephalopathy** (more in children): headache, irritability, convulsions, coma; learning and behaviour problems.
7. **Kidney**: nephropathy, hypertension, gout (saturnine gout).
8. **Reproductive**: abortion, stillbirth, reduced sperm count.
9. Fatigue, muscle and joint pain.

**B. Laboratory diagnosis**

| Test | Finding |
|---|---|
| Blood lead | Raised (best indicator of recent exposure) |
| Urinary lead | Raised |
| **Urinary coproporphyrin (CPU)** | Raised (useful screening test) |
| **Urinary δ-aminolaevulinic acid (ALA)** | Raised |
| Blood film | Basophilic stippling, anaemia |
| Zinc protoporphyrin | Raised |

**C. Management**

Remove from exposure; chelation (calcium disodium EDTA, DMSA/succimer, penicillamine); treat anaemia and colic.

**D. Prevention**

Substitution; exhaust ventilation; wet methods; personal hygiene (wash before eating, no eating at work); PPE; periodic blood lead and CPU tests; job rotation.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.292
**Two purposes of periodic medical examination of workers**

1. **Early detection** of occupational diseases and adverse health effects (e.g., silicosis, lead poisoning, NIHL) at a reversible or treatable stage, so the worker can be treated or transferred.
2. **Evaluation of control measures**: shows whether the working environment and preventive measures (ventilation, PPE) are effective.

**Other purposes**

- Detect **non-occupational diseases** (TB, diabetes, hypertension).
- Check continued **fitness** for the job; protect vulnerable workers (pregnant women, adolescents).
- Maintain **records** for research and compensation claims.
- Opportunity for **health education** and counselling.

**Examples**

Blood lead and CPU in battery workers; audiometry in noisy industries; chest X-ray and lung function in dust-exposed workers; urine cytology in dye workers.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.293
**Occupational hazards of agricultural workers (Park)**

| Hazard | Examples |
|---|---|
| **1. Zoonotic / biological** | Brucellosis, leptospirosis, anthrax, tetanus, Q fever, rabies, hookworm and cutaneous larva migrans, fungal infections; vector-borne (malaria, kala-azar, dengue, scrub typhus); Nipah (date-palm sap) |
| **2. Accidents** | Injuries from tractors, threshers, power tillers, sharp tools; falls from trees; **snake bites**, insect and animal bites; lightning strikes; drowning |
| **3. Toxic (chemical)** | **Pesticide poisoning** (organophosphates, carbamates); herbicides; fertilisers; skin and eye irritation |
| **4. Physical** | Heat stress and heat stroke, sun exposure (skin cancer, cataract), rain and cold, noise of machines, vibration |
| **5. Respiratory** | Organic dusts: farmer's lung, grain dust, bagassosis; smoke from crop burning |
| **6. Ergonomic** | Backache, joint pain from bending, lifting, prolonged squatting |
| **7. Psychosocial** | Economic insecurity, debt, crop failure → stress, depression, suicide (often by pesticide ingestion) |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.294
**Biological hazards faced by agricultural workers**

| Disease | Source / mode |
|---|---|
| **Tetanus** | Soil-contaminated wounds |
| **Leptospirosis** | Rat urine in flooded paddy fields, water |
| **Hookworm**, cutaneous larva migrans | Walking barefoot on faecally contaminated soil |
| **Brucellosis** | Contact with cattle, goats; raw milk |
| **Anthrax** | Handling infected animals, carcasses, hides |
| **Q fever** | Cattle, sheep, goats |
| **Rabies** | Bites of dogs, jackals, mongooses |
| **Snake bites**, scorpion and insect stings | Fields, bushes, during rainy season |
| **Fungal infections** | Wet work: tinea, candidiasis; mycetoma (Madura foot) |
| **Vector-borne diseases** | Malaria, kala-azar, dengue, Japanese encephalitis, scrub typhus |
| **Nipah virus** | Raw date-palm sap contaminated by bats |
| **Farmer's lung** | Mouldy hay and grain (actinomycetes) |
| **Schistosomiasis** (where endemic) | Water contact |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.295
**Protection of agricultural workers**

**A. Against biological hazards**

1. **Tetanus immunisation** (Td) for all farmers.
2. **Footwear and gloves**; protect wounds.
3. Rodent control; avoid wading in contaminated water (leptospirosis).
4. **Vaccination of animals**; safe handling of carcasses; boiling milk.
5. Rabies post-exposure prophylaxis; dog vaccination.
6. Bed nets, repellents against vectors.
7. Avoid raw date-palm sap (Nipah).
8. **Antivenom** at upazila health complexes; first aid for snake bite.

**B. Against chemical hazards (pesticides)**

1. Training in **safe use**: correct dose, mixing, spraying with the wind.
2. **PPE**: gloves, mask, goggles, long clothes; wash after spraying.
3. Safe storage away from children and food; proper disposal of containers.
4. **Integrated pest management**; ban highly hazardous pesticides.
5. Atropine and pralidoxime available at health facilities.

**C. Against physical and accident hazards**

Machine guards and training; safe use of tools; rest, shade and drinking water in heat; hats; lightning safety; first-aid kits.

**D. Ergonomic**

Better tools; correct lifting; rest breaks; mechanisation.

**E. Health services and welfare**

Primary health care in rural areas; periodic health check-ups; health education; crop and health insurance; social protection; mental health support.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.296
**General measures for prevention of occupational hazards (Park)**

**A. Medical measures**

1. **Pre-placement examination**: place the right worker in the right job; baseline data.
2. **Periodic medical examination**: early detection; evaluation of controls.
3. **Medical and health care services**: first aid, treatment, immunisation, rehabilitation.
4. **Notification** of occupational diseases.
5. **Supervision of working environment**: inspection, monitoring.
6. **Maintenance and analysis of records**.
7. **Health education and counselling**.

**B. Engineering measures**

1. Proper **design of building** (space, light, ventilation).
2. **Good housekeeping** (cleanliness, wet sweeping).
3. **General ventilation**.
4. **Mechanisation** and automation of hazardous processes.
5. **Substitution** of less harmful materials or processes.
6. **Dust control**: prevent formation, wet methods, suppression.
7. **Enclosure** of hazardous processes.
8. **Isolation** of hazardous processes (in space or time).
9. **Local exhaust ventilation**.
10. **Protective devices** (PPE): respirators, ear plugs, gloves, goggles, aprons.
11. **Environmental monitoring** of hazards.
12. **Statistical monitoring** of health and absence data.
13. **Research**.

**C. Legislative measures**

Labour and factory laws; permissible exposure limits; inspection; compensation; ILO conventions.

**Hierarchy of control**

Elimination → Substitution → Engineering controls → Administrative controls → PPE (least effective)

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.297
**Legislative measures to prevent industrial health hazards (Bangladesh)**

**Bangladesh Labour Act 2006 (amended) and Labour Rules 2015** provide for:

| Area | Provisions |
|---|---|
| **Health and hygiene** | Cleanliness; ventilation and temperature; dust and fume control; artificial humidification standards; overcrowding limits; lighting; safe drinking water; latrines and urinals; spittoons; waste disposal |
| **Safety** | Fire precautions, exits and drills; fencing of machinery; safety of buildings and machinery; pressure plant; protection of eyes; dangerous fumes; restriction on lifting excessive weights; safety committees |
| **Welfare** | First aid; canteen; rest rooms; **crèche** for children of women workers; washing facilities |
| **Working hours** | Limits on daily and weekly hours; rest intervals; weekly holiday; limited overtime |
| **Women** | **Maternity benefit** (16 weeks); restrictions on dangerous work |
| **Children and adolescents** | Prohibition of employment of children; restrictions and fitness certificates for adolescents |
| **Notification** | Accidents, dangerous occurrences and occupational diseases |
| **Compensation** | For injury, occupational disease and death |
| **Inspection** | Department of Inspection for Factories and Establishments (DIFE) |

**Other measures**

National Occupational Health and Safety Policy (2013); Environment Conservation Act 1995; building codes; post-Rana Plaza factory safety inspections (fire, electrical, structural); ratification of ILO conventions; permissible exposure limits; licensing of hazardous industries.

**Requirements for effectiveness**

Enforcement, adequate inspectors, trade union participation, employer and worker awareness.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.298
**Anthracosis (coal workers' pneumoconiosis)**

Pneumoconiosis caused by inhalation of **coal dust** in coal mines (Bangladesh: Barapukuria).

**Prevention**

**A. Dust control (engineering)**

1. **Wet methods**: water sprays at cutting points; wet drilling; water infusion into coal seams.
2. **Adequate mine ventilation** to dilute and remove dust.
3. Dust extraction at source; enclosed conveyors.
4. **Regular dust monitoring** against permissible limits.

**B. Personal protection**

Respirators / dust masks; protective clothing.

**C. Medical measures**

1. **Pre-placement examination**: chest X-ray, lung function; exclude TB and chronic lung disease.
2. **Periodic examination**: chest X-ray (ILO classification), spirometry, sputum for TB.
3. Early cases transferred to dust-free work.
4. Treatment and rehabilitation; TB screening.

**D. Administrative**

Limit duration of exposure; job rotation; training and health education; **no smoking** (adds to lung damage).

**E. Legislative**

Mines Act and labour laws; inspection; notification; compensation.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.299
**a) Type of occupational hazard (1)**

**Biological hazard**: percutaneous exposure to **blood-borne pathogens** (hepatitis B, hepatitis C, HIV) through a **needle-stick (sharps) injury**; caused by unsafe practice (recapping).

**b) Factors to assess the risk of transmission (3)**

| Factor | Higher risk if |
|---|---|
| **Type of device** | **Hollow-bore** needle containing blood (vs solid suture needle) |
| **Depth of injury** | Deep penetration |
| **Visible blood** on the device | Present |
| **Site of prior use** | Needle placed directly in the patient's vein or artery |
| **Volume of blood** | Larger; recently used needle |
| **Barrier** | No gloves (gloves wipe off some blood) |
| **Source patient** | HBsAg positive (especially HBeAg positive / high viral load), anti-HCV positive, HIV positive (high viral load, advanced disease); test source with consent |
| **Exposed person** | **Hepatitis B vaccination status and anti-HBs level**; baseline HBV, HCV, HIV status |
| **Time and first aid** | Delay in washing and reporting |

Approximate transmission risk after needle-stick: HBV ~6–30% (non-immune), HCV ~1.8%, HIV ~0.3%.

**c) Measures if the source is HBsAg positive (3)**

1. **Immediate first aid**: wash with soap and running water; do not squeeze or suck; report and document the incident.
2. **Baseline tests** of the nurse: HBsAg, anti-HBs, anti-HCV, HIV.
3. **Post-exposure prophylaxis by vaccination status**:

| Nurse's status | Action |
|---|---|
| Unvaccinated | **HBIG** (0.06 ml/kg IM) + start **hepatitis B vaccine series**, as soon as possible (ideally within 24 hours) |
| Vaccinated, known responder (anti-HBs ≥10 mIU/ml) | No treatment |
| Vaccinated, known non-responder | HBIG ×2 (one month apart), or HBIG + revaccination |
| Vaccinated, response unknown | Test anti-HBs: adequate → nothing; inadequate → HBIG + vaccine booster |

4. **Follow-up testing** (HBsAg, anti-HBc, anti-HBs) at about 6 months; also HCV and HIV follow-up as indicated.
5. **Counselling**; avoid donating blood, organs or semen during follow-up; no work restriction needed.
6. **Prevention of recurrence**: no recapping; sharps containers at point of use; safety-engineered devices; training; hepatitis B vaccination of all health workers; incident reporting system; compensation if infection occurs.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health; Epidemiology of Communicable Diseases).

## DU.300
**(a) Diagnosis and occupation (2)**

- **Diagnosis**: **Silicosis** (irritant cough, exertional dyspnoea, "snow-storm" nodular opacities on chest X-ray).
- **Likely occupations**: stone crushing, quarrying, mining, tunnelling, **sand blasting**, glass and pottery making, foundry work, construction (cutting concrete or stone), ceramic and tile industries.

**(b) Preventive measures (2)**

1. **Dust control**: wet drilling and cutting; enclosure; local exhaust ventilation; substitute silica sand with safer abrasives (steel grit) in blasting.
2. **Dust monitoring** against permissible limits.
3. **Respirators** and protective clothing.
4. **Medical examination**: pre-placement and periodic chest X-ray and lung function; early removal from exposure.
5. **TB screening** (silicosis greatly raises TB risk); no smoking.
6. Health education; limit exposure time; legislation, notification and compensation.

**(c) Diseases due to organic and inorganic dusts (3)**

| Inorganic dusts | Organic dusts |
|---|---|
| **Silicosis** (silica) | **Byssinosis** (cotton, flax, hemp) |
| **Anthracosis** (coal) | **Bagassosis** (sugarcane) |
| **Asbestosis** (asbestos); mesothelioma, lung cancer | **Farmer's lung** (mouldy hay, grain) |
| **Siderosis** (iron) | Bird fancier's lung |
| Berylliosis (beryllium) | Mushroom worker's lung, malt worker's lung |
| Stannosis (tin), talcosis, baritosis | Tobacosis; occupational asthma (wood dust) |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Occupational Health).

## DU.301
**Common health problems of Bangladesh**

| Group | Problems |
|---|---|
| **Communicable diseases** | ARI and pneumonia; diarrhoeal diseases; tuberculosis; **dengue** (large urban outbreaks); typhoid; hepatitis B and E; malaria (hill tracts); measles and other vaccine-preventable diseases (outbreaks when coverage drops); STIs/HIV (low prevalence, key populations); emerging infections (Nipah, COVID-19) |
| **Non-communicable diseases** (now the leading causes of death) | Hypertension, ischaemic heart disease, **stroke**, **diabetes**, COPD and asthma, cancers (lung, oral, cervical, breast), chronic kidney disease |
| **Maternal and child health** | Maternal and neonatal deaths (birth asphyxia, prematurity, sepsis); home deliveries; child marriage and adolescent pregnancy |
| **Nutrition** | Stunting (23.6% of under-5s, 2022), wasting, underweight; anaemia in women and children; micronutrient deficiencies; rising overweight and obesity |
| **Mental health** | Depression, anxiety, substance use, suicide |
| **Injuries** | Road traffic injuries, **drowning** (leading killer of children aged 1–4), burns, falls, occupational injuries |
| **Environmental** | Arsenic in groundwater, air pollution, unsafe water and sanitation in slums, climate-related disasters |
| **Others** | Snake bite, population ageing, disability, antimicrobial resistance, tobacco use |

**Underlying factors**

Poverty, illiteracy, high population density, rapid urbanisation, out-of-pocket health spending, shortage and maldistribution of health workers.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community); Our World in Data, Bangladesh health profile, https://ourworldindata.org/profile/health/bangladesh

## DU.302
**Important national health programmes of Bangladesh**

| Area | Programmes |
|---|---|
| **Immunisation** | **Expanded Programme on Immunization (EPI)** (since 1979) |
| **Communicable diseases** | National Tuberculosis Control Programme; National Leprosy Programme; National Malaria Elimination Programme; Kala-azar Elimination Programme; Lymphatic Filariasis Elimination Programme; National AIDS/STD Programme; dengue control; hepatitis control; zoonotic disease control |
| **Maternal, newborn, child and adolescent health** | MNC&AH programme; IMCI; safe motherhood; adolescent health |
| **Family planning** | National Family Planning Programme (DGFP) |
| **Nutrition** | National Nutrition Services (vitamin A, IYCF, micronutrients, SAM management); salt iodisation |
| **Non-communicable diseases** | NCD Control programme (hypertension, diabetes, cancer, stroke, COPD screening); tobacco control; mental health; eye care |
| **Primary health care** | Community Based Health Care (community clinics) |
| **Environmental health** | Arsenic mitigation; WASH (with DPHE) |
| **Others** | School health; occupational health; emergency preparedness; hospital services; health information (MIS) |

These run as operational plans under the national Health, Population and Nutrition Sector Programme.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.303
**Essential Service Package (ESP)**

A set of cost-effective, high-priority primary-level services provided free or at low cost to all, especially women, children and the poor; introduced under the Health and Population Sector Programme (1998) and continued in later sector programmes.

**Components**

| Component | Services |
|---|---|
| **1. Reproductive health care** | Safe motherhood (ANC, delivery, PNC, EmOC referral); family planning; menstrual regulation and post-abortion care; adolescent reproductive health; RTI/STI management |
| **2. Child health care** | EPI; IMCI (ARI, diarrhoea, malaria, measles, malnutrition); newborn care; vitamin A; growth monitoring |
| **3. Communicable disease control** | TB (DOTS), leprosy, malaria, kala-azar, filariasis, HIV/AIDS, dengue |
| **4. Limited curative care** | First aid, treatment of common illnesses and injuries; referral |
| **5. Behaviour change communication (BCC)** | Health, nutrition and family planning messages |

**Later additions (revised ESP)**

Nutrition services; NCD screening (hypertension, diabetes); mental health; eye and oral health; elderly care.

**Delivery points**

Community clinics, union facilities, Upazila Health Complexes, urban PHC centres, outreach (satellite clinics, EPI sites).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.305
**Diabetic Association of Bangladesh (BADAS)**

- Voluntary, non-profit social welfare organisation founded in **1956** by **Prof. Mohammad Ibrahim**.
- Pioneer in comprehensive diabetes care in Bangladesh and South Asia.
- Central institute: **BIRDEM** (Bangladesh Institute of Research and Rehabilitation in Diabetes, Endocrine and Metabolic Disorders), Dhaka; a WHO Collaborating Centre.
- Network of affiliated associations and diabetes centres in districts and upazilas.

**Activities**

1. **Registration and care**: lifelong registration of diabetic patients (guide book); outpatient and inpatient care at low cost or free for the poor.
2. **Health education**: patients, families, public; diet, exercise, foot care, insulin use.
3. **Prevention**: screening campaigns; awareness on World Diabetes Day; primary prevention programmes.
4. **Specialist services**: BIRDEM General Hospital (endocrinology, cardiology, nephrology, eye, foot care), maternal and child health in diabetes (gestational diabetes).
5. **Rehabilitation**: vocational and social support for diabetic patients.
6. **Training and education**: Ibrahim Medical College, Bangladesh University of Health Sciences, nursing and allied health institutes; postgraduate courses.
7. **Research**: epidemiological and clinical research on diabetes and NCDs.
8. **Supply** of insulin and drugs at fair prices.
9. **Advocacy** and partnership with government on NCD policy.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Epidemiology of Chronic Non-communicable Diseases).

**International health organisations (general)**

WHO, UNICEF, UNFPA, UNDP, ILO, FAO, World Bank; International Red Cross and Red Crescent Movement; bilateral agencies (USAID, JICA, FCDO, SIDA); global partnerships (Gavi, Global Fund); international NGOs (Save the Children, CARE, MSF).

**Four important (short answer)**

WHO, UNICEF, UNFPA, UNDP (or WFP).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health).

## DU.307
**WHO regions and regional headquarters**

| Region | Regional office (headquarters) |
|---|---|
| **South-East Asia Region (SEARO)** | **New Delhi, India** (Bangladesh belongs here) |
| African Region (AFRO) | Brazzaville, Republic of the Congo |
| Region of the Americas (AMRO / PAHO) | Washington, DC, USA |
| European Region (EURO) | Copenhagen, Denmark |
| Eastern Mediterranean Region (EMRO) | Cairo, Egypt |
| Western Pacific Region (WPRO) | Manila, Philippines |

**WHO headquarters**: Geneva, Switzerland.
**WHO founded**: 7 April 1948 (World Health Day).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health).

## DU.308
**Functions of WHO (Constitution, Article 2: main points)**

1. Act as the **directing and coordinating authority** on international health work.
2. Collaborate with the UN, specialised agencies, governments and professional groups.
3. **Assist governments** to strengthen health services.
4. Provide **technical assistance** and, in emergencies, necessary aid.
5. Stimulate work to **eradicate epidemic, endemic and other diseases**.
6. Promote prevention of accidental injuries.
7. Promote improved **nutrition, housing, sanitation**, working conditions and environmental hygiene.
8. Promote **maternal and child health** and **mental health**.
9. Promote and conduct **research**.
10. Propose **conventions, agreements and regulations** (IHR 2005, Framework Convention on Tobacco Control).
11. Set **international standards** for food, biological, pharmaceutical products; **international nomenclature** of diseases (ICD).
12. Promote **training** of health personnel.
13. Develop informed **public opinion** on health.

**Main areas of work (Park)**

Prevention and control of specific diseases; development of comprehensive health services; family health; environmental health; health statistics; biomedical research; health literature and information; cooperation with other organisations.

**Organs**

World Health Assembly (policy) → Executive Board (executive) → Secretariat (Director-General; administrative and technical).

**Current strategic aim**

Triple billion targets (GPW13): 1 billion more people benefiting from UHC, 1 billion better protected from health emergencies, 1 billion enjoying better health and well-being.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health); WHO Constitution, 1948.

## DU.309
**WHO's health-related activities in Bangladesh**

1. **Immunisation**: technical support to EPI; polio and measles-rubella surveillance; vaccine safety; campaigns.
2. **Communicable disease control and elimination**: TB, malaria, dengue, hepatitis, NTDs; supported elimination of **lymphatic filariasis** and **kala-azar** as public health problems (validated 2023).
3. **Health emergencies**: Rohingya humanitarian response, disease outbreaks (dengue, cholera, COVID-19), floods and cyclones.
4. **International Health Regulations**: strengthening surveillance and response (with IEDCR).
5. **NCD prevention and control**: hypertension and diabetes management (HEARTS), tobacco control (FCTC), cancer, mental health.
6. **Maternal, newborn, child and adolescent health**; nutrition.
7. **Health systems and UHC**: health financing, workforce, primary health care, quality of care, health information (DHIS2).
8. **Medicines and regulation**: essential medicines, drug regulation (DGDA), antimicrobial resistance.
9. **Environmental health and climate**: air quality, WASH, climate-resilient health systems.
10. **Policy advice, guidelines, research and training**.

**Five WHO-supported programmes (short answer)**

EPI; TB control; NTD elimination (kala-azar, filariasis); NCD/hypertension control; emergency and outbreak response.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health); WHO, 2023, https://www.who.int/news/item/31-10-2023-bangladesh-achieves-historic-milestone-by-eliminating-kala-azar-as-a-public-health-problem

## DU.310
**World Health Day (7 April) themes**

| Year | Theme (slogan) |
|---|---|
| **2024** | **"My health, my right"** |
| 2025 | "Healthy beginnings, hopeful futures" (maternal and newborn health; year-long campaign) |

**About World Health Day**

- Marks WHO's founding on **7 April 1948**.
- Each year WHO selects a priority theme to raise global awareness.

**2024 theme meaning**

Health is a **human right**: everyone, everywhere should have access to quality health services, education and information, safe water, clean air, good nutrition, decent housing and working conditions, free from discrimination.

**Ref:** WHO, World Health Day.

## DU.311
**Two roles of WHO in global health**

1. **Directing and coordinating authority on international health**: leads global responses (e.g., smallpox eradication, polio eradication, COVID-19 response) and coordinates countries and partners.
2. **Setting norms and standards**: International Health Regulations (2005), Framework Convention on Tobacco Control, International Classification of Diseases (ICD), essential medicines list, treatment guidelines, drinking-water and air-quality guidelines.

**Other roles**

- Technical support to countries (health systems, UHC).
- Monitoring global health trends (World Health Statistics).
- Research promotion and prequalification of vaccines and medicines.
- Emergency preparedness and response (declaring PHEIC).
- Advocacy for health equity and health as a human right.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health).

## DU.312
**Executive body of WHO: the Executive Board (EB)**

**Composition**

- **34 members**, technically qualified in the field of health, designated by Member States elected by the World Health Assembly.
- Members serve **three-year terms**; seats distributed among WHO regions.

**Meetings**

At least twice a year: a main session in January–February and a short session after the World Health Assembly (May).

**Functions**

1. **Gives effect to the decisions and policies** of the World Health Assembly (executive organ).
2. Prepares the **agenda** of the World Health Assembly.
3. Advises the Assembly on questions referred to it.
4. Submits the **programme of work** and budget proposals.
5. Takes **emergency measures** within WHO's functions (e.g., outbreaks, disasters).
6. Nominates the **Director-General** for appointment by the Assembly.

**WHO organs (for context)**

| Organ | Role |
|---|---|
| World Health Assembly | Supreme policy-making body; all Member States; meets annually in Geneva |
| **Executive Board** | Executive body |
| Secretariat | Director-General and staff; technical and administrative work |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health).

## DU.313
**UNICEF (United Nations Children's Fund)**

Founded 1946; works for the survival, development and protection of children; in Bangladesh since the early 1950s.

**Activities in Bangladesh**

| Area | Activities |
|---|---|
| **Child health** | Support to **EPI**: vaccine procurement, cold chain, campaigns (polio, measles-rubella, typhoid conjugate, HPV); newborn care (special care newborn units); IMCI |
| **Communicable disease control** | Immunisation; ORS and zinc promotion; diarrhoea and pneumonia control; HIV prevention among adolescents; outbreak response (cholera vaccines, COVID-19 vaccine delivery) |
| **Nutrition** | Vitamin A supplementation; IYCF and breastfeeding promotion; management of severe acute malnutrition; salt iodisation; micronutrients |
| **Maternal health** | ANC, facility delivery, midwifery support (with UNFPA) |
| **WASH** | Safe water (arsenic-safe options), sanitation, hygiene, WASH in schools |
| **Education** | Primary and early childhood education; out-of-school children |
| **Child protection** | Birth registration; prevention of child marriage, child labour and violence; Meena communication initiative |
| **Adolescents** | Health, menstrual hygiene, life skills |
| **Emergencies** | Rohingya response; floods and cyclones |
| **Social policy** | Child-sensitive social protection; advocacy |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health).

## DU.314
**USAID support to the health sector of Bangladesh: critical analysis**

**A. Contributions (strengths)**

1. **Family planning**: long-term support for contraceptive supply and social marketing (SMC); contributed to the fall in fertility.
2. **Maternal, newborn and child health**: community-based newborn care, chlorhexidine cord care, safe delivery, nutrition.
3. **Health surveys and data**: Bangladesh Demographic and Health Surveys (BDHS) and other surveys.
4. **NGO service delivery**: urban and rural clinic networks (Smiling Sun / Surjer Hasi).
5. **TB control**: case finding, MDR-TB management.
6. **Research**: support to icddr,b (ORS, zinc, newborn care evidence).
7. **Nutrition and food security**; humanitarian aid (Rohingya response).
8. Health systems strengthening: supply chain, health information.

**B. Limitations (criticisms)**

1. **Donor dependence**; questions of sustainability after funding ends.
2. **Vertical, project-based** programmes not always aligned with national sector priorities.
3. Funding linked to US policy priorities and conditions (e.g., restrictions on abortion-related services under the Mexico City Policy).
4. High administrative and consultancy costs; parallel systems.
5. Uneven geographic coverage; limited capacity transfer in some projects.

**C. Recent change**

The **2025 US foreign-aid freeze and restructuring of USAID** abruptly stopped or cut many programmes (family planning, TB, nutrition, surveys, humanitarian support), showing the risk of dependence on a single donor.

**Lesson**

Strengthen government ownership and domestic financing; align donor support with the national sector programme; build sustainable systems.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health).

## DU.315
**Role of international health agencies in Bangladesh's health services**

| Role | Examples |
|---|---|
| **1. Policy and technical support** | WHO guidelines and technical advice on EPI, TB, NCDs, UHC |
| **2. Financing** | World Bank and development partners through the Health, Population and Nutrition Sector Programme (pooled funds); Global Fund (TB, malaria, HIV); Gavi (vaccines) |
| **3. Supplies and logistics** | UNICEF vaccine procurement and cold chain; UNFPA contraceptives and reproductive health supplies |
| **4. Disease control and elimination** | WHO and partners: polio eradication, kala-azar and filariasis elimination, malaria, dengue response |
| **5. Maternal and child health, nutrition** | UNICEF, UNFPA, WFP: newborn care, midwifery, vitamin A, school feeding |
| **6. Emergencies** | WHO, UNICEF, UNHCR, IOM, WFP, MSF, Red Cross/Red Crescent: Rohingya crisis, floods, cyclones, COVID-19 |
| **7. Research** | Support to icddr,b and universities |
| **8. Human resources** | Training of health workers, midwifery education, fellowships |
| **9. Health information** | Surveys (BDHS, MICS), DHIS2, surveillance |
| **10. Advocacy** | Health rights, gender, tobacco control, SDGs |

**Challenges**

Dependence on external funds, fragmentation, alignment and coordination problems, sustainability; sudden aid cuts.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health).

## DU.316
**Red Crescent**

Part of the **International Red Cross and Red Crescent Movement** (ICRC, IFRC and national societies). In Bangladesh: **Bangladesh Red Crescent Society (BDRCS)**, a national voluntary humanitarian organisation (formerly Bangladesh Red Cross Society; renamed in 1988).

**Fundamental principles**

Humanity, impartiality, neutrality, independence, voluntary service, unity, universality.

**Activities**

| Area | Activities |
|---|---|
| **Disaster management** | **Cyclone Preparedness Programme** (with government): early warning, evacuation by volunteers; relief and recovery after floods, cyclones, fires |
| **Health services** | Hospitals (Holy Family Red Crescent Medical College Hospital), maternity and child health centres, community health programmes |
| **Blood services** | Voluntary blood donation and blood banks |
| **First aid** | Training of volunteers, students and public; ambulance services |
| **Refugee and humanitarian aid** | Rohingya response: health, WASH, shelter, psychosocial support |
| **WASH** | Safe water, sanitation, hygiene in disasters |
| **Restoring family links** | Tracing missing persons (with ICRC) |
| **Youth** | Red Crescent Youth: volunteering, health awareness |
| **Climate adaptation** | Community resilience, early action |
| **Dissemination** | International humanitarian law, protection of the emblem |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health).

## DU.317
**CARE in Bangladesh**

CARE (Cooperative for Assistance and Relief Everywhere) is an international NGO working in Bangladesh for decades, focusing on poverty, women's empowerment and humanitarian response.

**Health sector**

1. **Maternal and newborn health**: community-based skilled birth attendants, ANC, safe delivery, postnatal care.
2. **Nutrition**: food security and nutrition programmes for poor households (e.g., SHOUHARDO), growth monitoring, IYCF.
3. **WASH**: safe water, sanitation, hygiene behaviour change.
4. **Reproductive health and family planning**; adolescent health.
5. **HIV prevention** among vulnerable groups (earlier programmes).
6. **Health systems**: community engagement and accountability (community scorecards), support to local government health services.
7. **Emergency health**: Rohingya response, disasters.

**Agriculture sector**

1. **Food security** and improved farming for poor and women farmers.
2. **Climate-resilient agriculture**: saline- and flood-tolerant crops, homestead gardening.
3. **Livestock and dairy** value chains; market linkages.
4. Women's economic empowerment; savings groups.
5. Disaster risk reduction for farming communities.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health).

## DU.318
**Achievements of BIRDEM**

BIRDEM (Bangladesh Institute of Research and Rehabilitation in Diabetes, Endocrine and Metabolic Disorders), Dhaka: central institute of the **Diabetic Association of Bangladesh**; a **WHO Collaborating Centre**.

1. **Model of comprehensive diabetes care**: registration, treatment, education and rehabilitation under one roof, at low cost; free care for the poor.
2. **Large tertiary hospital** (BIRDEM General Hospital) with endocrinology, cardiology, nephrology and dialysis, eye, foot care, surgery, ICU.
3. **Nationwide network** of affiliated diabetic centres in districts and upazilas.
4. **Health education** of patients (diet, insulin, foot care) and public awareness.
5. **Research**: epidemiological and clinical research on diabetes, gestational diabetes, NCDs; publications.
6. **Training and education**: postgraduate courses; Ibrahim Medical College; Bangladesh University of Health Sciences; nursing and allied health institutes.
7. **Maternal and child health in diabetes**; childhood diabetes services.
8. **Prevention programmes**: screening, primary prevention, World Diabetes Day activities.
9. **Recognition**: WHO Collaborating Centre for research and training in diabetes; regional leader in diabetes care.
10. **Rehabilitation**: social and vocational support for diabetic patients.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Epidemiology of Chronic Non-communicable Diseases).

## DU.319
**International Health Regulations (IHR, 2005)**

A **legally binding international instrument** adopted by the World Health Assembly in **2005**, in force since **2007**, binding on all WHO Member States (States Parties).

**Purpose and scope**

To prevent, protect against, control and provide a public health response to the **international spread of disease**, in ways that are proportionate to the public health risk and that avoid unnecessary interference with international traffic and trade.

**Key features**

1. **All-hazards**: covers infectious, chemical, radiological and other events, not a fixed list of diseases.
2. **Notification** to WHO within **24 hours** of assessing an event that may constitute a **Public Health Emergency of International Concern (PHEIC)** (decision instrument, Annex 2).
3. **National IHR Focal Point** in each country (Bangladesh: DGHS / IEDCR).
4. **Core capacities**: surveillance, laboratory, response, risk communication, points of entry (airports, ports, land crossings).
5. **PHEIC** declared by the WHO Director-General on advice of an **Emergency Committee**; temporary and standing recommendations.
6. **Health measures at points of entry**: vaccination certificates (yellow fever), screening, isolation.
7. Monitoring by annual reporting and joint external evaluation.
8. **Amended in 2024** (in force 2025): adds a "pandemic emergency" level and strengthens equity and coordination.

**Examples of PHEICs**

H1N1 influenza (2009), polio (2014), Ebola (2014, 2019), Zika (2016), COVID-19 (2020), mpox (2022, 2024).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health); WHO, International Health Regulations (2005).

## DU.320
**Importance of IHR in public health**

1. **Global health security**: early detection and containment of outbreaks before they spread across borders.
2. **Legal obligation**: binding on all States Parties; ensures timely **notification** and information sharing.
3. **All-hazards approach**: covers infectious, chemical, radiological and food-safety events.
4. **Builds national core capacities**: surveillance, laboratories, rapid response teams, risk communication, emergency preparedness.
5. **Points of entry**: health measures at airports, ports and land crossings (screening, isolation, vaccination certificates).
6. **Coordinated international response**: WHO can declare a **PHEIC** and issue recommendations to guide countries.
7. **Balances health with trade and travel**: prevents unnecessary restrictions that harm economies.
8. **Protects human rights** of travellers (dignity, privacy).
9. **Accountability**: monitoring and evaluation of country capacities.
10. **One Health** linkage: zoonotic and environmental threats.

**Bangladesh examples**

Surveillance and response for avian influenza, Nipah, MERS, COVID-19, mpox; IEDCR as national focal point; health screening at Hazrat Shahjalal International Airport and land ports.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (International Health); WHO, International Health Regulations (2005).

## DU.321
**Common insects in Bangladesh and diseases transmitted**

| Insect | Diseases |
|---|---|
| **Mosquito** | *Aedes*: **dengue**, chikungunya, Zika; *Anopheles*: **malaria**; *Culex*: **lymphatic filariasis**, Japanese encephalitis |
| **Sandfly** (*Phlebotomus argentipes*) | **Kala-azar** (visceral leishmaniasis), PKDL |
| **Housefly** (*Musca domestica*) | Diarrhoea, dysentery, cholera, typhoid, hepatitis A, trachoma, conjunctivitis, worm eggs, myiasis |
| **Louse** | Pediculosis; epidemic typhus, relapsing fever, trench fever (body louse) |
| **Rat flea** (*Xenopsylla cheopis*) | Plague, murine typhus |
| Cockroach | Mechanical carrier of enteric pathogens; allergy |
| Bed bug | Nuisance, sleep loss, anaemia (heavy infestation) |

**Other arthropods (non-insects)**

Itch mite → **scabies**; trombiculid mite → **scrub typhus**; ticks → tick-borne fevers; cyclops → guinea worm (not in Bangladesh).

**Three insects (short answer)**

Mosquito (dengue, malaria, filariasis), sandfly (kala-azar), housefly (diarrhoeal diseases, typhoid).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.322
**Definition**

**Medically important arthropods**: invertebrate animals with jointed legs, segmented body and chitinous exoskeleton (phylum **Arthropoda**) that cause disease directly or transmit infectious agents to man.

**Classification (Park)**

| Class | Main features | Examples and importance |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Insecta** | Body: head, thorax, abdomen; **3 pairs of legs**; 1 pair of antennae; usually wings | Mosquito, housefly, sandfly, tsetse fly, blackfly, louse, flea, reduviid bug, cockroach |
| **2. Arachnida** | Body: cephalothorax + abdomen; **4 pairs of legs**; no antennae, no wings | Ticks (tick fevers), mites (scabies, scrub typhus), spiders, scorpions (venom) |
| **3. Crustacea** | Two pairs of antennae; mostly aquatic; gills | Cyclops (guinea worm, fish tapeworm), crabs and crayfish (paragonimiasis) |
| **4. Chilopoda** | Centipedes: one pair of legs per segment | Venomous bites |
| **5. Diplopoda** | Millipedes: two pairs of legs per segment | Irritant secretions |

**Illustration (body plans)**

```
Insect:     [Head]-[Thorax: 3 pairs legs, wings]-[Abdomen]
Arachnid:   [Cephalothorax: 4 pairs legs]-[Abdomen]
Crustacean: [Cephalothorax: 2 pairs antennae]-[Abdomen]
```

**Ways arthropods harm man**

1. **Transmit disease** (vectors): malaria, dengue, kala-azar, plague.
2. **Cause disease directly**: scabies, pediculosis, myiasis.
3. **Bites and stings**: venom, allergy.
4. **Nuisance** and psychological stress.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.323
**Personal protective measures against arthropod-borne diseases**

1. **Bed nets**: insecticide-treated / long-lasting insecticidal nets (LLIN); use also for daytime naps (dengue).
2. **Repellents**: DEET, picaridin, IR3535 or plant-based (citronella) on exposed skin.
3. **Protective clothing**: long-sleeved shirts, full trousers, socks; light colours; insecticide-treated clothing.
4. **Screening** of doors and windows with wire mesh; fine mesh against sandflies.
5. **Mosquito coils, vaporisers, mats**, electric bats (for nuisance relief).
6. **Avoid exposure** at peak biting times (dusk and dawn for *Anopheles* and *Culex*; early morning and late afternoon for *Aedes*).
7. **Personal hygiene**: regular bathing, clean clothes and bedding → prevents lice and scabies; avoid sharing combs and clothes.
8. **Footwear** and avoiding rodent-infested areas (fleas, mites); insect repellent on legs in scrub areas (scrub typhus).
9. **Chemoprophylaxis** for travellers to malaria-endemic areas.
10. **Vaccination** where available (Japanese encephalitis).
11. Removing breeding sites around one's own home (water containers, flower vases).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.324
**Modes of transmission of arthropod-borne (vector-borne) diseases (Park)**

| Mode | Mechanism | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Direct contact** | Arthropod transferred by close contact; causes disease itself | Scabies (itch mite), pediculosis (lice) |
| **2. Mechanical transmission** | Agent carried on body, legs, mouthparts or in gut without multiplication or development | Housefly: typhoid, cholera, dysentery, trachoma |
| **3. Biological transmission** | Agent multiplies and/or develops in the vector before it can be transmitted (extrinsic incubation period) | |
| (a) Propagative | Multiplication only, no change in form | Plague bacilli in rat flea; dengue virus in *Aedes* |
| (b) Cyclo-propagative | Change in form + multiplication | Malaria parasite in *Anopheles* |
| (c) Cyclo-developmental | Change in form, no multiplication | Filarial worm in *Culex*; guinea worm in cyclops |
| **4. Transovarial transmission** | Agent passes from infected female to her eggs and next generation | Scrub typhus in mites; some arboviruses in mosquitoes and ticks |

**Routes from vector to man**

Bite (inoculation of saliva: malaria, dengue); regurgitation (blocked flea in plague); contamination of bite wound with vector faeces (murine typhus, Chagas disease); crushing of vector on skin (louse in relapsing fever); ingestion (cyclops in water).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.325
**Biological transmission**

The infectious agent undergoes **multiplication and/or developmental changes inside the vector** before it becomes infective; there is an **extrinsic incubation period** before the vector can transmit.

**Types (Park)**

| Type | What happens in the vector | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Propagative** | Agent **multiplies** but does not change form | Plague bacillus in rat flea; dengue, chikungunya, Japanese encephalitis viruses in mosquitoes; yellow fever virus in *Aedes* |
| **Cyclo-propagative** | Agent **changes form (cycle) and multiplies** | **Malaria**: gametocytes → gametes → zygote → ookinete → oocyst → thousands of sporozoites in salivary glands of *Anopheles* |
| **Cyclo-developmental** | Agent **develops (changes form) but does not multiply** | **Filariasis**: microfilaria → L1 → L2 → L3 (infective larva) in *Culex*; one microfilaria gives at most one L3; guinea worm in cyclops |

**Modes of biological transmission in mosquitoes**

| Mosquito | Disease | Type |
|---|---|---|
| *Anopheles* | Malaria | Cyclo-propagative |
| *Culex* | Filariasis | Cyclo-developmental |
| *Culex* | Japanese encephalitis | Propagative |
| *Aedes* | Dengue, chikungunya, Zika, yellow fever | Propagative (also transovarial for some viruses) |

**Importance**

Extrinsic incubation period depends on temperature; control can target vector longevity (adult control) so that it dies before becoming infective.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.326
**Principles of arthropod control (Park)**

| Principle | Methods | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Environmental control** (most permanent) | Source reduction: eliminate breeding and resting places; drainage, filling, cleaning; sanitation; improved housing | Emptying water containers (*Aedes*); clearing drains (*Culex*); plastering cracks (sandfly); refuse disposal (flies) |
| **2. Chemical control** | Insecticides: residual sprays (indoor residual spraying), space sprays (fogging), larvicides; repellents | Pyrethroids for IRS; malathion fogging; temephos larvicide |
| **3. Biological control** | Living organisms or their products that kill vectors | Larvivorous fish (*Gambusia*, guppy); *Bacillus thuringiensis israelensis* (Bti), *B. sphaericus* |
| **4. Genetic control** | Altering vector reproduction | Sterile male technique; *Wolbachia*-infected mosquitoes |
| **5. Personal protection** | Nets, repellents, screens, clothing | LLINs |
| **6. Integrated vector management (IVM)** | Rational combination of the above, based on local ecology, with community participation and intersectoral action | Dengue control in cities |

**Supporting measures**

Entomological surveillance (larval indices); monitoring insecticide resistance; legislation (fines for breeding sites); health education and community participation.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.327
**Class Insecta**

The largest class of the phylum Arthropoda.

**Characteristic features**

1. Body divided into **three parts**: head, thorax, abdomen.
2. **Head**: one pair of **antennae**, compound eyes, mouthparts (piercing-sucking in mosquito, sponging in housefly, biting).
3. **Thorax**: **three pairs of jointed legs**; usually **two pairs of wings** (one pair in flies and mosquitoes; none in lice and fleas).
4. **Abdomen**: segmented; no legs.
5. Chitinous exoskeleton; breathe through tracheae and spiracles.
6. Sexes separate.

**Metamorphosis**

| Type | Stages | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Complete (holometabolous)** | Egg → Larva → Pupa → Adult | Mosquito, housefly, sandfly, flea |
| **Incomplete (hemimetabolous)** | Egg → Nymph → Adult | Louse, bed bug, reduviid bug, cockroach |

**Medically important insects**

Mosquitoes, flies (housefly, sandfly, tsetse, blackfly), lice, fleas, bugs (reduviid, bed bug), cockroaches.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.328
**Importance of knowing medical entomology**

1. **Identify vectors** of important diseases (malaria, dengue, kala-azar, filariasis, plague).
2. Understand **modes of transmission** (mechanical, biological) and the extrinsic incubation period.
3. Know **life cycle, breeding, biting and resting habits** → choose the right control method and timing (e.g., daytime protection against *Aedes*).
4. **Plan and evaluate vector control** programmes (IRS, larviciding, source reduction).
5. **Entomological surveillance**: larval indices, vector density, infection rates → predict outbreaks.
6. **Epidemiological investigation** of outbreaks.
7. **Monitor insecticide resistance** and choose effective insecticides.
8. Recognise **emerging and re-emerging** vector-borne diseases (Zika, chikungunya) and effects of climate change on vectors.
9. **Health education** of the community on prevention.
10. Diagnose and manage diseases caused directly by arthropods (scabies, pediculosis, myiasis).
11. Support elimination programmes (kala-azar, filariasis, malaria in Bangladesh).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.329
**Definition**

**Medical entomology**: the branch of science that deals with the study of **arthropods of medical importance** (insects and related arthropods): their identification, structure, life cycle, habits, role in causing and transmitting disease to man, and their control.

**Scope**

1. **Vectors** of disease: mosquitoes, sandflies, fleas, lice, ticks, mites.
2. Arthropods causing disease directly: itch mite (scabies), lice, myiasis-producing flies.
3. Venomous arthropods: scorpions, spiders, bees, wasps.
4. Nuisance pests: bed bugs, cockroaches.
5. **Control** methods: environmental, chemical, biological, genetic, personal protection, integrated vector management.

**Related term**

Vector biology / public health entomology; essential in community medicine for control of vector-borne diseases.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.330
**Definition**

**Vector**: an arthropod or other living carrier that transports an infectious agent from an infected individual (or its wastes) to a susceptible individual, or to its food or immediate surroundings (Park).

**Types**

| Type | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Mechanical vector** | Carries agent on body or in gut without multiplication or development | Housefly → typhoid, cholera |
| **Biological vector** | Agent multiplies and/or develops in the vector before transmission | *Anopheles* → malaria; *Aedes* → dengue; sandfly → kala-azar; rat flea → plague |

**Related terms**

- **Extrinsic incubation period**: time for the agent to develop in the vector until it becomes infective.
- **Vector-borne disease**: disease transmitted by a vector.
- **Vectorial capacity**: efficiency of a vector population in transmitting disease (density, biting rate, longevity).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.331
**Aedes vs Anopheles mosquitoes**

| Feature | *Aedes* | *Anopheles* |
|---|---|---|
| **Eggs** | Laid singly; cigar-shaped; no floats; resist drying for months | Laid singly; boat-shaped with **lateral floats** |
| **Larva** | Short, stout **siphon tube**; hangs head-down at an angle to water surface | **No siphon**; lies **parallel** to water surface; palmate hairs |
| **Adult colour** | Black with **white stripes** on body and legs ("tiger mosquito") | Dark; **spotted wings** (dark and pale scales) |
| **Resting position** | Body **parallel** to surface (humped) | Body at an **angle** (~45°) to surface; proboscis and body in one line |
| **Palps (female)** | Short | As long as proboscis |
| **Biting time** | **Daytime** (early morning, late afternoon) | **Night** (dusk to dawn) |
| **Breeding** | **Clean water in artificial containers**: tyres, flower pots, water tanks, coconut shells, construction sites | Clean water: ponds, streams, rice fields, wells; some in brackish water |
| **Habitat** | Urban, domestic; rests indoors in dark places | Rural, forest, foothills (Bangladesh: hill tracts) |
| **Flight range** | Short | Longer |
| **Diseases** | **Dengue**, chikungunya, Zika, yellow fever | **Malaria** (also filariasis in some areas) |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.332
**Anopheles vs Culex mosquitoes**

| Feature | *Anopheles* | *Culex* |
|---|---|---|
| **Eggs** | Laid **singly**; boat-shaped with **lateral floats** | Laid in **rafts** (clusters); cigar-shaped; no floats |
| **Larva** | **No siphon tube**; rests **parallel** to water surface | **Long, narrow siphon tube**; hangs **at an angle** with head down |
| **Pupa** | Short, broad respiratory trumpets | Long, narrow respiratory trumpets |
| **Adult resting position** | Body at an **angle** to the surface | Body **parallel** to the surface (humped back) |
| **Wings** | **Spotted** | **Unspotted** |
| **Palps (female)** | **As long as** proboscis | **Much shorter** than proboscis |
| **Colour** | Dark, spotted | Dull brown |
| **Breeding** | **Clean** water: ponds, streams, rice fields | **Dirty, polluted** water: drains, cesspools, septic tanks, ditches |
| **Biting time** | Night | Night (major urban nuisance) |
| **Diseases** | **Malaria** | **Lymphatic filariasis** (*C. quinquefasciatus*), **Japanese encephalitis** (*C. tritaeniorhynchus*), West Nile fever |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.333
**Breeding habits of mosquitoes (general)**

| Genus | Breeding places |
|---|---|
| *Anopheles* | Clean, unpolluted water: ponds, streams, rice fields, wells, hill streams; some in brackish water |
| *Culex* | Dirty, polluted water: drains, cesspits, septic tanks, ditches, burrow pits |
| *Aedes* | Clean water in **artificial and natural containers** |
| *Mansonia* | Ponds with aquatic plants (larvae attach to roots) |

Life cycle: Egg → Larva → Pupa → Adult (aquatic stages ~7–10 days in warm weather).

**Aedes aegypti: breeding habits**

1. Breeds in **clean, stagnant water** in **artificial containers**: water tanks and drums, flower vases and pots, discarded tyres, tins, bottles, coconut shells, refrigerator trays, air-cooler trays, construction sites, roof gutters.
2. **Eggs laid singly** just above the water line on container walls; **withstand drying for months** → hatch when flooded (rain).
3. Small amounts of water are enough (bottle caps).
4. *Aedes albopictus* also breeds in natural containers: tree holes, leaf axils, bamboo stumps.

**Aedes aegypti: biting habits**

1. **Day-biter**: peaks in early morning and late afternoon.
2. **Anthropophilic** (prefers human blood); bites mainly around ankles and legs.
3. **Domestic and endophilic**: rests indoors in dark, cool places (under furniture, behind curtains).
4. **Multiple feeding**: bites several people in one blood meal cycle → efficient epidemic spread.
5. Short flight range (around the house).
6. Nervous feeder: disturbed easily, then bites again.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.334
**Mosquito-borne diseases (prevailing in Bangladesh)**

| Mosquito | Disease | Agent |
|---|---|---|
| ***Aedes aegypti*, *Ae. albopictus*** | **Dengue** (and severe dengue) | Dengue virus (4 serotypes) |
| | **Chikungunya** | Chikungunya virus |
| | Zika (sporadic) | Zika virus |
| ***Anopheles*** | **Malaria** (mainly Chattogram Hill Tracts and border districts) | *Plasmodium falciparum*, *P. vivax* |
| ***Culex quinquefasciatus*** | **Lymphatic filariasis** (eliminated as public health problem, 2023) | *Wuchereria bancrofti* |
| ***Culex tritaeniorhynchus*** / *Culex vishnui* group | **Japanese encephalitis** | JE virus |
| *Culex* | West Nile fever (risk) | West Nile virus |

**Other mosquito-borne diseases (global)**

Yellow fever, Rift Valley fever, brugian filariasis (*Mansonia*).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology; Epidemiology of Communicable Diseases).

## DU.335
**General principles of mosquito control**

**A. Anti-larval measures**

1. **Environmental control (source reduction)**: drain, fill or clean breeding places; remove containers; proper water storage; clean drains.
2. **Chemical larvicides**: temephos (Abate), fenthion, mineral oil (MLO), insect growth regulators (pyriproxyfen).
3. **Biological control**: larvivorous fish (*Gambusia*, guppy) in ponds and tanks; *Bacillus thuringiensis israelensis* (Bti).

**B. Anti-adult measures**

1. **Indoor residual spraying** (malaria vectors).
2. **Space spraying / fogging** (malathion, pyrethroids) in outbreaks.
3. Genetic control (sterile males, *Wolbachia*).

**C. Personal protection**

Bed nets (LLIN), repellents, screening, protective clothing, coils and vaporisers.

**D. Supporting**

Surveillance (larval and adult indices), insecticide-resistance monitoring, health education, community participation, legislation, **integrated vector management**.

**Control of Aedes (tiger mosquito) in Dhaka city / urban areas**

| Measure | Actions |
|---|---|
| **Source reduction (most important)** | Empty, clean and cover water containers every week; remove tyres, tins, bottles, coconut shells; clean roof gardens, flower pots, air-cooler and fridge trays; manage water at construction sites; clean gutters |
| **Community action** | Weekly "search and destroy" campaigns in homes, offices, schools; volunteer and ward-level drives |
| **Larviciding** | Temephos or Bti in containers that cannot be emptied |
| **Adult control** | Targeted fogging around dengue cases during outbreaks (not routine) |
| **Surveillance** | Larval indices: house index, container index, **Breteau index** (positive containers per 100 houses; >20 = high risk); case reporting |
| **Legislation** | City corporation inspection; fines for breeding sites on premises (mobile courts) |
| **Personal protection** | Daytime nets for children and patients, repellents, long clothes, screens |
| **Health education** | Mass media, schools, mosques before and during monsoon |
| **Intersectoral** | City corporations, DGHS, housing, education, construction regulators |
| **Research** | *Wolbachia*, insecticide resistance |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology; Epidemiology of Communicable Diseases).

## DU.336
**Why organochlorine compounds (DDT, BHC/lindane, dieldrin) are not suitable for larvicidal operations**

1. **Very persistent** in the environment; do not break down easily.
2. **Bio-accumulation and bio-magnification** in the food chain (water → plankton → fish → birds and man).
3. **Highly toxic to fish** and other aquatic non-target organisms, including larvivorous fish used for biological control.
4. **Contaminate water** sources used for drinking, bathing and fishing; poorly soluble in water.
5. **Widespread resistance** in mosquito larvae (e.g., *Culex*, *Anopheles*).
6. **Health hazards** in man: neurotoxic; stored in body fat; possible carcinogenic and endocrine effects.
7. **Banned or restricted** under the Stockholm Convention (2001); DDT allowed only for limited indoor residual spraying.

**Preferred larvicides**

Temephos (Abate; low mammalian toxicity, safe for drinking water at recommended dose), fenthion, mineral oils, *Bacillus thuringiensis israelensis* (Bti), insect growth regulators (pyriproxyfen, methoprene).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.337
**Genus**

Night-time biting nuisance mosquitoes in hostels and urban buildings are usually ***Culex*** (*Culex quinquefasciatus*): breeds in dirty, polluted water (drains, septic tanks, cesspits), bites at night, vector of **lymphatic filariasis**.

**Control measures**

**A. Anti-larval**

1. **Environmental**: clean and repair drains; remove stagnant polluted water; fill ditches and cesspits; cover septic tanks and manholes; proper sewage disposal; mosquito-proof soak pits.
2. **Polystyrene beads** floated in pit latrines, cesspools and soak pits (block larval breathing).
3. **Chemical larvicides**: temephos, fenthion, mineral oil in drains.
4. **Biological**: larvivorous fish in ponds; *Bacillus sphaericus*.

**B. Anti-adult**

1. Space spraying (fogging) with pyrethroids or malathion in heavy infestation.
2. Indoor residual spraying where needed.

**C. Personal protection in the hostel**

Bed nets (LLIN), wire-mesh screens on windows and doors, repellent creams, mosquito coils or vaporisers, electric mosquito bats, long-sleeved clothes in the evening.

**D. Administrative and community**

Regular hostel sanitation inspections; student and staff involvement in cleaning drains; coordination with city corporation; health education.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.338
**Diseases and hazards transmitted by housefly (*Musca domestica*)**

| Group | Diseases |
|---|---|
| **Enteric (faeco-oral)** | Typhoid and paratyphoid, cholera, bacillary dysentery, amoebic dysentery, infantile diarrhoea, gastroenteritis, hepatitis A, poliomyelitis (possible) |
| **Eye** | Trachoma, conjunctivitis |
| **Parasitic** | Eggs of roundworm, whipworm, tapeworm; protozoal cysts |
| **Skin** | Yaws, cutaneous infections; anthrax (rare) |
| **Myiasis** | Maggots in wounds, nose, ear |
| **Others** | Tuberculosis bacilli carried; nuisance; food spoilage |

**How the housefly acts as a vector (mechanical transmission)**

1. **Habits**: breeds and feeds on filth (faeces, garbage, manure) and then visits human food indiscriminately.
2. **Carries pathogens on body**: legs, sticky foot pads (pulvilli) and body hairs pick up germs from filth and deposit them on food.
3. **Vomit drop**: liquefies food by regurgitating saliva and gut contents (containing germs) onto food.
4. **Faeces**: defecates frequently on food; pathogens survive in its gut.
5. **Flight**: moves between latrines, garbage and kitchens; large numbers in summer.
6. Fast breeding: life cycle (egg → larva → pupa → adult) in about a week in hot weather.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.339
**Control of housefly (Park)**

**1. Environmental control (most important and permanent)**

- Proper **storage, collection and disposal of refuse** (covered bins, daily removal, sanitary landfill, composting).
- **Sanitary excreta disposal**; stop open defecation; fly-proof latrines (water seal).
- Sanitary disposal of animal manure; clean cattle sheds.
- Clean markets, slaughter houses and food establishments; general cleanliness.

**2. Insecticidal control**

- **Residual sprays** on resting surfaces (organophosphates: malathion, diazinon; pyrethroids).
- **Space sprays** (pyrethrum, pyrethroids) for quick kill.
- **Baits**: sugar or syrup with insecticide.
- Insecticide-impregnated cords and strips.
- Larvicides on breeding material.
(Flies develop resistance quickly → rotate insecticides.)

**3. Mechanical / physical**

Fly papers, fly traps, electric fly killers.

**4. Protection against flies**

**Screening** of kitchens, dining areas, hospitals and food shops; food covers; mosquito-net cupboards.

**5. Health education**

Covering food, cleanliness, safe disposal of waste and faeces, hand-washing.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.340
**Identification**

Small insects moving in **short hops** in a village house, especially at night near mud walls and cattle sheds → **sandfly** (*Phlebotomus argentipes*), vector of **kala-azar**.
(If the insects jump on people, rats or pets and are wingless, they are **fleas**.)

**Habits of sandfly**

| Habit | Features |
|---|---|
| **Appearance** | Very small (1.5–3.5 mm), hairy, yellowish or sandy-coloured; wings held **erect in a V-shape** at rest |
| **Flight** | Weak fliers; move in **short hops**; short flight range; cannot fly in wind |
| **Breeding** | **Not in water**: moist soil rich in organic matter in **cracks and crevices of mud walls and floors**, cattle sheds, termite hills, rubbish |
| **Resting** | Dark, humid places: cracks in walls, under furniture, cattle sheds, usually low (below ~2 m) |
| **Biting** | **Night-biters**; only females suck blood; bite exposed skin; silent (no buzzing) |
| **Host preference** | *P. argentipes* feeds on cattle and humans |
| **Life cycle** | Egg → larva → pupa → adult (complete metamorphosis; ~1–2 months) |
| **Season** | More numerous in warm, humid months |
| **Passage** | Small enough to pass through ordinary mosquito nets |

**Diseases**: kala-azar, PKDL (Bangladesh); elsewhere cutaneous leishmaniasis, sandfly fever, Oroya fever.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.341
**Mosquito vs sandfly**

| Feature | Mosquito | Sandfly |
|---|---|---|
| Size | Larger (4–10 mm) | Very small (1.5–3.5 mm) |
| Body covering | Scales | Dense **hairs** |
| Wings at rest | Lie **flat** over the body | Held **erect, V-shaped** |
| Flight | Strong fliers | Weak; move in **hops** |
| Sound | Buzzing | Silent |
| Proboscis | Long | Short |
| Breeding | **Water** (aquatic larvae and pupae) | **Moist soil**, cracks and crevices (terrestrial larvae) |
| Larva | Aquatic; breathes at surface | Caterpillar-like, on land |
| Biting time | Varies by genus (day: *Aedes*; night: *Anopheles*, *Culex*) | Night |
| Net | Stopped by ordinary nets | Passes through ordinary nets (needs fine mesh or treated nets) |
| Diseases | Malaria, dengue, chikungunya, filariasis, Japanese encephalitis | Kala-azar, PKDL, cutaneous leishmaniasis, sandfly fever, Oroya fever |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.342
**Diseases transmitted by sandflies**

| Disease | Agent | Sandfly |
|---|---|---|
| **Kala-azar** (visceral leishmaniasis) | *Leishmania donovani* | *Phlebotomus argentipes* (Bangladesh, India) |
| **Post-kala-azar dermal leishmaniasis (PKDL)** | *L. donovani* (sequel) | Reservoir for further spread |
| **Cutaneous leishmaniasis** (oriental sore) | *L. tropica*, *L. major* | *Phlebotomus* spp. |
| **Mucocutaneous leishmaniasis** (espundia) | *L. braziliensis* | *Lutzomyia* (Americas) |
| **Sandfly fever** (phlebotomus / pappataci fever) | Phlebovirus | *P. papatasi* |
| **Oroya fever / Carrion's disease** (bartonellosis) | *Bartonella bacilliformis* | *Lutzomyia* (South America) |
| Chandipura encephalitis | Chandipura virus | Sandflies (India) |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.343
**Life history of lice**

Lice undergo **incomplete metamorphosis**: Egg (nit) → Nymph (3 moults) → Adult

| Stage | Features |
|---|---|
| **Egg (nit)** | Oval, white; firmly **cemented to hair** (head louse) or to **seams of clothing** (body louse); hatches in about a week |
| **Nymph** | Resembles a small adult; sucks blood from the start; moults **three times** over about 1–2 weeks |
| **Adult** | Wingless, flattened dorso-ventrally; 3 pairs of legs with claws for gripping hair or fibres; both sexes suck blood several times a day; lives about a month; female lays a few hundred eggs in her life |

Whole cycle (egg to egg-laying adult): about 2–3 weeks.

**Types of human lice**

| Louse | Site | Importance |
|---|---|---|
| Head louse (*Pediculus humanus capitis*) | Scalp hair | Pediculosis capitis; school children |
| Body louse (*P. h. corporis*) | Clothing seams | **Epidemic typhus**, **relapsing fever**, **trench fever** |
| Crab louse (*Phthirus pubis*) | Pubic hair | Pediculosis pubis (sexual contact) |

**Control**

Personal hygiene; washing clothes in hot water; ironing seams; combing with fine-toothed comb; permethrin 1% lotion or other pediculicides; delousing in camps; avoid sharing combs, caps, clothes.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.344
**Control of sandfly in a rural community**

**1. Insecticidal control (main method)**

- **Indoor residual spraying (IRS)** of houses and **cattle sheds** with synthetic pyrethroids (deltamethrin, alpha-cypermethrin); earlier DDT.
- Spray walls up to about 2 m height (sandflies rest low), cracks, crevices, under furniture.
- Two rounds a year in endemic areas, timed before peak season.

**2. Environmental control (source reduction)**

- **Plaster cracks and crevices** of mud walls and floors with mud, lime or cement.
- Remove rubbish, vegetation and damp organic matter near houses.
- Keep **cattle sheds away** from human dwellings; improve housing (brick, cement).
- Proper drainage; reduce dampness.

**3. Personal protection**

- **Insecticide-treated / long-lasting insecticidal nets**; fine-mesh nets.
- Repellents; covering the body at night; sleeping on raised beds (sandflies fly low).

**4. Control of the reservoir (anthroponotic kala-azar)**

Active case detection and prompt treatment of kala-azar and **PKDL** cases.

**5. Community participation and health education**

Awareness of sandfly habits; cooperation with spraying teams; early reporting of fever and splenomegaly.

**6. Surveillance**

Entomological monitoring of sandfly density and insecticide resistance.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.345
**Fleas of public health importance**

| Group | Species | Diseases / importance |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Rat fleas** | ***Xenopsylla cheopis*** (oriental rat flea: most important) | **Plague**, **murine (endemic) typhus**, *Hymenolepis diminuta* |
| | *X. astia*, *X. brasiliensis* | Plague (less efficient vectors) |
| | *Nosopsyllus fasciatus* (northern rat flea) | Plague, murine typhus |
| **2. Human flea** | *Pulex irritans* | Nuisance, dermatitis; plague (minor role) |
| **3. Cat and dog fleas** | *Ctenocephalides felis*, *C. canis* | Dog tapeworm (*Dipylidium caninum*), cat-scratch disease (*Bartonella*), dermatitis |
| **4. Sand flea (chigoe)** | *Tunga penetrans* | **Tungiasis** (burrows in skin of feet) |

**Features of fleas**

Wingless, laterally compressed, strong hind legs for **jumping**; both sexes suck blood; complete metamorphosis (egg → larva → pupa → adult).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.346
**Habits of the rat flea (*Xenopsylla cheopis*)**

1. **Both sexes are blood feeders**; feed on rats, and on man when rats are not available.
2. **Leave a dead or dying host** quickly and seek a new one → when rats die of plague, fleas move to humans.
3. **Jump** onto hosts; live in rat burrows and nests.
4. **Favourable conditions**: moderate temperature (about 20–25 °C) and high humidity → seasonal plague.
5. **Survive** for weeks without feeding.
6. **Eggs** laid in rat nests and burrows; larvae feed on organic debris; complete metamorphosis.

**Disease transmission**

| Disease | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| **Plague** (*Yersinia pestis*) | Bacilli multiply in the flea's proventriculus and form a **block** → "**blocked flea**" cannot swallow blood, becomes hungry, bites repeatedly and **regurgitates** infected blood into the bite wound (biological, propagative transmission) |
| **Murine typhus** (*Rickettsia typhi*) | Infected **flea faeces** rubbed into bite wounds or abrasions |
| *Hymenolepis diminuta* | Accidental ingestion of infected fleas |

**Flea indices (plague surveillance)**

- **Total flea index**: average number of fleas per rat.
- **Specific flea (cheopis) index**: average number of *X. cheopis* per rat; **>1 indicates danger** of plague outbreak.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medical Entomology).

## DU.347
**Distinguishing features of rodents (order Rodentia)**

1. **One pair of chisel-shaped incisors** in each jaw that **grow continuously**; worn down by gnawing.
2. **No canine teeth**; gap (**diastema**) between incisors and molars.
3. **Gnawing habit**: damage food, buildings, cables.
4. **High fertility**: breed rapidly, many litters a year.
5. Mostly **nocturnal**; omnivorous; can climb, burrow or swim.
6. Live close to man (commensal).

**Types of rodents**

| Type | Species | Habits |
|---|---|---|
| **Domestic (commensal)** | *Rattus rattus* (black / house / roof rat) | Slender, pointed snout, large ears, **tail longer than head + body**; good climber; lives in roofs |
